# Coding Conventions — read before editing YAML or engine C#

## WDist notation

OpenRA uses `WDist` (World Distance) units throughout. Notation `NcXXX`:

- `1c0` = 1 cell = 1024 WDist units
- `1c512` = 1.5 cells (1024 + 512)
- `3c768` = 3.75 cells
- Plain `512` = 512 WDist units (half a cell)

## WAngle facing — counterclockwise (opposite of typical conventions!)

`WAngle` facings rotate **counterclockwise**, 0–1024 range. Easy to get wrong.

| WAngle | Direction | Screen (top-down) |
|--------|-----------|-------------------|
| 0      | North     | Up                |
| 256    | **West**  | **Left**          |
| 512    | South     | Down              |
| 768    | **East**  | **Right**         |

Map placement: units on the LEFT facing right → `Facing: 768` (East); on the RIGHT facing left → `Facing: 256` (West). Conversion: `WAngle.FromFacing(old)` where old RA facing × 4 = WAngle.

**The four compass NAMES exist only in the Lua binding — `FieldLoader` has no name table for `WAngle`, so a `Facing: East` in YAML is a hard parse error.** `AngleGlobal` publishes `North => WAngle.Zero`, `NorthWest => 128`, `West => 256`, `SouthWest => 384`, `South => 512`, `SouthEast => 640`, `East => 768`, `NorthEast => 896` (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Scripting/Global/AngleGlobal.cs` — note they walk *counterclockwise*, per this section). `FieldLoader.ParseWAngle` parses an integer and nothing else (`engine/OpenRA.Game/FieldLoader.cs`, `ParseWAngle`), so a placed actor's `Facing: East` in a `map.yaml` throws `FieldLoader: Cannot parse 'East' into WAngle` **at map load, before a single tick.** The trap is that a scenario's `.lua` doing `Actor.Create(..., Facing = Angle.East)` is correct and the `map.yaml` line next to it is not, so the same word is right in one file and fatal in the other. Corpus check costs nothing and needs no build: `grep -rnE '^\s+(Turret)?Facing: ' … | grep -vE ': -?[0-9]+$'`.

**Converting a bearing back to a cell step:** `WVec.FromSpeedAndAngle(speed, angle)` is the exact inverse of `WVec.Yaw` (both in `WVec.cs`). To turn a bearing `WAngle` (e.g. one built from a cell-space `WVec(dx,dy).Yaw`) back into a cell-space step, use `FromSpeedAndAngle` and take `Sign(X)/Sign(Y)` — it respects the "north = −Y" + counterclockwise convention automatically, avoiding hand-rolled `Cos()/Sin()` sign errors.

**A `RateOfTurn`-style field in YAML is a raw `WAngle`, not a facing — a misreading that recurs.** `new WAngle(20)` is 20 raw units; consumers read `.Facing`, which is `Angle / 4` (`WAngle.Facing`). So `HorizontalRateOfTurn: 20` (`MissileInfo.HorizontalRateOfTurn`) means **5 facings/tick ≈ 7.03°/tick**, not 20 of anything a reader would guess. **Grep the consumer for `.Facing`, `>> 2` or `(sbyte)` before trusting any raw angle you see in YAML** — `WAngle`'s own `[0, 1023]` normalisation guarantees nothing about the range or units the *consumer* accepts. (Worked case in [`missiles.md`](missiles.md) §4: a field the engine re-decodes through `(sbyte)(Angle >> 2)` has a usable range of `[0, 511]`, and `Missile` decodes that one field three different ways.)

**`Mobile.Speed` in YAML is not the speed at the point of use.** `MovementSpeedForCell` appends the locomotor's per-terrain percentage to the modifier list before applying it (`Mobile.MovementSpeedForCell`), so a `lightwheeled` unit crossing `Clear` moves at **70%** of its declared figure (`world.yaml`, the `lightwheeled` locomotor's `TerrainSpeeds`) — a humvee's `Speed: 150` (`vehicles-america.yaml`, `humvee:`) is 105 wdist/tick on open ground and only hits 150 on `Road`/`Bridge` at 100. Any timing or intercept arithmetic taken from the YAML number alone is ~30% optimistic over most of the map.

**`WVec.Yaw` is silently wrong once a component passes 2,097,151, so scaling a vector up "for precision" can make the answer worse rather than better.** `WAngle.ArcTan` finds the closest tabulated angle with `Math.Abs(1024 * ay - (long)ax * TanTable[i])` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/WAngle.cs`, `ArcTan`). The second operand is promoted to `long`; **`1024 * ay` is not** — so any component above `2^31 / 1024 = 2,097,151` overflows `int`, the comparison runs against a wrapped value, and the method returns a plausible-looking arbitrary angle with no exception and no warning. Its own comment concedes it "may still fail for unrealistically large ax and ay"; the point worth carrying is that **2 million is not unrealistically large** — it is a WDist vector a couple of thousand cells long, or any intermediate deliberately scaled up for integer precision. Measured: `BallisticMissileFly` built its direction vector at magnitude `1<<20` to hold division error under one WAngle step, and at a 45° heading with slope 3.1 the returned facing was **94.9° wrong** — the nose snapped nearly perpendicular. **The ceiling applies to every caller of `WVec.Yaw`**, which goes straight to `ArcTan`. Before choosing a working magnitude, bound the largest component the arithmetic can produce — the multiplied-through worst case, not the typical one — and keep it under 2,097,151; `1<<16` still resolves direction to 0.0009° against a WAngle step of 0.352°. A unit test that sweeps gentle inputs cannot find this: the sweep has to reach the ceiling. **`ArcTan` itself still overflows**: `BallisticMissileFly` avoids it by choosing a smaller magnitude, which fixes that call site and not the engine.

## `Timestep` is MILLISECONDS PER TICK, and the number that looks like a tick rate is its inverse

**`mods/ww3mod/mod.yaml` really does contain `Timestep: 40` under `GameSpeeds`, and that is the whole trap.** A reader hunting the tick rate finds a plausible integer, in the right file — and it is wrong twice over: it is a **duration in milliseconds**, not a rate, and it belongs to the **`fastest`** speed, not the default. Read as a rate it says 40 ticks/s. It means 1000/40 = 25 ticks/s, on a speed nobody plays at.

**The default is the only one that matters for anything computed offline:** `GameSpeeds: DefaultSpeed: default` selects the `default:` block, whose `Timestep: 60` gives **1000/60 = 16.67 ticks per second**.

**All eleven, by block name, so nobody has to guess which block a line sits in:** `strategical` 120, `tactical` 100, `slowest` 86, `slower` 75, `slow` 67, **`default` 60**, `fast` 55, `faster` 48, `fastest` 40, `crazy` 34, `insane` 30. Every one is ms/tick, so the *smallest* number is the *fastest* speed — that inversion is why the file reads like a rate table when it is a period table.

**Proof it is ms, from the engine rather than by inference.** `GameSpeed.Timestep` is `public readonly int` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/GameSpeed.cs`) and is consumed as a millisecond interval (`Game.cs`, the `logicInterval` local in the main loop).

**There is one tick base: 16.667 ticks/s, everywhere.** Every conversion in the tree divides *into* 1000 and multiplies before dividing. The engine converts through `TickTime.TicksForSeconds` / `TicksForMinutes` (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/TickTime.cs`), so the engine Lua `DateTime.Seconds(60)` (`Scripting/Global/DateTimeGlobal.cs`) is exactly 1000 ticks, and `TimeLimitManager` uses the same conversion. The Lua harness derives `TestHarness.TicksPerSecond` from `TestHarness.TimestepMs = 60` (`mods/ww3mod/scripts/test-helpers.lua`) and converts through `TestHarness.TicksForSeconds`, which mirrors the engine, so `AssertWithin(n)` and `DateTime.Seconds(n)` are the same tick count.

**What guards it and what does not.** `AutotestTickRateTest` (`engine/OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/AutotestTickRateTest.cs`) resolves the default properly — `DefaultTimestepMs()` reads `DefaultSpeed`, then that named block's `Timestep` — and fails `dotnet test` if the default moves off 60 ms (`SingleTestRunsPlayAtTheModDefaultTimestep`), if `TestHarness.TicksPerSecond` stops being `1000.0 / 60` (a hardcoded 25 or a truncated 16 both fail), or if harness and engine disagree on any integer second from 0 to 600 (`HarnessAndEngineAgreeTickForTickOnEveryIntegerSecond`). That is real protection against the value changing. It is **no** protection against a human or an agent reading the wrong one of eleven blocks, which is the failure that actually occurs; nothing can fail a build over a number someone typed into a prompt. Read as a rate, `fastest`'s 40 makes every duration 2.4× too short — a `ReloadDelay` of 50 reported as 1.25 s instead of 3.00 s.

**The rules, in the order you need them:**
1. **Any duration computed from a tick count divides by 16.67** — never by a nearby-looking integer, and never by 25 or 40. **Do not "correct" either harness constant to 16 or 25.**
2. **Never cite a `Timestep` line without checking which of the eleven blocks it sits in.** Cite it by symbol — `GameSpeeds: DefaultSpeed: default` and that block's `Timestep: 60` — not by line number; the line numbers in `mod.yaml` move whenever anything above them is edited.
3. **Budget in TICKS, convert once through `TicksForSeconds`, and never materialise an integer ticks-per-second value.**

**Why the conversion has to be `TicksForSeconds` and not `ticks / TicksPerSecond` round-tripped by hand: a float round-trip through a non-power-of-two divisor is not an identity.** In IEEE-754 doubles `floor((t/(1000/60))*(1000/60))` returns `t - 1` for 9405 of the first 20000 integers (225 ticks comes back as 13.499999999999998 s). `TestHarness.TicksForSeconds` adds a 1e-9 epsilon before truncating, which cancels exactly that representation error, so every integer tick budget round-trips exactly — executed by `AutotestTickRateTest.AssertDeadlineIsRateImmune`. One tick rarely matters on its own, and it matters exactly where it is most likely to be spent — a deadline trimmed to its measured margin has already given the tick away.

**`1000 / world.Timestep` is the error that uses the right constant and truncates it.** At 60 ms it is `1000 / 60 = 16`, not 16.667, so every duration built by multiplying it runs **4% fast** (ten minutes becomes 9600 ticks = 576 s; a 90-minute limit ends about 3.6 minutes early). It is invisible wherever the display derives from the same tick count — **the clock lies consistently, which is the whole reason it survives** — and becomes visible the moment anything else draws a boundary against real minutes. **The remedy is arithmetic, not a bigger constant: multiply before dividing.** `minutes * 60 * 1000 / timestep` is exact (`10 * 60 * 1000 / 60 = 10000`), and an `int` cannot hold 16.667, so any code that materialises a ticks-per-second number has already lost the 4% before it multiplies. This and the 25-tps error both land within a few per cent of plausible, which is what makes them survive review.

**Sequence `Tick` is milliseconds against a FIXED 40 ms clock, and is NOT scaled by `Timestep`.**
`Animation.Tick()` calls `Tick(40)`
unconditionally (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Graphics/Animation.cs`) and the default sequence tick is also
40 (`CurrentSequenceTickOrDefault`, `// 25 fps == 40 ms`). The two clocks are unrelated: a sequence with `Tick: T` advances one
frame per `T / 40` **game ticks** whatever the game speed, and the wall-clock duration then scales with the
`Timestep` the player picked. So for an N-frame sequence, **game ticks = N x Tick / 40**, and seconds at the
default speed = that x 0.06. Worked, for the 32-frame `factmake` deploy: no `Tick:` line gives 32 game ticks
= **1.92 s** — not the 1.28 s that assuming a 40 ms `Timestep` produces, which is the same 1.5x family of
error catalogued below. `Tick: 60` gives 48 ticks (2.88 s); `Tick: 80` gives 64 (3.84 s). A `Transforms`
actor's deploy and undeploy play the **one** sequence — forwards from `WithMakeAnimation.Forward`, backwards
from `Transform.Tick` (`Activities/Transform.cs`, `makeAnimation.Reverse`) — so a single `Tick:` sets both durations and they
cannot drift apart.

**Do NOT "fix" the 40 — change the CONSUMER.** `Animation.Tick()` crediting a flat 40 is load-bearing for every non-decoration animation *and* for sim paths that gate on animation completion, because `RenderSprites` is `ITick` (`Traits/Render/RenderSprites.cs`) so animations advance inside `World.Tick`'s synced trait loop: `Activities/Transform.cs` (actor replacement, `makeAnimation.Reverse`'s callback), `Traits/Sellable.cs` (refund, `makeAnimation.Reverse(…, new Sell(…))`) and `Activities/GenericDockSequence.cs` (dock state machine, `PlayThen`/`PlayBackwardsThen`) all key off an animation-completion callback. **Changing the constant is a determinism change, not a render change.** The safe scope is one trait at a time: `WithDecoration` builds its own `Animation` whose `PlayRepeating` tickFunc only increments a counter, so it was moved to `Animation.PlayFetchIndex` and fetches its frame from `Game.RunTime`, never consulting the sequence clock. `Length: 1` sequences fetch `x % 1 == 0` unconditionally, so every other decoration is untouched by construction.

Four properties of that seam, each of which decides whether a follow-on is buildable:

- **The fetch lambda runs once per WORLD TICK, not once per rendered frame**, and it is inside the `if (!Paused …)` guard — so *stateful* accumulation is safe there (no multi-viewport double-advance), unlike `WithDecorationBase.ShouldRender`, which is on the render path and must stay stateless.
- **A stateless absolute-time phase (`RunTime / stepMs % length`) re-phases DISCONTINUOUSLY whenever `stepMs` changes**: at `RunTime` 300000 ms, 450 gives frame 0 and 440 gives frame 1. With a health-scaled interval the rate changes on every damage event, so the indicator would stutter and skip precisely while the unit is under fire — the one moment it exists to be read. Carrying phase forward across rate changes is what avoids it (`BlinkPhase`, `Traits/Render/WithDecorationBase.cs`, in thousandths of a frame to stay integer).
- **The floor is the tick rate, not the ramp.** Because the lambda is per world tick, a blink cannot resolve faster than about two world ticks per cycle — **240 ms at `strategical`'s 120 ms `Timestep`**. Below that it aliases rather than speeds up. Going finer means moving the computation to the render path, which re-inherits the statelessness constraint and therefore the discontinuity.
- **A health ramp belongs to the SEQUENCE, not to the trait** (`HealthRampTick`/`HealthRampStart`, `Graphics/DefaultSpriteSequence.cs`, exposed on `ISpriteSequence` in `Graphics/SequenceSet.cs`). The same blinking artwork is drawn by more than one trait once a ladder spans two damage bands, so a per-trait ramp means several YAML sites that must agree — and any disagreement shows up as the blink rate **jumping at a band boundary**, a discontinuity in the exact signal the ramp exists to make readable. On the sequence it is stated once and rides along with the artwork.

**Incidental: `ChangeTick` is silently ignored by a fetched index.** `CurrentSequenceTickOrDefault` supports per-frame durations via `ChangeTick`, and an index fetched from wall-clock cannot honour it. Harmless while no animated *decoration* uses it, and silent — no error, no lint — if one ever does.

**This field's line citations rot unusually fast — cite it by symbol and re-read the file rather than trusting any prose about it, including this section.** Same family as the scar in §"A change believed made, documented as made, and inert": `SupplyRouteContestation`'s duration comments were wrong by 1.5× from assuming 25 tps. **The generalisation worth carrying beyond this field: a units-bearing config value with no unit in its name is a trap, and it is worst when the wrong reading produces a plausible number** — 40 tps is not absurd, which is exactly why it survives review.

## YAML

### Templates (prefixed with ^)

```yaml
^Aircraft:          # Base template for fixed-wing planes
^Helicopter:        # Base template for helicopters
^Drone:             # Base template for drones
^Airborne:          # Common airborne traits
^NeutralAirborne:   # Airborne without faction-specific traits
^AirRadar:          # Radar trait for aircraft (range 24c0)
```

### Precedence inside an actor is POSITIONAL — a later `Inherits@` overwrites an earlier own field, including a map's override

**There is no "own beats inherited" rule.** `MiniYaml.ResolveInherits` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/MiniYaml.cs`) walks `node.Nodes` in **source order**, and both branches funnel into the same accumulator: an `Inherits`/`Inherits@` key expands its parent and folds each resulting node through `MergeIntoResolved`, and a plain own node is folded through the *same* `MergeIntoResolved` (the loop's final `else`). The leaf rule is `new MiniYaml(overrideNodes.Value ?? existingNodes.Value, …)`, the return of `MergePartial(MiniYaml, MiniYaml)` — **the later contributor wins, per field, recursively.** An actor's own trait block wins only because it is conventionally written *below* its `Inherits@` lines; move an `Inherits@` beneath it and the parent overwrites it. The same positionality governs removals: `-Key:` does `resolved.RemoveAll(…)` against what has resolved **so far** (the `-`-prefixed branch of `ResolveInherits`), so a `-Trait:` written above an `Inherits@` that re-supplies the trait is undone by it.

**Why this bites hardest on MAP rules.** Map-vs-mod precedence is resolved by `Merge` *before* inheritance runs, so a map's override lands **inside the template node** it edited — and is then overwritten by any parent the derived actor imports further down its own node. A map that overrides a field on `^CamoSoldier` therefore need not reach `^AR`, whose `Inherits@AutoTarget: ^AutoTargetLMG` is written *after* `Inherits@Type: ^CamoSoldier` (the head of `^AR` in `infantry.yaml`) and re-supplies the same trait from `^AutoTarget` (`rules/defaults.yaml`, reached through `^AutoTargetLMG`'s `Inherits: ^AutoTarget`).

**What hides it is a sibling field in the same block that keeps working.** Only fields the later parent *also* sets are clobbered; fields it leaves alone survive from the earlier contributor. `^AutoTarget` sets `InitialResupplyBehavior` in its `AutoTarget:` block but sets no `ScanRadius`, while `^CamoSoldier` sets `ScanRadius: 25` in its own (`infantry.yaml`) — so an override of the first is silently reverted while an override of the second **in the same trait block** holds. That reads as "my edit half-applied", not as an inheritance-order problem, which is why it survives review.

**Detector:** when a YAML override does not take, resolve the ruleset (`--dump-balance-json`, below) and list the derived actor's `Inherits@` lines *in order* — anything declared after the line you edited is a candidate to have overwritten it. This is the multi-parent form of the same merge machinery described in [§A duplicate trait key inside ONE actor](#a-duplicate-trait-key-inside-one-actor-last-value-wins-at-the-first-keys-position).

**The same machinery makes an OVERLAY TEMPLATE: a template whose whole body re-states keys it inherits, carrying only a gate.** `^StandardVisionWhenLoaded` (`rules/defaults.yaml`) inherits `^StandardVision` and then re-states `Vision@1`..`Vision@10` carrying nothing but `RequiresCondition: loaded`. MiniYaml merges those onto the identically-keyed nodes already present, so the ranges survive and the gate lands on top — **it removes nothing and adds no band; the gating is done by key collision.** Two consequences. It only gates a ladder that is already there by the time it is folded in, so its position among the `Inherits@` lines matters (above). And **the actor that is wrong is the one with a line MISSING**, invisible to any check comparing trait VALUES: PBOX revealed shroud while unmanned, alone of the three emplacements, because GTWR and HBOX carried `Inherits@DetectionWhenLoaded: ^StandardVisionWhenLoaded` and PBOX did not — PBOX now carries it too (`PBOX` in `structures-defenses.yaml`, with the reasoning in-comment above the line). Every defence's Vision ranges were identical throughout, and the inherit's name does not contain the word it gates on.

### Conditions system

**A condition variable resolves to its STACK DEPTH, so a band selector needs no engine code.** `Actor.UpdateConditionState` assigns `conditionCache[condition] = conditionState.Tokens.Count`, and `VariableExpression` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Support/VariableExpression.cs`, `TokenType.Equals` … `GreaterThanOrEqual`) implements the full set of comparison operators against those variables. **So any trait that grants one token per unit of something exposes a countable quantity to YAML for free.** `AmmoPool` already does exactly this — one token pushed per remaining round, popped on spend (`AmmoPool.UpdateCondition`) — which is what makes `RequiresCondition: ammo-primary == 2` on the Iskander's missile overlay (`iskander`'s `WithWeaponOverlay@Missile_1`, `vehicles-russia.yaml`) work; `ammo-primary >= 6 && ammo-primary <= 10` is equally valid and needs no C#.

**Before adding a `FooHigh`/`FooMedium`/`FooLow` condition triple to a trait, grant one stacking token instead and let the YAML compare.** The thresholds then live with the actor that cares rather than baked into the C# field list. `SupplyProvider` has the triple (`SupplyHighCondition`/`SupplyMediumCondition`/`SupplyLowCondition`, each granted at most once), which cannot express eighths; it also offers `SupplyLevelCondition`/`SupplyLevelSteps`, defaulting to null/0 so no existing actor changes. **Note the operators require surrounding whitespace** — `ammo-primary>=6` throws *"Missing whitespace at index N"* (`VariableExpression.cs`, two throws in the tokenizer), a genuine exception rather than a silent false.

Traits grant and consume named conditions:

```yaml
GrantConditionOnDamageState:
    Condition: heavy-damage-attained    # Granted at heavy damage
SpeedMultiplier@HeavyDamage:
    Modifier: 90
    RequiresCondition: heavy-damage-attained
```

Common conditions: `airborne`, `cruising`, `moving`, `empdisable`, `dronedisable`, `heavy-damage-attained`, `critical-damage`, `rank-veteran`, `suppression-*`, `unit.docked`

**A consumed-but-ungranted condition is a `make test` ERROR (not a warning).** `ConditionalTraitInfo.RequiresCondition` is `[ConsumedConditionReference]` (`ConditionalTrait.cs`), so a trait gated on `foo || bar` marks BOTH names *consumed on that actor*. `CheckConditions.Run` (`Lint/CheckConditions.cs`) calls `emitError` — which fails `make test` — for any condition an actor consumes but nothing grants. (The reverse, granted-but-unconsumed, is only an `emitWarning`.) To make a name grantable-but-not-yet-fired (e.g. a seam for later Lua/warhead activation) without a live grant, declare an `ExternalCondition` whose `Condition` field is `[GrantedConditionReference]` (`Conditions/ExternalCondition.cs`) — it satisfies the lint at zero runtime cost and a later `GrantCondition("name")` resolves to it.

**The Fluent lint is bidirectional, and the unused-key half is a WARNING that rots.** `CheckFluentReferences` checks referenced-but-undefined (an error) *and* defined-but-unreferenced (`CheckUnusedKey` in `Lint/CheckFluentReferences.cs`, `emitWarning`), reached for every key of every non-external file — and a file under `mods/ww3mod/` is not external. Unused keys therefore only fail the run under `TREAT_WARNINGS_AS_ERRORS=true` (`UtilityCommands/CheckYaml.cs`), and they arrive in a pile nobody reads to the end. That is how the entire server/lobby notification block in `mods/ww3mod/languages/en.ftl` — 38 keys from `timeout-in` through `chat-temp-disabled` — came to be **dead**: not one of those bare names appears as a string literal anywhere in `engine/OpenRA.Game/` or `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/`, because the `[FluentReference]` consts name the `notification-`-prefixed forms that live in `engine/mods/common/fluent/common.ftl` (30 of the 38 have such a twin; the other 8 were renamed *and* reworded upstream). Editing the ww3mod copies changes nothing any player sees. **Treat a large standing warning count as the defect it is** — this one had a real bug hiding in it.

**`GrantConditionOnLobbyOption`'s polarity decides what an UNREGISTERED option does, so the two ways of writing "gate a feature on a checkbox" are NOT equivalent.** The trait falls back to `OptionOrDefault(Option, !GrantWhenOptionDisabled)` (`Conditions/GrantConditionOnLobbyOption.cs`) when the lobby never registered the option at all — a stripped options trait, an old saved session, a map that removes it:

| Form | Absent option resolves to |
|---|---|
| `Condition: X-disabled`, `GrantWhenOptionDisabled: true`, feature `RequiresCondition: !X-disabled` | **feature OFF** |
| `Condition: X-allowed`, `GrantWhenOptionDisabled: false`, feature `RequiresCondition: X-allowed` | **feature ON** |

Both read identically at a glance and behave identically in a normal lobby. **For anything that must fail safe — the tactical nuclear strike is the live case — only the first form is correct.**

**Grants scoped to the actor:** `GrantConditionOnBotOwner` grants only on the actor it sits on, checking `self.Owner.IsBot && Bots.Contains(self.Owner.BotType)` (`Conditions/GrantConditionOnBotOwner.cs`). A **unit** trait's `RequiresCondition` sees only conditions granted on that *unit*, so gating a unit trait to bot-only needs a per-unit `GrantConditionOnBotOwner` on the unit template — a Player-actor grant (e.g. the `enable-ai-experimental` grants in `ai.yaml`) does nothing for a unit trait.

**Deleting a condition GRANT orphans every consumer that had no OTHER granter, and the tell is an unreachable trait rather than an error.** When you remove a granter, the compiler and the linter both stay silent: a `RequiresCondition` naming a now-ungrantable condition is not an error, it is a trait that quietly never activates again.

The worked case. `9e46f141` deleted LOGISTICSCENTER's `ProximityExternalCondition@ReplenishSoldiers`, which had granted `replenish-soldiers` to everyone within 4c0 unconditionally (removal recorded in place in `structures.yaml`: the `REMOVED … ProximityExternalCondition@ReplenishSoldiers` comment on LOGISTICSCENTER). That was correct — it was free ammunition. But the condition then came from `SupplyProvider.SyncTargetCondition` alone, and only to the **single client it has selected**, where selection reads demand exclusively from `Rearmable.RearmableAmmoPools`. So the delete silently converted "a pool not listed in `Rearmable.AmmoPools`" from a harmless omission into a permanent one: the combat engineer listed `secondary-ammo` (C4) alone while also carrying an SMG pool, so **with full C4 he presented `NoDemand` however empty his rifle was**, was never selected, never granted the condition, and his `ReloadAmmoPool@1` — whose sole gate is that condition — never ran again.

**The partial shape is why it survived review and would survive hand-testing.** While the engineer *is* being served for his charges he holds the condition and the rifle trickles as a side effect. The bug appears only when the C4 is full — his normal state — so every casual test of "does the engineer rearm at a depot" says yes.

- **The rule:** enumerate the consumers before deleting a granter, and for each one ask whether any **other** granter reaches it — not whether the deletion looks correct at the grant site.
- **A corroborating signal that generalises:** the omission was already **priced**. The same merge added a costing comment budgeting five batches for a pool that was not listed to reach the metered path. **A cost annotation on an unreachable path is evidence the unreachability is accidental**, and it is far cheaper to notice than the behaviour is.
- **`Rearmable.AmmoPools` order is inert** — the field is a `HashSet` (`Rearmable.cs`) and `RearmableAmmoPools` is built in the actor's *trait declaration* order (`TraitsImplementing<AmmoPool>().Where(…)`), not the list's. Do not read meaning into it.

**`evacuating` buys a unit NOTHING defensively — the "it is deprioritised as a target" framing is wrong, and wrong in the dangerous direction.** The condition is granted in `Activities/RotateToEdge.cs` (`self.GrantCondition("evacuating")`). Enumerated by grepping for every reader rather than by walking outward from the grant site, it has **five kinds of consumer and not one of them touches targeting**:

| where | what it does |
|---|---|
| `SelectionPriorityModifier@Evacuating` (`vehicles.yaml`, `infantry.yaml`, `aircraft.yaml`) | `Modifier: -20` into `ISelectionPriorityModifier` |
| `WithDecoration@Evacuating` (the same three files; `aircraft.yaml` declares it twice, once per airframe template) | draws the orange pip |
| `UnitBuilderBotModule.CensusExcludeCondition` | drops the unit from the bot's composition census |
| `AutoSeekSupplies.EvacuatingCondition` | "already leaving — don't send it to a depot" |
| `DropsSupplyCache.EvacuatingCondition` | exempts an evacuating truck from the dry break-off |

`ISelectionPriorityModifier`'s **only** reader engine-wide is `SelectableExts.SelectionPriority` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/SelectableExts.cs`) — the local player's own mouse and box-select. No weapon, no `AutoTarget`, no targeting path consults it. **An evacuating unit is a completely ordinary auto-target for its whole drive**, so any design that lengthens an evacuation is buying exposure at full price with nothing softening it. The two logistics readers are *self*-suppression ("don't interrupt a departure"), not protection.

**Second-order, and it moves BOTH bot profiles:** `CensusExcludeCondition` defaults to `evacuating`, so an evacuating unit reads as "already gone" to `UnitBuilderBotModule` for the whole drive. Lengthen evacuations and the bot pulls replacement buys forward by that much — unmeasured, and named here so the next benchmark baseline is re-taken knowingly rather than read as noise.

### Faction-specific files

Each unit type has a base template file and two faction files:

- `aircraft.yaml` → `aircraft-america.yaml` + `aircraft-russia.yaml`
- `infantry.yaml` → `infantry-america.yaml` + `infantry-russia.yaml`
- `vehicles.yaml` → `vehicles-america.yaml` + `vehicles-russia.yaml`
- `naval.yaml` → `naval-america.yaml` + `naval-russia.yaml`

A unit type is defined in three tiers: an abstract template `^E6` and a bare concrete `E6:` (`Inherits: ^E6`) live in the base file (`infantry.yaml`); the buildable faction variants `E6.america:` / `E6.russia:` (each `Inherits: ^E6`, adding `Buildable`/`RenderSprites`) live in the faction file (`E6.america:` in `infantry-america.yaml`, etc.). Keys are declared uppercase but actor lookup is case-insensitive. **To annotate every variant at once, override the `^Template`** — a trait added to `^E6` reaches the bare concrete and both faction variants in one line; single-hull tweaks (`bradley`, `bmp2`) go on the concrete key.

**A faction variant REPLACES the whole value of a field it redeclares, so judging a `^Template`'s `Buildable` reads the opposite of the truth.** The mod's idiom for *"this template is not itself buildable"* is `Buildable: Prerequisites: ~disabled` — **50 declarations across `mods/`, and `~disabled` has no `ProvidesPrerequisite` anywhere**, so the template alone genuinely can never be built. But each faction variant redeclares `Prerequisites` outright — `TECN.america` (`infantry-america.yaml`) and `TECN.russia` (`infantry-russia.yaml`) each set `~player.<faction>, ~techlevel.infonly`, and `techlevel.infonly` is granted at every tech level (every `ProvidesTechPrerequisite@*` block in `player.yaml`) — so the unit is fully buildable through the variant. MiniYaml replaces rather than merges here, exactly as it does for a warhead override (see §"Weapons live under `Weapons:`" below). **Read a `~disabled` on a `^Template` as "see the faction files", never as "this unit is switched off"**; the mistake reads as a whole mechanic being disabled when it is live.

This is also the reliable way to add a trait from **map/test `rules.yaml`**: override a `^Template` (e.g. `^Combatant`) or a bare hull key (`t90`, `bradley`), as `demo-wgm-suite/rules.yaml` does. Overriding a **faction-suffixed** concrete key (`ar.america`) from map rules has been observed to throw `LoadFromManifest<Rules>, duplicate values found for the following keys: ar.america: [ActorInfo,ActorInfo]` at load — prefer the template.

### Weapon `ValidTargets`: `Air` ⊃ `Helicopter` (the two are NOT disjoint)

**An airborne helicopter carries BOTH `Air` and `Helicopter`.** `^Helicopter` inherits `^Airborne` → `^NeutralAirborne`, whose `Targetable@Airborne` grants `Air, AirDetonateAttack` while the `airborne` condition holds (`rules/ingame/aircraft.yaml`, `^NeutralAirborne`), and `^Helicopter` adds its own *unconditional* `Targetable@Helicopter: Helicopter` (same file). Nothing in `rules/` removes `Targetable@Airborne`. A **landed** helicopter is `Ground, Vehicle` + `Helicopter` and is no longer `Air`; a fixed-wing aircraft is only ever `Air`. So:

- `Helicopter` is the **heli-only** selector — it is how a weapon engages helicopters *without* being able to touch fixed-wing.
- `Air` **sweeps both** helicopters and planes (while airborne).

That asymmetry is the design: a ground gun that lists `Helicopter` can shoot helis without becoming air-defence. In `weapons/weapons-ballistics.yaml` the weapons naming `Helicopter` are `^12.7mm` (`Infantry, Unarmored, Helicopter, Light`), `12.7mm.Hind.AA`, `7.62mm.Minigun.AA` and Tunguska's dedicated `30mm.Tunguska.AA` autocannon (`ValidTargets: Helicopter`, so it cannot engage a jet); recount with `grep -rnE '^\s*ValidTargets:.*\bHelicopter\b' mods/ww3mod/rules/weapons/`. The rifle and GPMG templates do **not** name `Helicopter`: `^5.56mm` is `Infantry, Vehicle, AirLight` and `^7.62mm` is `Infantry, Unarmored, Vehicle, AirLight`, both with `InvalidTargets: Medium, Heavy` — they reach only light-armoured airframes, through the armour-qualified type below. Guided SAMs list `Air`: `MANPAD`, `Stinger`, `Stinger.quad` and `9M311` (`weapons/weapons-missiles.yaml`; `9M311 Inherits: Stinger`), which is how Tunguska gains true air-defence — via its `9M311` missile (`vehicles-russia.yaml`, the `tunguska` armament with `Weapon: 9M311`), not its gun. An "is this an air-defence weapon?" test must therefore key on the literal `Air` target type; keying on `Helicopter` sweeps in MG-armed vehicles.

**There are FOUR airborne selectors, not two — the armour-qualified `AirLight`/`AirMedium`/`AirHeavy` are the third, and a bare armour class is the fourth (ground-only).** The canonical author-facing copy is in-tree in `mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/aircraft.yaml` (the `AIRBORNE TARGET-TYPE VOCABULARY` comment in `^NeutralAirborne`), where a weapon author actually hits it, and is pinned by `AirborneArmorTargetableTest` — read it there rather than duplicating the table. The three facts worth carrying here:

- **`Helicopter` is armour-BLIND, and that is the whole trap.** One flat type covers a 5-thickness littlebird and a 20-thickness Mi-28 alike, so a weapon naming it *necessarily* reaches attack helicopters — no tuning expresses "light rotorcraft only". That is why `^5.56mm` and `^7.62mm` name `AirLight` instead of `Helicopter`, and why the armour-qualified triple exists. `Targetable@Armor` is gated `!airborne` and `Targetable@AirArmor` `airborne`, so a bare `Light`/`Medium`/`Heavy` never reaches a flying actor.
- **`InvalidTargets: Air` does NOT exclude `AirLight`.** Target types are exact strings in a `BitSet<TargetableType>` — no prefix matching, no hierarchy. The ground ordnance that carries `InvalidTargets: Air` to stay off aircraft remains correct only because `Targetable@Airborne` always advertises plain `Air` alongside the armour-qualified type. **Adding a target type can never reduce reachability, but it can defeat an exclusion written against a different string** — an airframe advertising `AirHeavy` *without* `Air` would walk through every one of those exclusions silently.
- **An `InvalidTargets` entry that only ever fired because of a bug is load-bearing in reverse.** While `Targetable@Armor` was ungated, every helicopter advertised `Light` or `Heavy`, and that was the only thing that made `^5.56mm`'s former `InvalidTargets: Light, Medium, Heavy` suppress its own former `ValidTargets: … Helicopter`. Gating the leak removed the exclusion's grip and riflemen could shoot down attack helicopters — **the inverse of the rule, introduced by the change that fixed the rule's other half**, and the reason `^5.56mm` was moved to `AirLight`. When you remove a leaked target type, audit every weapon whose `InvalidTargets` named it; nothing lints for this, and each such weapon reads as unchanged in the diff because it *is* unchanged.

**Reachability is not damage, and say so in the commit message.** `Versus` resolves off the `Armor` **trait**, not off target types (`DamageWarhead.DamageVersus`), so every change in this area moves what a weapon may *engage* and nothing about what a hit does. "5.56mm can now hit a littlebird" reads to most people as a damage claim.

**Consequence for `CreateEffect` warheads:** an air-impact effect wants `ValidTargets: Air` alone. Writing `Air, Helicopter` also matches a *landed* helicopter, which the template's ordinary `Ground` warhead already covers — so the impact draws two explosions stacked on one hit. Note also that an in-flight ballistic missile (`^ShootableMissile`, `rules/defaults.yaml`) is `ICBM` and **not** `Air`, and that one unlisted target type on the victim suppresses the whole effect rather than degrading it (`CreateEffectWarhead.DoImpact` early-returns on `ImpactActorType.Invalid`).

**The suppression is caused by BYSTANDERS, not only by the intended victim — an actor with a `HitShape` and no enabled `Targetable` swallows the explosion and the sound of any shell landing on it.** `CreateEffectWarhead.ActorTypeAtImpact` walks `FindActorsOnCircle(pos, WDist.Zero)` and, for every actor whose hitshape contains the impact point, sets `anyInvalidActor = true` unless the warhead `IsValidAgainst` it — and `IsValidAgainst` fails immediately when `GetEnabledTargetTypes()` is empty. One such actor is enough: the method returns `Invalid` and `DoImpact` returns before the sprite and before `Game.Sound.Play`. Because `^1x1Shape` spans a whole cell, an untargetable full-cell actor silences *every* shell in its cell. **Damage is unaffected** — `CreateEffectWarhead` and `WarheadAS.IsValidImpact` are the only two sites carrying this early-out; `DamageWarhead.DoImpact` has no position-level gate. So "the shell did nothing" and "the shell did damage but looked and sounded like nothing" are different defects. Since `db01b0ae` both sites skip cosmetic ground cover (`victim.IsGroundCover()`, in `CreateEffectWarhead.ActorTypeAtImpact` and in `WarheadAS`), which is what un-silenced shells landing on `^CivField`; any *other* untargetable-but-hitshaped actor still swallows them.

### An armament's `RequiresForceFire` is a per-WEAPON opt-out from AutoTarget, so "does this unit auto-attack X" is not an actor-level question

> **There are TWO fields named `RequiresForceFire` and they sit on opposite ends of the shot.** This section is `Armament.Info.RequiresForceFire`, on the SHOOTER's weapon, filtered inside the `Armaments.Where(...)` in `AttackBase.ChooseArmamentsForTarget` (`Traits/Attack/AttackBase.cs`). The other is `Targetable.RequiresForceFire`, on the VICTIM, which short-circuits the whole method in its opening `!forceAttack && (… target.RequiresForceFire)` guard and composes into an AI blind spot — see [`architecture.md` §"`RequiresForceFire` is an AI-participation switch two layers away"](architecture.md).

`AttackBase.ChooseArmamentsForTarget` walks **every** armament, and each armament excludes itself only by its
own flags. So a unit's AutoTarget configuration says what the unit *may* do and says nothing about which of its
weapons will actually be picked: reading the actor's `AutoTarget` block alone answers "yes" for everything, and
reading only the primary armament answers "never".

The shipped instance is the drone operator, and it is correct as authored rather than a defect: `DR`'s primary
`DroneTargeter` carries `RequiresForceFire: True`, so every AutoTarget scan skips it, while the secondary
`DroneJammer` carries no such flag, no `RequiresCondition` and no `AmmoPool` binding — leaving it the **sole
survivor of the scan filter and therefore DR's only auto-targetable weapon**. The jammer is live *because* the
gun is not. `^DR` additionally removes `StancePositioningExecutor` and `GrantConditionOnBotOwner@tacpos` so a
bot lane cannot silence it, with a protective comment saying so at the site.

**Rule: answer "does this auto-attack X" per ARMAMENT.** The per-actor stance is a permission; the per-armament
flags are the selection.

### There are TWO `ValidTargets` gates on every shot: the weapon's gates AIMING, the warhead's gates IMPACT — and only one of them has a default

A weapon-level `ValidTargets` decides what the armament will **aim at**; each warhead's own `ValidTargets` decides what it **affects on arrival** (`Warhead.IsValidAgainst`). They are separate BitSets on separate objects, and the asymmetry that makes this a trap is that **a warhead which declares nothing is not permissive — it silently gets `Ground, Water`** (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Warheads/Warhead.cs`, the `ValidTargets` initialiser `new("Ground", "Water")`). A weapon that declares nothing simply inherits its template.

So a capability granted at weapon level and never restated at warhead level is **dead for any target type outside `Ground, Water`**, and it fails in the most misleading way available: the shot is legal, the projectile flies, the impact plays, and nothing happens.

**Worked instance, `^ArtilleryRound`** (`rules/weapons/weapons-ballistics.yaml`): it declares `ValidTargets: Ground, Trees, Water`, so a Paladin will happily aim at a tree — while both of its damage warheads (`Warhead@Target`, `Warhead@Spread`) declare only `InvalidTargets: Air` and no `ValidTargets` at all, and therefore fall back to `Ground, Water`. `^Tree` is `Targetable: TargetTypes: Trees` and nothing else, so the shell lands on the tree and does zero damage. The consequence is mod-wide: **no kinetic or HE weapon in this mod can damage a tree**, which is why burnt woodland is a flame-and-thermobaric phenomenon rather than a shelling one.

**Rule: before writing anything that depends on a weapon reaching an exotic target type, read the WARHEAD, not the weapon.** The weapon-level declaration is the one you find first and the one that cannot do the work. Same detector as [§Four shapes](README.md#four-shapes-of-confident-correctness-claim-with-nothing-behind-it) #3 — name the line you would delete to break the property.

**Corollary for `^Tree`, whose name lies:** it is this mod's template for *"neutral decoration with forest cover and `Trees`-only targeting"*, not for trees. `BOXES01-09`, `ICE01-05` and `UTILPOL1/2` inherit it too, so a behavioural trait hung on `^Tree` also lands on crates and ice floes — which is how `b74f2aaa` briefly made them invulnerable. The template carries a comment saying so (`rules/ingame/decoration.yaml`, the `NO Inherits@Indestructible HERE` note inside `^Tree`); **check an abstract template's inheritor list before hanging anything on it.**

### The warhead's per-cell actor test is a CONJUNCTION, and the state it reads is the state at IMPACT, not at authoring time

`Warhead.IsValidAgainst` is one
expression — `ValidTargets.Overlaps(targetTypes) && !InvalidTargets.Overlaps(targetTypes)`
(`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Warheads/Warhead.cs`). A cell is skipped when **either** half rejects the
actor standing in it, so **deleting the clause that names your case does not grant it.** Three shapes make
the surviving clause bite where nobody expects it:

- **A vehicle husk is not a vehicle.** `^Husk` advertises `NoAutoTarget, Husk` and carries no `Ground`,
  `Water` or `Trees` (`rules/husks/husks-vehicles.yaml`), so the **default** `ValidTargets` of
  `Ground, Water` (`Warhead.ValidTargets`) never overlaps it. Removing `Vehicle` from an `InvalidTargets` list
  therefore admits nothing: the cell is still skipped, by the other clause.
- **An actor with no `Targetable` at all has an EMPTY target-type set**, and `BitSet.Overlaps` is false
  against any `ValidTargets` whatsoever. A dropped supply crate is in that state, and so is `^CivField`
  (deliberately — see §"Crop fields and trees carry a `Building` trait" in
  [`architecture.md`](architecture.md)).
- **Warhead delays reorder which state gets tested.** A nuclear weapon's damage warheads fire at
  `Delay: 0-1` and its smudge warheads at `Delay: 2-6`, so **every vehicle inside the blast is already a
  husk by the time the later warhead runs its test** — and the husk fails the `ValidTargets` half. A
  target-type edit made by reading the YAML is an edit against the wrong state.

**Rule: when a permission test is a conjunction, check the other clause against the state the actor will
be in at the moment the check runs.** The detector is cheap — name the target-type set the victim
actually advertises at that delay, not the one its live template declares.

**The terrain half of the same loop is a whitelist with the same failure mode.** Earlier in the same per-cell loop,
`LeaveSmudgeWarhead` drops any cell whose *terrain type* omits the smudge type from `AcceptsSmudgeType`
(`Warheads/LeaveSmudgeWarhead.cs`, `DoImpact`), and a `TerrainType@` block that names no `AcceptsSmudgeType`
accepts **none** — silently, with no lint and no log. Because terrain templates are multi-cell and
axis-aligned, that gate paints *rectangles*, while an actor-test skip paints single footprint cells: the
SHAPE of an unmarked region says which of the two gates dropped it. (Worked instance: TEMPERAT `Rock` and
`Cliffs` once accepted nothing at all; they and `Beach` now accept the five `Scar*` types only —
`TerrainType@Rock`, `@Cliffs` and `@Beach` in `tilesets/temperat.yaml`. `Crater` and `Scorch` are
deliberately absent from all three, because adding them would change every non-nuclear weapon in the
mod.) A `SmudgeType` that a terrain type accepts but no `SmudgeLayer` declares is a runtime
`NotImplementedException` (`Unknown smudge type`), not a lint error.

**And only the occupied cells (`x`, `X`, `+`) of a `Building` footprint are in the ActorMap**, so a per-cell actor test reaches
far fewer cells than `Dimensions:` suggests: `BuildingInfo.OccupiedTiles` (`Traits/Buildings/Building.cs`)
excludes `OccupiedPassable` (`=`) entirely. A `4,3` tree template with one `x` blocks one cell, not twelve.

### A warhead's `ValidTargets` is checked against `Air` once the impact clears `AirThreshold` — so an airburst on the wrong weapon detonates silently

*(Pinned by `OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/MissileStrikeArrivalTest.cs`.)*

`Warhead.AirThreshold` defaults to `128` — one eighth of a cell (`Warheads/Warhead.cs`). Above it,
`CreateEffectWarhead.IsValidAgainstTerrain` stops asking the terrain what it is and substitutes the `Air`
target type (`TargetTypeAir`, `Warheads/CreateEffectWarhead.cs`); a warhead that does not list `Air` then returns from
`DoImpact` before spawning anything. Combined with the per-warhead `Ground, Water` default documented above,
**raising a detonation off the deck deletes the explosion sprite, the impact sound and the crater** while the
`SpreadDamage` rows — which test the *victim*, not the terrain — go on working. A strike that damages things
with no visible explosion.

`^HugeExplosionEffects` (`weapons/weapons-effects.yaml`), inherited by both `IskanderExplosion` and
`MOPPenetration`, writes `ValidTargets: Ground, Ship, Trees, Mine` on every `CreateEffect` row and never
`Air`. `Atomic` is the one warhead in the mod written for an airburst and says so twice: an explicit
`ValidTargets: Ground, Water, Air` on `Warhead@Fireball` (`Atomic`, `weapons/weapons-superweapons.yaml`) and
`AirThreshold: 10c0` on all ~30 of its damage, fire, EMP, suppression and smudge rows — which makes
**10240 a hard ceiling on the nuke's burst height**. Above it the missile would still fly, still be aimed,
still be announced, and do nothing to the ground.

**Deleting `Targetable` to make an actor unacquirable makes it immune to every damage warhead instead.**
`WeaponInfo.IsValidAgainst(Actor, Actor)` reads `victim.GetEnabledTargetTypes()` and gates on
`IsValidTarget` (`GameRules/WeaponInfo.cs`), and splash warheads run each candidate through it — an actor
with no enabled target types is an invalid victim for every weapon in the game. **Override `TargetTypes`
to a value nothing lists** rather than removing the trait.

### A valid ACTOR at the impact point DELETES the terrain test, so a water effect can splash on a bridge deck

The natural hypothesis — that impact effects read the map's raw terrain instead of a bridge's custom terrain — is **false in this engine**: `Map.GetTerrainIndex` consults `CustomTerrain` first (`Map.cs`), and an intact bridge template types its deck `Bridge`, whose target types do not include `Water`. The terrain answer is already correct. What goes wrong is one level up: `CreateEffectWarhead.DoImpact` consults terrain **only when no actor matched** — `if (actorAtImpact == ImpactActorType.None && !IsValidAgainstTerrain(world, pos)) return;` (`CreateEffectWarhead.DoImpact`, with the comment above it saying so outright) — and `ActorTypeAtImpact` returns `Valid` on the **first** actor the warhead is valid against. So **one matching actor is enough**, and `^Bridge` advertises `Targetable: TargetTypes: Ground, Water, Bridge` (`^Bridge` in `mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/civilian.yaml`) — it literally claims to be water. `Warhead.ValidRelationships` defaults to `Ally | Neutral | Enemy` (`Warheads/Warhead.cs`), so the bridge being neutral rules nothing out.

The guard is `InvalidTargets`, which beats `ValidTargets` (see the section below), so naming `Bridge` there is the whole fix — and both this mod and upstream RA already knew: WW3MOD applies `Ship, Structure, Bridge` to its water effect warheads from `^Explosion`'s `Warhead@EffectWater` down (`rules/weapons/weapons-defaults.yaml`). **Two things generalise past bridges.**

- **When an effect is picked by target type, ask which ACTOR is standing at the impact before you suspect the terrain query.** Terrain is the half with a lookup you can read and misread; the actor half has no lookup at all, just a bitset on a trait, which is why it does not attract suspicion.
- **An idiom applied fourteen times and skipped four is a defect, not a design.** The four misses here all traced by `git log` to one ancient WIP commit, i.e. they predate the idiom rather than being deliberately excluded. **Check the skipped ones before assuming intent.**

And a bound worth keeping, because it says which reports this does *not* explain: `HitShape.DistanceFromEdge` uses a **single** origin at `self.CenterPosition`, and a bridge inheriting `^1x1Shape` is a one-cell rectangle there. **So the bug was never the whole deck — it is roughly one cell at the span's centre, and hits at a bridge's ends were always correct.** Any report of splashes along a whole bridge is a *different* defect and must not be closed by this fix.

### A launcher that fires a DUMMY weapon: its firepower is not reachable from its `Armament`

Three shipped launchers bind their ammo pool to an armament whose weapon does nothing, because what is actually
delivered is a **spawned actor**: `iskander` and `HIMARS` through `MissileSpawnerMaster`, `DR` through
`CarrierMaster`. `IskanderTargeter` is an `InstantHit` carrying `Damage: 0`; `HIMARSTargeter` inherits it;
`DroneTargeter` is `Damage: 0` and its armament sets `AmmoUsage: 0` as well. **`IskanderTargeter`'s `Damage: 0` is
load-bearing**: its `Versus` table zeroes six classes but omits
`Kevlar`, and an omitted class takes the unmodified 100% (§"`Versus`: an OMITTED armor class is FULL damage"),
so any non-zero `Damage` lands in full on every soldier in the game (at its former `Damage: 50`, it did). **That table is still deliberately
incomplete and must not be "finished"**: `ArmorInfo.ProvideTooltipDescription` (`Traits/Armor.cs`) asks
whether *any* warhead in the ruleset names a type, so naming `Kevlar` even at 0 flips every infantry tooltip
from "None" to "Kevlar". The `Damage: 0` is what makes the weapon harmless; the zeroes are belt-and-braces.

**Consequence beyond tooltips: any code that assesses a unit's firepower by walking `Armament` → `Weapon` →
warhead concludes these three are harmless.** The damage lives on the spawned actor's own weapon
(`IskanderExplosion`), which is not reachable from the launcher's armament at all. Check for a spawner trait
before trusting a threat-assessment, scoring or UI path that walks armaments.

### `TargetDamage` scales by distance from the victim's CENTRE, so which cell of a building you clicked changes the damage

*(The arithmetic is driven against the shipped shape code by
`OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/SupportPowerAimPointTest.cs` rather than derived by hand.)*

`TargetDamageWarhead` multiplies every hit by `HitShape.CenterProximityPercent` (`Warheads/TargetDamageWarhead.cs`),
which normalises the offset from the victim's **centre** against its half-diagonal. A building's `Location`
— the cell a map places it on and the cell a player clicks — is its **top-left footprint cell**, and
`BuildingInfo.CenterOffset` puts the centre half the footprint away from it (`Buildings/Building.cs`).
So aiming at a multi-cell building's own map cell delivers a **fraction** of the warhead's stated damage,
and the fraction is per-cell rather than per-building: for a 3x3, the centre cell reads 100%, each of the
four mid-edge cells 52%, each of the four corners 33%; for a 2x2, every footprint cell is the same
`(512, 512)` from the centre and reads **50%**. Reading this as "a big building takes a third" understates
four of a 3x3's nine cells by 1.6x.

**`SpreadDamage` does not have this problem, and is the better primitive for anything aimed at structures.**
At the default `DamageCalculationType.HitShape` it measures `DistanceFromEdge`, which is **zero anywhere
inside** the shape. Widening `Spread` does not rescue a `TargetDamage` warhead either — `Spread` widens
*admission* only, and the proximity percentage is computed independently, so a wider spread admits more
victims at low percentages rather than restoring damage to the intended one.

**Which shipped weapons this reaches.** Only a `TargetDamage` warhead consults the percentage. Of the three
missile support powers, only the Kinzhal's `IskanderExplosion` carries one (its `Warhead@Target: TargetDamage`, `weapons/weapons-explosions.yaml`);
the GBU-57's `MOPPenetration` and the nuke's `Atomic` are `SpreadDamage` throughout and deliver identically
from any footprint cell — so a scenario built to demonstrate an aim-point fix on either of those would go
green having measured nothing.

**The support-power path is now closed; direct fire is not.** `SupportPowerAimPoint` resolves an order's
target to the occupying actor's `CenterPosition` inside `SupportPowerInstance.Activate`, so every support
power order — human, bot or Lua — lands on the centre. The same `IskanderExplosion` fired by the Iskander
launcher's own `Explodes` (`vehicles-russia.yaml`) never builds a support-power order and is untouched.

### An armour class is a TARGET TYPE, so a weapon can reach a victim by a channel neither of them names

`Targetable` decides whether a shot is **legal**; `Armor` decides what it **does on arrival**. They are independent axes — `DamageWarhead.DamageVersus` resolves `Versus:` multipliers from the victim's `Armor` trait only (`victim.TraitsImplementing<Armor>()` filtered by `a.Info.Type`) and never consults target types. But the *class name* appears on both axes, and an actor that advertises its armour class as a target type becomes reachable by every weapon that lists that class.

**Aircraft and ground vehicles carry the class in structurally different places, which is why a leak here goes unnoticed.** A vehicle folds it into its always-on ground targetable — e.g. `TargetTypes: Ground, Vehicle, Light` on `humvee` (`vehicles-america.yaml`) — which is correct, because a vehicle is never airborne. Aircraft split it into a separate per-airframe `Targetable@Armor`, beside the three targetables `^NeutralAirborne` gates (`aircraft.yaml`: `Targetable@Ground` on `!airborne`, `Targetable@Airborne` on `airborne`, `Targetable@REPAIR` on `!airborne && damaged`). An ungated `Targetable@Armor` announces the armour class **in flight**, and `GrenadeLauncher` — `ValidTargets: Infantry, Unarmored, Light` (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`), naming neither `Air` nor `Helicopter` — could then shoot a hovering littlebird out of the sky through `Light`. That was the shipped state until `15b6201a` gated it.

**Rule: any `Targetable` on an actor that has an `airborne` condition must be gated on it.** Every `Targetable@Armor` declaration carries `RequiresCondition: !airborne` (`grep -rn 'Targetable@Armor:' mods/ww3mod/rules` lists them), pinned by `AirborneArmorTargetableTest.EveryArmorClassTargetableIsGroundOnly`, which parses the three aircraft rules files from disk rather than a hardcoded roster — so a new airframe **in those files** is caught automatically (one added to a fourth rules file would not be). Count *declarations*, not airframes: `A10.Airstrike` and `FROG.Airstrike` inherit the gate from their parents and declare none of their own.

**The two deliberate ungated entries must stay ungated:** `Targetable@Helicopter` on `^Helicopter` and `Targetable@Drone` on `^Drone` (both `aircraft.yaml`). They are the designed channels by which heavy MGs, the dedicated AA guns and `DroneJammer` engage rotorcraft and drones — not the same defect. The weapon×airframe reachability matrix built from the parsed corpus when the gate landed showed **every AA weapon reaching air via `Air` or `Helicopter`, none via a bare armour class**, so the gate disarmed nothing; the rifle/GPMG `AirLight` channel is the separate, `airborne`-gated `Targetable@AirArmor`.

**An actor's armour class is TWO independent facts, and nothing validates that they agree.** `Armor: Type:` feeds `Versus:` and decides how much damage a hit does; the `Light`/`Medium`/`Heavy` string inside a `Targetable`'s `TargetTypes` decides whether a weapon may *aim* at the actor at all. They are separate YAML in separate traits, no lint compares them, and shipped actors do disagree with themselves — so **"which vehicles are Light?" has two different answers and you must say which one you are asking.** A census taken off `Armor: Type:` and used to reason about targeting (or the reverse) will be wrong on exactly the actors where it matters.

### `InvalidTargets` beats `ValidTargets`, so an armour class can silently cancel a declared capability

`WeaponInfo.IsValidTarget` is `ValidTargets.Overlaps(t) && !InvalidTargets.Overlaps(t)` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/GameRules/WeaponInfo.cs`) — **the exclusion wins.** The worked case is `^5.56mm` before its move to `AirLight`: it declared `ValidTargets: Infantry, Vehicle, Helicopter` with `InvalidTargets: Light, Medium, Heavy`, plainly intending "rifles hit helicopters but not armour". Every helicopter in the mod is light or heavy — TRAN, littlebird, HALO light; HELI, HIND, MI28 heavy; **none is medium** — so while the bare armour class leaked into flight, the exclusion tripped on every one of them and the rifle's declared anti-helicopter capability was **100% dead**, while the MGs that named `Helicopter` without excluding armour worked. Today `^5.56mm` is `ValidTargets: Infantry, Vehicle, AirLight` / `InvalidTargets: Medium, Heavy` (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`), and since a target type is an exact string, `InvalidTargets: Medium` does not cancel `AirMedium`.

**When a weapon both grants a capability in `ValidTargets` and excludes an armour class in `InvalidTargets`, check that no target of that capability advertises the excluded class.** The grant fails silently: there is no lint for a `ValidTargets` entry that can never match. See also [§A change believed made, documented as made, and inert](#a-change-believed-made-documented-as-made-and-inert) — this is one of its worked instances.

### A condition BAND and a condition LATCH look identical in YAML, and two traits gated on the pair MULTIPLY

The damage ladder ships **both** shapes and the suffix is the only tell. `heavy-damage-attained` is `ValidDamageStates: Heavy, Critical` — a **latch** that stays on all the way down — while `critical-damage` is `Critical` alone (`GrantConditionOnDamageState@HeavyDamageAttained` / `@CriticalDamage`, `mods/ww3mod/rules/defaults.yaml`). So **below 25% HP both conditions hold at once**, and a template that declares one modifier trait per condition has *both* enabled.

**`Util.ApplyPercentageModifiers` then MULTIPLIES them — there is no max-wins and no dedupe** (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Util.cs`). `133% x 200%` is `266%`, not `200%`. The infantry ladder does **not** have this shape: it uses the exclusive `light-damage` / `medium-damage` / `heavy-damage` bands, so **reading an `-attained`/`critical` pair as if it were the exclusive pattern is the trap**, and the mod's aircraft template is exactly such a pair (with a `PITFALL: these two bands STACK` comment naming this in `mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/aircraft.yaml`). *Anyone sizing a replacement ladder from a single band's number lands a third off.* **Size any change to a paired template for the PRODUCT, not for one band** — and see [§"Critical damage" names TWO thresholds](#engine-behaviors-that-surprise-debugging-gotchas) for the converse failure, where picking the non-latched condition leaves a consumer dark in the band it was meant to cover.

**And `ApplyPercentageModifiers` TRUNCATES** — it accumulates in `decimal` and returns `(int)a`. A 10-tick base at 133% is 13 ticks, not 13.3 and not 14; **small base values lose a meaningful fraction to the cast**, which is its own reason not to reason about a paired ladder from the percentages alone.

**Related misnomer, because it decides whether a rate-of-fire change happens at all: `BurstWaitMultiplier` does NOT scale reload, and does not scale the within-burst gap either.** Its `[Desc]` reads *"Modifies the reload time of weapons fired by this actor"* (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/Multipliers/BurstWaitMultiplier.cs`), but the only consumer applies it to `Weapon.BurstWait` — the delay *between* bursts (`Util.ApplyPercentageModifiers(Weapon.BurstWait, burstWaitModifiers)` in `Traits/Armament.cs`). `Weapon.ReloadDelay` and `Weapon.BurstDelays` (the shot-to-shot gap inside one burst) are both untouched. **A weapon with `Burst: 1` is therefore barely affected by it, and a weapon that leans on `ReloadDelay` is immune.** Check which of the three fields a weapon actually uses before predicting anything from this trait.

### `DamageState` has no `None`, so `Dead` is the only "never" a threshold field has — and that off-state is emergent

`DamageState` is a `[Flags]` enum starting at `Undamaged = 1` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Traits/TraitsInterfaces.cs`) — **there is no zero member.** So a trait field of that type cannot be switched off by the usual `None`, and the only value that reads as "never" is `Dead`.

It works, but for a reason that has nothing to do with switching anything off: a threshold test shaped `current >= threshold && current < DamageState.Dead` is false for every `current` when `threshold` is `Dead`. That `< Dead` clause exists to stop a killing blow being swallowed by the mechanism — **the off-position is a side effect of a guard written for an unrelated purpose, and is one edit away from silently switching back on.**

**Generalisable: when you rely on an "impossible" enum value as an off switch, check whether the impossibility is STATED or merely emergent — and if it is emergent, state it.** The repair is a one-line explicit sentinel (`if (threshold == DamageState.Dead) return false;`), provably behaviour-neutral because both spellings return false for every input, plus a `[Desc]` saying so. Same family as [§A change believed made, documented as made, and inert](#a-change-believed-made-documented-as-made-and-inert): the mechanism whose behaviour is produced by something other than the line you would point at.

### A duplicate trait key inside ONE actor: LAST value wins, at the FIRST key's position

**Scope first, because the rule inverts.** What follows holds while **one source** declares the actor. The moment a *second* source names it — a map's `rules.yaml`, or another mod rules file — the merge inspects the accumulated child list and a duplicate key becomes an `ArgumentException` **at rules load** (`MiniYaml.MergePartial(MiniYaml, MiniYaml)` → `Exts.IntoDictionaryWithConflictLog`; that helper throws, despite the name). A shipped duplicate is therefore not "inert" — it is a latent load error armed against anyone who overrides the actor, and since overriding a `^Template` from map rules is the recommended idiom (below), a duplicate inside a template is the sharpest form. `DuplicateChildKeyTest` (`engine/OpenRA.Test/DuplicateChildKeyTest.cs`) fails `dotnet test` on any such duplicate in the files the manifest merges and in each shipped map's declared rules; it does not scan autotest scenarios. Details and a no-build reproduction: `WORKSPACE/DISCOVERIES.md` 2026-08-17. With a single source:

Two same-named trait nodes inside a single actor do **not** produce two traits and do **not** throw. `ResolveInherits` hands each child to `MergeIntoResolved` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/MiniYaml.cs`), which on a repeat key finds the earlier node's index and writes `existingNodes[existingNodeIndex] = existingNode.WithValue(MergePartial(...))`. So the survivor **keeps the FIRST occurrence's position in the node list but takes the LAST occurrence's field values** — and the merge is per-field, so a later block that sets only *some* fields overrides only those and inherits the rest from the earlier one. (`MergeSelfPartial` is a *different* function that de-dupes only **top-level** keys per source and never sees this case.)

**The blast radius is bigger than a wrong number: the dead block is invisible to every reader AND to `make test`.** The YAML lint does not flag duplicate child keys — only `DuplicateChildKeyTest` does, and only over the files it scans — so an actor elsewhere can carry a plausible-looking stat the engine has never used, indefinitely. Any balance pass that reads top-down and stops at the first `Health:` tunes a value with no effect. (Real instance: `tunguska` declared `HP: 14000` and then `HP: 8000` thirteen lines later, and the actor fielded **8000**.)

- **`./utility.sh --resolved-rules <actor>` settles "which value wins" in one command** (`UtilityCommands/OutputResolvedRulesCommand.cs`) — it needs only a successful build, launches no match, and so is not gated by the no-autonomous-test-runs rule. Actor names are **case-sensitive** here even though actor lookup elsewhere is not — and the key it matches is the **YAML key verbatim**, not the lowercased ruleset key: `humvee` resolves, `mi28` returns *"Could not find actor"*, `MI28` resolves. Copy the key as written in the rules file. **Use it rather than hand-rolling a resolver for bulk "which actors carry trait X" sweeps.** A two-stage script (merge each actor across rule files, then apply `Inherits:`) must carry `-Trait:` nodes *through* the merge stage; the natural implementation applies each removal as it merges, where the definition table is still empty, so the node matches nothing and is then not carried forward. The removal vanishes and every descendant reports the trait as present — silently, and in the **over**-reporting direction. That is how `^CivBuilding`'s five `-Capturable*`/`-CaptureManager` lines (`civilian.yaml`) disappeared from a sweep. If a script is unavoidable, cross-check it against the utility on at least one actor with an explicit removal.
- **Technique worth reusing for any "clean up this YAML without changing behaviour" task:** capture `--resolved-rules <actor>` before and after and `diff`. Identical output is a byte-exact proof of behaviour-neutrality across the *whole* actor — inheritance, `-Removals`, faction variants and all — far stronger than eyeballing the edited lines, and it catches the blank-line/adjacent-entry merge accident below as a side effect.
- **Corollary when de-duplicating: preserving the resolved value is NOT the same as keeping the block you would expect.** Because the survivor sits at the *first* position but carries the *last* values, the behaviour-neutral edit is "keep the earlier block's slot, write the later block's values into it, delete the later block". Deleting the *first* block also preserves behaviour but silently relocates the trait in the file. **Deleting the second block while keeping the first block's values is the one option that changes behaviour — and it is the one that looks most obviously correct.**

### `Versus`: an OMITTED armor class is FULL damage, the opposite of a zero

`DamageWarhead.DamageVersus` (`Warheads/DamageWarhead.cs`) starts at `damage = 100`. An **empty** `Versus` early-returns that full damage (`Versus.Count == 0`). A **non-empty** one filters the victim's armors to the classes it *lists* — `Versus.ContainsKey(a.Info.Type)` — so an **unlisted** class matches nothing, `ApplyPercentageModifiers` runs over an empty sequence, and the victim takes the unmodified **100%**. **A table that zeroes six classes and omits a seventh is a full-damage weapon against the seventh, not a weak one.**

WW3MOD's armor population is exactly nine: `Concrete, Heavy, Indestructable, Kevlar, Light, Medium, None, Unarmored, Wood`. Two traps follow from that list:

- **`Kevlar` is `^Soldier`'s armor** (`infantry.yaml`) and reaches essentially the whole combat-infantry roster by inheritance, while non-`^Soldier` actors keep `^Infantry`'s `Type: None`. So a `Versus` table that zeroes `None` but omits `Kevlar` is harmless to a civilian and **lethal at full damage to every soldier** — selective in exactly the way that survives eyeballing.
  **The conditional above is currently unconditional.** `Type: Kevlar` is set exactly once in the whole ruleset, on that one template, and it appears in **zero** `Versus:` tables — enumerating every `Versus:` block under `mods/ww3mod/rules/weapons/` yields only the six classes `Concrete`, `Light`, `Medium`, `Heavy`, `None` and `Wood`, and **no table in the mod names an undefined class**. So **no warhead in the game modifies infantry damage by armour class at all**; every one of them lands on infantry at the unmodified 100%. `Unarmored` (on `truk`, `gtwr` and `^Drone`) and `Indestructable` are likewise absent from every table and likewise inert. *(A census — the enumeration is one command and needs no build; re-run it rather than trusting this sentence.)*
  The practical consequence: **the trait string is the phantom.** The unit tooltip's Armour row reads "None" for infantry only because `ArmorInfo.ProvideTooltipDescription` refuses to print a type no table names (see below). Any feature that surfaces armour by reading `Armor.Type` directly — an encyclopedia entry, a bot targeting heuristic — will present a protection class that does not exist, and will look *more* authoritative for having come from structured data rather than from prose. **The type name is not a claim about the damage model; only the `Versus` tables are.**
- **Writing a class name that does not exist is silently a no-op.** A table zeroing `Brick` accomplishes nothing, because `Brick` is not an armor class in this mod. **No shipped table does so**, but the hazard stands for anything newly written, and it is invisible: **no lint validates a `Versus` key.** `CheckUnknownWeaponFields` descends exactly one level into a warhead — it checks the warhead's own child node names against the warhead type's fields (`Lint/CheckUnknownWeaponFields.cs`, the inner `foreach (var warheadField in field.Value.Nodes)`), so `Versus` passes as a real field and its children are never looked at. No other file under `Lint/` mentions `Versus` at all. So a misspelt or invented armour class is accepted by `make test`, by `--check-yaml` and by the Windows merge gate alike, and the only signal is that the weapon quietly does full damage.

**So any "this weapon cannot hurt anything" judgement must be made against the ruleset's armor population, never against the table alone** — and three further shapes make such a judgement unprovable outright, because each ends at the same empty filter and therefore **fails open to full damage**: a null `ArmorInfo.Type`, a **conditional** `Armor` (it is a `ConditionalTraitInfo`, so it can be absent at runtime, and the filter also drops `IsTraitDisabled`), and a non-empty `HitShapeInfo.ArmorTypes` that excludes the class. `DangerFieldLayer.RulesetArmorTypes`/`WarheadIsHarmless` implement this test and fail open on all three; they are NUnit-pinned in `DangerFieldKernelTest`.

**Before writing an identity value into a `Versus` table to "make it explicit", grep for who reads the
table's KEY SET rather than its values.** `ArmorInfo.ProvideTooltipDescription`
(`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/Armor.cs`) decides what a unit tooltip's Armour row says by asking
whether **any** `DamageWarhead` in the entire ruleset names this type —
`rules.Weapons.Values…Any(wh => wh.Versus.ContainsKey(Type))`. It is a global existence check, not
a per-weapon one. So adding `Kevlar: 100` to a single table is mechanically a no-op on damage (100 is the
identity) and **flips that flag for the whole game**, changing every infantry tooltip from "None" to
"Kevlar" and advertising a protection the damage model still does not grant. `ArmourTooltipRowTest` cannot
catch it: it passes `discriminated` as a literal, so it pins the formatter and never exercises the lookup.
**Presence and magnitude are two different signals, and here one of them is load-bearing for the UI.**

**So when a `Versus` table is wrong, ask whether the defect is in its VALUES or its KEY SET before editing it — a value is local, a key is global.** `IskanderTargeter`'s dummy `Warhead@Target` zeroes six classes and omits `Kevlar`, so while it carried `Damage: 50` it dealt 50 to every soldier per shot; completing the table would have flipped every infantry tooltip. The levers that do not touch the key set are `Damage: 0` (what shipped, `IskanderTargeter` in `rules/weapons/weapons-missiles.yaml`, with the reasoning in-comment), `DamagePercent: 0`, or dropping the warhead. **One residue:** `DangerFieldLayer.WarheadIsHarmless` reads only `Versus`, never `Damage`, so a `Damage: 0` warhead is a **fail-open false positive** in the bot danger field; correcting that moves bot belief and breaks replay byte-identity, so it was left.

### `Penetration` is compared against `Thickness × ArmorDirectionPercent`, so reading it without the attack direction ranks weapons BACKWARDS

*(Arithmetic over read code — not measured in game.)*

`DamageWarhead` computes `effectiveThickness = thickness * armorPercent / 100` and passes **that**, not `Armor.Thickness`, to `ApplyPenetration` (`Warheads/DamageWarhead.cs`; helper `ApplyPenetration`, which returns full damage when `penetration >= thickness` and `damage * penetration / thickness` otherwise). `ArmorDirectionPercent` returns `Armor.Distribution[3]` — the **roof** entry — for a `TopAttack: true` warhead, and `[4]` for `BottomAttack`.

Worked on shipped data against `abrams` (`Thickness: 700`, `Distribution: 100,40,15,10,10` — `abrams`'s `Armor` in `vehicles-america.yaml`):

| | `Penetration` | effective thickness | damage |
|---|---:|---|---:|
| `ATGM` (`TopAttack: true`, `weapons-missiles.yaml`) | 100 | roof = 700 × 10% = **70** → penetrates | **10000** (full) |
| `RPG` (direct, `weapons-ballistics.yaml`) | 500 | frontal = **700** → does not | 6000 × 500/700 = **4285** |

**Read as bare fields, 100 against 500 says the dedicated anti-tank specialist is five times worse than a rifleman's throwaway rocket. The truth is the reverse** — and that comparison was filed as a suspected balance bug and had to be retracted (`bugs/discovered.md`, 2026-08-30). `Penetration: 100` is correctly sized against roof armour. The engine now carries the same warning inline at the call site, precisely so the local is not re-inlined.

Two rules follow. **(1)** Any surface showing penetration must also show attack direction, or it is actively misleading rather than merely incomplete. **(2)** `Armor.Thickness` alone never answers "will this hurt it" — the direction term is not a modifier you can round away; it is 10× on the weapon class designed to exploit it.

`TopAttack: true` is set at exactly five sites (census: `grep -rn 'TopAttack: true' mods/ww3mod/rules`): `ATGM` (`weapons-missiles.yaml`) and `^ArtilleryRound`, `GradRockets`, `TosRockets`, `M270Rockets` (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`; tube and rocket artillery). It is **not** on `RPG`, `TankRound.*`, `WGM.*`, `Hellfire.*` or `Stinger.quad` — so `ATGM` is the only infantry weapon in the mod that behaves this way.

#### `Armor.Type` and `Armor.Thickness` are two independent halves, and only one is gated on a `Versus` table

They are adjacent fields on one trait and are
consumed by different code on different terms:

- **`Type` is inert unless some warhead's `Versus` table names it.** `DamageWarhead.DamageVersus` applies a
  modifier only for classes a table actually lists; an unlisted class takes the default 100 % (the full rule is
  §"`Versus`: an OMITTED armor class is FULL damage" above, where `Kevlar` is the standing example).
- **`Thickness` is read straight off the trait on EVERY hit** and compared against `Penetration` whatever the
  type is called. It never consults a table.

So an actor can be meaningfully armoured through a type that no weapon in the game discriminates on. `gtwr` is
the shipped case — `Type: Unarmored, Thickness: 25` (`GTWR`'s `Armor`, `structures-defenses.yaml`) — and a tooltip that gated the whole armour row on the type
being discriminated suppressed the thickness with it. **Giving a class real protection is a `Versus:` edit in
the weapons files, or a `Thickness:`, never an `Armor.Type:` edit.** *(The tooltip's own discrimination test is
the global key-set existence check described above — which is also why writing an identity `100` into one
table changes every tooltip in the game.)* `Thickness` is **millimetres**
(`ArmorInfo.Thickness`'s `[Desc]`), and the roster is coherent in that unit (aircraft 3–20, APC 10–19, MBT 280–700, bunker 2000).

#### Five facings, four values — and the two flag-selected ones are hard switches

`ArmorInfo.Distribution` is `{ Front, Side, Rear, Top, Bottom }` **in percent** (`ArmorInfo.Distribution`), consumed
only by `DamageWarhead.ArmorDirectionPercent`. Three things that are easy to get wrong:

1. **`distribution[1]` is read for BOTH flanks**, so left and right cannot differ. Any UI must show one
   mirrored side number.
2. **The entries are percentages, never millimetres.** The per-facing mm figure is `thickness × armorPercent /
   100` (**integer** division) and exists as a number nowhere in the YAML.
3. **The horizontal facings interpolate; roof and belly do not.** The four cardinals are exact and the code
   blends between neighbours by impact angle, while `TopAttack` → `[3]` and `BottomAttack` → `[4]` are hard
   switches chosen by an explicit weapon flag — real distinct facings, not approximations of an angle.
   `BottomAttack` is authored on exactly one weapon, `ATMine`.

**Per-facing armour is a VEHICLES-ONLY feature and is near-mute across the roster** *(census — re-derive with
`grep -rn 'Distribution:' mods/ww3mod/rules` before relying on it)*: every `Distribution` in the mod is in `vehicles*.yaml`, and
