`ArmorDirectionPercent` returns a flat 100 % unless `distribution.Length == 5`, so aircraft, structures and
defences — which author `Thickness` but no `Distribution` — really are uniform. Of the fifteen vehicles that
author one, only `abrams` (`100,40,15,10,10`) and `t90` (`100,60,40,15,15`) are differentiated; thirteen carry
the identical flat `100,80,80,80,60` against a `^Vehicle` fallback of `100,50,25,10,10`. **A vehicle wearing
the APC boilerplate at MBT thickness gets an absurd roof** — at 280 mm that is 280 × 80 % = 224 mm
overhead against the T-90's 42 mm, halving `ATGM` effectiveness against it. No loaded hull does this today (every
flat-distribution vehicle is 5–19 mm); the one that did, `t72`, sat in a rules file `mod.yaml` never loaded. That is a class of authoring bug
invisible in the YAML, because the numbers look like every neighbour's.

### Bullets DO lead a moving target — the lead lives in `Armament`, not in `Bullet`

`Bullet` really does fly to a fixed point — `target = args.PassiveTarget` (`Projectiles/Bullet.cs`). That
reading is correct and it is the wrong **file boundary**: the lead is applied one level up, in `Armament`,
*before* `PassiveTarget` ever reaches the projectile. `Armament` (`Traits/Armament.cs`) enters a lead branch for any
`BulletInfo` projectile at a valid target (`Weapon.Projectile is BulletInfo bullet`), and calls
`WVec.CalculateLeadTarget(self.CenterPosition, initialPosition, targetPosition, Info.FireDelay, bullet.Speed.First().Length)`;
the result is added to the target's current position and only then assigned to `args.PassiveTarget`.

It is on by default, not opt-in. `ArmamentInfo.FireDelay` defaults to **3, not 0**, and its
own `[Desc]` says why: *"Cannot be 0 for Bullet Projectiles as it is used to calculate how much to lead target
by checking position change (speed) between this many ticks."* `initialPosition` is the target's position
exactly `FireDelay` ticks earlier — pushed in `FireBarrel`, popped FIFO inside the delayed-action lambda — so
the helper is differencing two real samples, not guessing.

**Two in-tree sources assert the opposite and both are wrong.** They post-date the lead code, which is why
this keeps being re-derived from the projectile file:

- `rules/weapons/weapons-ballistics.yaml`, in `30mm.Tunguska.AA`'s comment — *"Bullets do not lead (Bullet.cs:200 aims at the target's
  position at fire time)"*. It is load-bearing there for a tuning argument that wide scatter is a **buff**
  against movers; that argument does not survive, because the aim point is already displaced along the
  target's velocity. **Not corrected in place — this doc is the correction of record.**
- `WORKSPACE/recon/powers-interception.md` §4.2 — *"Guns do not lead. At all."*

**What is actually wrong with the lead is that it is a SINGLE iteration, not that it is missing**, and the
failure is counter-intuitive in two ways:

- The one-shot solve **over-leads against a closing target far worse than against a crossing one**, so a gun
  sited on the point being attacked is in its own worst case — the opposite of the intuition that a target
  driving at you is the easy shot.
- **Below one tick of bullet travel the lead is exactly zero**, because `ticksToReachTarget =
  distanceToTarget / projectileSpeed` is integer division. For a `Speed: 8c0` weapon that is every engagement
  inside 8 cells.

Any figure quoted for "how far a gun misses a mover" should say which of the two regimes it is in; a
no-lead derivation over-states the miss by roughly an order of magnitude at long range. Settle it empirically
rather than by reading: `WW3_GUNTRACE=1` (`GunTrace.cs`) makes `Bullet`'s `explode` trace line log `aimedAt=`, which is
either displaced along the target's velocity (lead live) or equal to its fire-time position (lead dead).

### A warhead field the consumer AGGREGATES across effects is not a per-effect field, and its zero is a global mute

Every `ShakeScreen` warhead in the mod used to produce **zero** screen shake. `ShakeScreenWarhead.Multiplier` defaulted to `new float2(0, 0)`, all nine ww3mod warheads omitted it, and the old `ScreenShaker.GetScrollOffset` computed `GetMultiplier() * GetIntensity() * (sin, cos)` where `GetMultiplier()` was the **sum of `Multiplier` across every live effect** — so four warheads each contributing `(0,0)` summed to zero and muted the whole product. `Intensity: 120` could not rescue it, because intensity was a separate factor in the same product.

**Two things made it survive years of play.** First, the tell nobody looked for: *every other mod in the tree sets it* — `engine/mods/ra/weapons/superweapons.yaml` (`Warhead@21Shake`), `cnc`, `ts` and `d2k` all write `Multiplier: 1,1`; ww3mod's nuke blocks were written fresh (`fa39d553`) and never did. Second, it was intermittent rather than dead: `ShakeOnDeath` uses the three-argument `AddEffect` overload, which passes `(1,1)`, so a building dying inside the nuke's shake window made the total non-zero and the nuke's `Intensity: 120` suddenly applied. A player got nothing on an empty field and a saturated buzz when the strike also collapsed a structure — **a user reporting a cosmetic effect as "annoying" was reporting a defect, not a taste preference.**

**The generalisation: nothing at the call site can reveal this, and only the consumer can.** `Multiplier` reads everywhere as "scale this one shake" and actually gated every concurrent shake in the world, so its default was a decision about everyone else's effects too. **When a trait or warhead field is summed, maxed or otherwise folded together with the same field on other instances, say so in its `[Desc]`.** The field now defaults to `1,1` and applies per effect (`ShakeScreenWarhead.Multiplier`, whose `[Desc]` carries the PITFALL inline).

### A warhead's documented effect is a claim about a class of VICTIM — verify it at the victim

`Atomic`'s `Warhead@TreeVaporize` / `@TreeFire_Kill` / `@TreeFire_Burn` / `@TreeFire_Singe` do **nothing to any tree the mod ships**. Every `T01`–`T17` and `TC01`–`TC05` actor carries `Inherits@Indestructible: ^TreeIndestructible`, which is a single `DamageMultiplier: Modifier: 0` (`mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/decoration.yaml`). `Health.InflictDamage` applies damage modifiers unless the caller passes `ignoreModifiers` (the `if (!ignoreModifiers && damage.Value > 0)` guard in `Health.InflictDamage`), so the vaporize warhead's 200,000 resolves to 0 — and so does the burn tick, because `ChangesHealth.Tick` goes through the same `self.InflictDamage(self, ...)` path (`ChangesHealth.cs`). Only `Health.Kill()` bypasses it (it passes `ignoreModifiers: true`), which no warhead reaches. This is stated plainly at the victim (the `^TreeIndestructible` header comment and `^Tree`'s own comment, `decoration.yaml`) and nowhere at the attacker.

**The warheads are still syntactically perfect, still lint-clean, still carry correct ranges and delays, and still fire on every detonation.** Nothing about reading the weapon file can reveal that their effect is multiplied by zero three files away — which is the shape worth carrying: when a warhead's documented effect is a claim about a class of victim, the victim can opt out from the other end of the codebase without the warhead changing at all.

`weapons-superweapons.yaml` states this at the attacker: the comments above each `Warhead@TreeVaporize` and each tree-fire group mark them INERT against every shipped tree. **The shape above still holds and is still the reason to read this section** — the warheads remain syntactically perfect, correctly sized, fired on every detonation, and multiplied by zero three files away.

### Weapons live under `Weapons:`, and a warhead override REPLACES rather than merges

Two authoring traps that both end in a confident wrong answer rather than an error.

**A map's weapon overrides belong in the file named by `Weapons:` in `map.yaml`, never `Rules:`.** They are separate manifest keys (`Map.cs`'s manifest-key table maps `Weapons` to `WeaponDefinitions`, distinct from `RuleDefinitions`). Put a weapon block in the rules file and the loader reads the weapon name as an **actor** and its `Warhead@…` children as **traits**, producing `Cannot locate type: WarheadInfo` — an error naming neither the file nor the weapon. The game then never reaches the Lua, no verdict is written, and the runner reports a **timeout rather than a rules error**. *Check `debug.log` for "Failed to load rules" before treating a hang as a hang.*

**`Inaccuracy` is a field of the `Projectile:` SUB-BLOCK, not of the weapon** — it is declared on `BulletInfo` (`Projectiles/Bullet.cs`), and `WeaponInfo` has no such field. Writing it as a direct child of the weapon leaves the resolved value at whatever the parent projectile block set. **Re-opening the sub-block MERGES with the inherited parent rather than replacing it** — `MiniYaml.MergePartial(MiniYaml, MiniYaml)` recurses into the children, so `Speed`, `Image` and the rest survive alongside the new value, confirmed in a resolved dump. *(This is stated deliberately against the warhead paragraph below, which describes the opposite behaviour for `Warhead@…` nodes. The two are not obviously reconcilable and the discrepancy has not been run to ground — if you are overriding either kind of sub-block, verify with a resolved dump rather than trusting either sentence.)*

> **A misplaced weapon field is not silent.** `CheckUnknownWeaponFields` walks every file in `Manifest.Weapons` and emits *"refers to a weapon field `X` that does not exist"* for any weapon-level child that is not a `WeaponInfo` field, `Projectile`, `Warhead` or `Inherits` (`Lint/CheckUnknownWeaponFields.cs`), and `mods/ww3mod/lint-baseline.txt` records no such waiver. The misplacement is a hard `--check-yaml` error.

**The countermeasure for "did my override actually take?" is the resolved dump, not care.** `./tools/combat-sim/scripts/dump-stats.sh` runs `--dump-balance-json` off `utility.ModData.DefaultRules` (`DumpBalanceJsonCommand.cs`) — i.e. post-inheritance — into `tools/combat-sim/data/stats.json` with lowercased keys. It is the only artefact that can tell you a value landed. The tell that one did *not*: the regenerated file is **byte-identical** to the previous one. (Check `_meta.generated_at` before trusting a committed copy; it is routinely stale.) `./utility.sh --resolved-rules <actor>` is the equivalent for traits.

**When overriding an existing warhead, restate its TYPE and every field the consumer reads.** `WeaponInfo.LoadWarheads` (`GameRules/WeaponInfo.cs`) builds each warhead with `Game.CreateObject<IWarhead>(node.Value.Value + "Warhead")` — **the node's *value* names the type**, so `Warhead@Spread:` written bare resolves `"Warhead"` and fails; write `Warhead@Spread: SpreadDamage`. And because the object is constructed fresh and `FieldLoader` fills only the fields present, **every omitted field falls back to the ENGINE default, not to the mod's value.** This is the opposite of the trait path, which merges per-field (see the duplicate-trait-key section above). The worked example: an override that restated only `Damage` and omitted `Penetration: 15` silently got the engine's `Penetration = 1` (`DamageWarhead.Penetration`), which against a `Thickness: 10` target took the reduction branch and cut the intended effect by an order of magnitude — **and the scenario still completed and reported `pass`**, returning a confident number about a world that was never built.

### "The override isn't taking effect" — blank lines are only the first of three causes

**1. Blank lines are significant.** Templates and top-level entries must be separated by a blank line. The parser silently merges adjacent ones, producing confusing override behavior — not a parse error. Cheapest to check, so check it first.

**2. Top-level keys merge with ORDINAL, CASE-SENSITIVE equality.** `MiniYaml.Merge` keys its tree with a plain `ToDictionary`, and every merge path underneath compares keys with `==` on a default-comparer `HashSet`/list scan (`MergePartial`'s `plainKeys`, `IndexOfKey`, `LastIndexOfKey`). So a rules override written `t03:` against `decoration.yaml`'s `T03:` does not override that actor — it contributes a **separate top-level key**. The `ToLowerInvariant` that makes actor names *feel* case-insensitive runs later and elsewhere (`Ruleset.LoadDefaults`, the `ActorInfo` ctor lambda inside the `MergeOrDefault("Manifest,Rules", …)` call, building from the already-merged tree), which is why a map's `Actors:` list may spell a type in any case (the `Actor` constructor lowercases before lookup) while **a rules override must match the defining file's casing exactly**. There is no rule of thumb: trees are uppercase `T01:`–`T17:`, most units are lowercase — read the defining file.

How it surfaces depends on what is inside the mis-cased block, and none of the three messages mentions case:

- **Contains a `-Trait:`** → `There are no elements with key 'X' to remove`, thrown during inheritance resolution (`MiniYaml.ResolveInherits`). The trait was never present *in any source for that key*, because the key is new. This fires first and masks the next one.
- **Field overrides only** → both keys survive to `Ruleset`, both lowercase to the same name, and `ToDictionaryWithConflictLog` throws `LoadFromManifest<Rules>, duplicate values found for the following keys` (`Exts.IntoDictionaryWithConflictLog`).
- **An abstract `^Template`** → **silent.** The `filterNode` argument drops `^`-prefixed keys before the duplicate check (in `Ruleset.LoadDefaults` for mod rules and `Ruleset.Load` for map rules), so a mis-cased `^vehicle:` never collides; it simply sits unused, because `Inherits:` also resolves ordinally (`ResolveInherits`'s `tree.TryGetValue`). The only tell is a `Parent type '^Vehicle' not found` if nothing supplies the correctly-cased name.

Both throwing forms prevent the mod loading **at all** — every map, not just the one at fault, because rules resolve once at load. Inside a map's `rules.yaml` even that is swallowed and reappears elsewhere; see [§Removing an inherited trait](#removing-an-inherited-trait--key-matches-the-full-node-key).

**3. Ordering inside the actor.** `Inherits@` and `-Key:` both apply at the point they appear, so a later parent can overwrite the field you set and a removal above its `Inherits@` is undone by it — [§Precedence inside an actor is POSITIONAL](#precedence-inside-an-actor-is-positional--a-later-inherits-overwrites-an-earlier-own-field-including-a-maps-override).

**Detector for all three:** `--dump-balance-json` ([below](#resolve-the-ruleset-before-you-trust-an-inheritance-edit---dump-balance-json)) resolves the whole ruleset in seconds without a game slot.

**The same case fact makes a case-sensitive READ unsound, and that direction has its own cost.** Everything above is stated as a hazard for **writing** YAML. But actor names are lowercased only *after* the merge, so `grep "^heli:"` is **not a test for whether the actor `heli` exists** — and its negative result looks exactly like proof of absence. The shipped mod mixes conventions freely *within a single file* (`aircraft-america.yaml` defines both `littlebird:` and `HELI:`), so a sweep over every rules file for `^heli:` and `^tran:` returns nothing while both actors are defined in uppercase and resolve normally. **Rule: grep for actor definitions with `-i`, always.** The near-miss it produced was a short and entirely wrong chain of inference — `[ActorReference]` → the indexer throws on a missing key → therefore picking that starting-units package crashes the match at world load — and what should have stopped it sooner was that **two independent signals said the actor existed**: the lint gate passes at its recorded baseline, and an `[ActorReference]` to a genuinely undefined actor would have to be either an error or a baselined one. **When a cheap local check contradicts a gate that runs over the whole ruleset, suspect the check.**

**Deleting a line from a MiniYaml block is a TWO-SIDED edit, and the silent side is the dangerous one.** Removing one key can eat the newline **above** it — gluing the following trait onto the previous value, e.g. `OwnerType: InternalName<TAB>RenderSprites:` — and it can eat the blank separator **below** it. The loader then reports `FieldLoader: Cannot parse 'InternalName\tRenderSprites:' into 'OwnerType'`, which reads as a problem with a field nobody touched. **When a FieldLoader parse error quotes a value containing another key's NAME, the defect is a missing newline, not a wrong value.**

The blank-line half fails silently and is the expensive one. Cause 1 above says blank lines between top-level entries are significant; what is worth adding is *how you get there without meaning to*: **the blank line is usually the line immediately after the last key in a block, so deleting that last key and deleting the separator are the same keystroke.** Three of four defects in one routine deletion arrived that way, and the loud glued-newline failure is what masked them — had it not fired, the mod would have loaded with one actor silently absorbing another, which is the far more expensive outcome.

**No gate but the YAML one can see this class at all.** Neither the Release build, nor the Debug analyzer gate, nor the NUnit suite loads mod rules — so **a green build and a green suite say nothing whatsoever about whether the mod can start.** And **a red YAML gate is not "one lint error": it may mean the sweep never happened.** A failure inside `Ruleset.LoadDefaults` aborts before the first `Testing map:` line, produces a few hundred lines against the ~111,000 a completed sweep produces, and validates **nothing** — not the other YAML in the same merge, not one map. **Check whether the run reached `Testing map:` before concluding anything about scope: an early abort exonerates every file it never read.** Same family as the zero-byte-log and piped-exit-code traps recorded elsewhere in this document.

### MiniYaml's indent arithmetic is not `leading_whitespace / 4` — a tab is one level and does not reset the space count

`MiniYaml.cs`'s line scanner treats a tab as one level on its own
**and does not reset the running space counter**, while a leftover run of 1-3 spaces is discarded and
contributes nothing. So a tab-two-spaces-tab prefix is **two** levels, six spaces is **one**, and two spaces
is **zero**. **Any tool reasoning about YAML structure by a regex on leading tabs silently returns an empty
result for a space-indented file** - and an empty result from a structural checker reads exactly like a
clean one. `lua_gate.map_actor_names` did that, and would have switched off every actor-global check for
such a scenario while still printing OK. If you write a static checker over this tree's YAML, port the
arithmetic rather than approximating it, and give the checker a file it is required to FAIL on.

### Removing an inherited trait: `-Key` matches the FULL node key

`-TraitName:` removes an inherited node only by **exact key match, including any `@label`** — `ResolveInherits` does `resolved.RemoveAll(r => r.Key == removed)` and throws `There are no elements with key '<key>' to remove` if nothing matched (`MiniYaml.cs`). So `-SquadManagerBotModule:` does **not** remove `SquadManagerBotModule@experimental.america.fixedwing`; you must write `-SquadManagerBotModule@experimental.america.fixedwing:`. There is no "remove all instances of this trait type" wildcard form — each labeled instance must be listed.

**Prefer ATTACHING a trait to the actors that need it over REMOVING it from the actors that do not.** Because a `-Key:` matching nothing is fatal, every removal is a standing dependency on a shared parent continuing to supply that trait — so the day someone takes the trait off the parent, *all N* removals throw at once and the mod stops loading, at N sites none of which were touched. Attaching cannot fail at load, and it makes the revert one edit instead of N.

**A REDUNDANT removal is a hard error too, and inside a map's `rules.yaml` that error is SWALLOWED.** Stripping a trait on a base actor and then stripping it again on a derived actor that already `Inherits:` the stripped parent leaves the second `-Key:` with nothing to match, so it throws like any other mismatch. In mod rules that surfaces as a load-error dialog; in **map** rules it does not — `Map.PostInit` wraps the whole rules load in a `catch`, logs `Failed to load rules for <title> with error` to `debug.log`, sets `InvalidCustomRules`, and **falls back to `Ruleset.LoadDefaultsForTileSet`** (`Map.PostInit`; the scenario-rules loader `Map.ApplyScenario` has an identical catch). Every actor type the map defined then ceases to exist. The map does not report a rules problem — it dies later and elsewhere, typically in the density pass at `actorsRules[actorType]` (`Map.SetDensityLayer`) with `KeyNotFoundException: The given key '<your custom actor>' was not present in the dictionary`, which reads exactly like a typo in the map's actor list and is nothing of the sort. **On a `KeyNotFoundException` naming a map-defined actor type, grep `debug.log` for `Failed to load rules` before debugging the actor list.**

### A merged top-level node means ONE FILE CANNOT ANSWER "is this gated?"

`Player:` is defined in five loaded files and MiniYaml merges them, so a trait block for one actor can be split across the tree — and a reader who opens the file the block "belongs" in and finds no `Prerequisites:` line has established **nothing about the gate**. The worked instance cost a confident, reviewed, propagated-into-three-files wrong conclusion: `nuclear-arsenal.yaml` really does declare no tier for its own entries, and every one of them is tiered in `player.yaml`'s `Player:` node, deliberately kept in one block because a tier table split across two files is a table nobody can read. **The premise was literally true and the conclusion was false, which is exactly why it survived review**, and nothing in the file being read says the other half exists.

**Resolve the ruleset instead of reading a file** — `--dump-balance-json`, or a fixture reading the same files through `MiniYaml.FromFile` (see §"Resolve the ruleset before you trust an inheritance edit"). Two corollaries:

- **A hand-rolled text scan over MiniYaml is evidence about your regex, not about the file, until a real parser agrees with it.** Both of these produced confident false alarms inside one task: `grep "^\t\tKey:"` in bash **double** quotes matches nothing, because the shell passes a literal backslash-`t` and basic grep reads `\t` as `t`, so the pattern means `^ttKey:` and returns a clean-looking empty result (use `grep -P`, `$'\t'`, or no anchor — and run a control that MUST match before believing an empty result); and an `awk` keyed on the last-seen block header **mis-attributes fields across intervening blocks**, which here attributed ten `RequiresCondition:` lines from *actor* definitions to the final power in the `Player:` node and manufactured a gate that did not exist. **When a quick scan and a parsing reader disagree, suspect the scan** — it is the one with no scope, no merge and no indentation model.
- **A capture taken with a lobby option that disables the mechanism under discussion cannot be evidence about the shipped configuration.** "I saw it in a screenshot" is the register in which that goes unchallenged, and the scenarios that exercise a feature are precisely the ones most likely to have switched its guard off. **Before citing a capture as evidence about default behaviour, read that scenario's `rules.yaml` for the option that makes the capture possible.** The general form is worse than one bad screenshot: *a scenario that switches a gate off to focus on something else stops being evidence about anything downstream of that gate*, and its green is genuine, so nothing in the tree flags it.

**Free gate nobody had mentioned: `--check-yaml` REFUSES a scenario map with no playable slot.** A new scenario whose players are all map-authored combatants fails the single-scenario lint with `Error: Found no playable player` and exit 1, before any launch. `PlayerReference.Playable` defaults to false and plenty of traits deliberately do not test it, so an all-non-`Playable` cast is otherwise a perfectly reasonable scenario shape. One `Playable: True` satisfies it.

**A test roster that reads `MiniYaml.FromFile` sees RAW nodes, so it is blind to anything a node INHERITS.** Four nuclear rosters — `NuclearYieldTest.AllNukes` (`OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/NuclearYieldTest.cs`), the `Warhead@TreeBurn` count in `BurntTreeScopeTest`, the per-file smudge count in `ScarUnderActorsTest`, and the `YIELDS` tables in `tools/nuke-light/gen_fireball_light.py` / `tools/nuke-shake/gen_shake.py` — iterate unresolved nodes, so a weapon whose body is `Inherits:` plus removals plus one override declares nothing they count and moves none of them. **Cuts both ways:** a SUBCLASS correctly stays out (its physics is checked once, at the base, and listing it would hand the roster a raw node missing the warheads its invariants test), while a SIBLING that restates the same physics is exactly what the roster exists to catch and looks identical from outside. Ask of any roster before adding to it: **"does this node STATE the quantity the roster checks, or inherit it?"** `ExchangeVariantTest.TheVariantsStateNoPhysicsOfTheirOwnAndSoJoinNoRoster` pins the inherit answer.

### `Rules:` as an INLINE VALUE is a file list, not a child block — and a static tool that walks child nodes sees nothing

`MiniYaml.Load` treats a non-null `mapRules.Value` as a file list and appends it to the mod's rule files (`MiniYaml.Load`, `engine/OpenRA.Game/MiniYaml.cs`), separately from `mapRules.Nodes`. Both forms are legal and several shipped maps and scenarios use the inline one. **Any static tool that reads a map by walking `Rules:`' child nodes therefore returns no overrides at all for those maps** — silently, and in the direction that looks like "this map overrides nothing". A map that overrode a **locomotor** that way would be invisible to `nav-guard`. Related: [§"Maps must declare `Rules: rules.yaml`"](#maps-must-declare-rules-rulesyaml).

### Resolve the ruleset before you trust an inheritance edit: `--dump-balance-json`

```sh
Mod=ww3mod ./utility.sh --dump-balance-json      # exits 0 only if the whole ruleset resolves
```

**Run this after any change to YAML inheritance — a `-Trait:` removal, a template reparent, a new `Inherits@`.** The command reads `utility.ModData.DefaultRules` (`UtilityCommands/DumpBalanceJsonCommand.cs`) and then walks every actor and every weapon, so it forces the full `ResolveInherits` pass that the game itself runs at startup. It takes seconds, needs no scenario and steals no window focus, and `utility.sh` runs under `set -e` so a load failure propagates as a non-zero exit.

**It catches a class of breakage that both other gates miss.** A change can compile clean and pass the whole NUnit suite and still stop the mod from starting, because the failure is neither a compile error nor a unit-testable one — it is a rules-resolution error raised at load. Unit tests do not resolve mod YAML; the build does not read it at all.

**It also gives a removal POSITIVE evidence, which is otherwise hard to come by.** Because a `-Key:` matching nothing is fatal rather than ignored (see [§Removing an inherited trait](#removing-an-inherited-trait--key-matches-the-full-node-key)), a clean exit proves the node was really there and really came out. Without that, "I removed the trait" and "I wrote a line that did nothing" look identical in the diff.

This is the cheap gate; it is not the thorough one. It proves the ruleset *resolves* — not that lint is clean and not that anything behaves. `make test` remains the lint authority.

### WW3MOD's map grid is `Rectangular`, not `RectangularIsometric`

`mods/ww3mod/mod.yaml`'s top-level `MapGrid:` block sets `TileSize: 24,24, Type: Rectangular`. So cell neighbours are the plain `(x±1, y±1)` grid, a Manhattan/Chebyshev disc maps directly to spatial adjacency, and `CellLayer.Contains(CPos)` has no `X<Y` isometric rejection. This is what lets the density-neighbour idiom (`CohesionMoveModifier.CoverScore`, and the Phase-1 map layers) step cells with raw `CVec` offsets.

**A POSITION carries no foreshortening; the ARTWORK does, and by 0.566 — so a facing is NOT the angle the sprite is drawn at.** Square tiles mean `WorldRenderer.ScreenPosition` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Graphics/WorldRenderer.cs`) maps the ground plane to the screen 1:1. The sprites were drawn from a camera **34.5° above** that plane: exporting all 32 frames of `iskander-missile.shp` (`OpenRA.Utility --png`) and fitting the principal axis of each frame's opaque pixels puts frame F at the screen angle of F's ground vector **with its y component multiplied by 0.566** — a least-squares fit with 0.47° RMS residual over the whole set. This is inherited RA convention rather than a mod defect; vanilla `ra` ships the same grid and the same style of art.

**The error is a derivative, not an offset, which is why it hides.** At the four cardinals the drawn angle matches the facing exactly (0.22–1.49° measured residual), so a north/south or east/west spot check agrees and confirms nothing. It peaks at 16° on the diagonals, and — the part that actually breaks models — the *rate* differs everywhere: near due north one WAngle unit swings the drawn nose by `1/0.566 = 1.77×` what the facing says, near due east by `0.566×`. Identical arithmetic therefore produces 3.1× different results at different headings.

**Do not model it as a correction on top of a ground facing.** It does not vanish as an arc flattens and has nothing to do with vertical motion — a perfectly level object on a diagonal is already drawn 15.5° off its own ground track — so anything that starts from the ground facing and adds a term is structurally unable to remove it. Convert the direction through the artwork once and read the facing off: setting the squashed drawn vector parallel to the true screen velocity gives `F = Yaw(vx, (vy - vz) / squash)`. The first fix for this composed instead, and shipped a *constant* drawn-nose error of up to 16° on every non-cardinal heading, present at every point of the flight. The number is exposed as `BallisticMissileInfo.SpriteFacingSquash` (default 1000 = true 1:1 art, so adopting it changes nothing), read in the `BallisticMissileFly` constructor (`Activities/BallisticMissileFly.cs`). **Re-measure per facing set — 0.566 is this art's number, not a property of the engine** — and convert before comparing a sprite's apparent direction against anything positional: turret lead indicators, formation facing, projectile pitch, aircraft bank, a debug overlay drawing a heading arrow.

### Footprint characters decide where a unit may STOP, and two near-identical indices disagree about where a building IS

`FootprintCellType` (an enum in `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/Buildings/Building.cs`) has five characters, and the
four list-builders below it — not the names — are what decide behaviour:

| char | name | `Tiles()` (placement blocks, `BuildingInfluence`) | `OccupiedTiles()` (`ActorMap`) | `PathableTiles()` | may a unit STOP there? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `_` | Empty | no | no | yes | yes |
| `=` | OccupiedPassable | yes | **no** | yes | **yes** |
| `+` | OccupiedPassableTransitOnly | yes | yes | no | **NO** |
| `x` | Occupied | yes | yes | no | no (blocked) |
| `X` | OccupiedUntargetable | yes | yes | no | no (blocked) |

`Tiles`, `OccupiedTiles`, `PathableTiles` and `TransitOnlyTiles` are all `BuildingInfo` methods in the same file.
The stop/no-stop line is drawn by `Locomotor.CanStayInCell` (`Traits/World/Locomotor.cs`), which returns
`!CellFlag.HasCellFlag(HasTransitOnlyActor)`, and only `+` cells set that
flag (in `Locomotor.UpdateCellBlocking`, fed from `Building.TransitOnlyCells()`). **A dock or parking cell must therefore be `=`**;
a unit sent to a `+` cell arrives and is immediately shoved off by the idle handler.

**The two indices answer different questions, and their names do not say which.** `Building` registers
`Info.Tiles(Location)` with `BuildingInfluence` (in `AddedToWorld`) but `Info.OccupiedTiles(TopLeft)` as its
`OccupiedCells` (in its constructor), which is what the `ActorMap` influence layer is keyed on:

| index | keyed on | includes `=` | question it answers |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ActorMap.GetActorsAt` | `OccupiedCells` | **no** | *can something stand here* |
| `BuildingInfluence.GetBuildingsAt` | `Tiles` | **yes** | *is the building here* |

`OccupiedCells` omitting `=` is correct and deliberate — the pathfinder is being told the truth about a cell
units may walk through. **Reading it to answer "does this cell hold an actor" is what is wrong**, and the
names are close enough that the mistake survives careful review and every solid-footprint building.
Anything asking "what is at this cell" for a reason other than movement — targeting, aiming, selection,
tooltips, capture ranges — wants `BuildingInfluence` or a hitshape test. The measured instance:
`SupportPowerAimPoint` resolved a clicked cell through `GetActorsAt` alone, found no actor on a passable
footprint cell, did not snap, and delivered a fraction of a centred shot's damage; it now reads both
(`Traits/SupportPowers/SupportPowerAimPoint.cs`, `CandidatesAt` and `FootprintSize`). The same fix's candidate ranking had to move
from `OccupiedCells().Length` to `Info.Tiles` for the same reason — the former under-counts any building
with passable cells.

**Two consequences that are easy to miss.**

- `targetableCells` is built from `x` **only** (`Building`'s constructor: `FootprintTiles(TopLeft, FootprintCellType.Occupied)`), so a footprint made entirely of `=` and `+` has
  **no** targetable cells. Such an actor needs explicit `HitShape.TargetableOffsets`, and turning on
  `UseTargetableCellsOffsets` would leave it with no targetable position at all. `LOGISTICSCENTER` is
  exactly this shape (`rules/ingame/structures.yaml`, `Footprint: ++ =+`) and carries the
  offsets (its `TargetableOffsets:` line).
- **A third index disagrees with both: an actor's `CenterPosition` is the DIMENSIONS-BOX centre, which need not sit over any occupied cell.** `Building`'s constructor sets `CenterPosition = Map.CenterOfCell(TopLeft) + Info.CenterOffset(world)`, and `BuildingInfo.CenterOffset` is half the diagonal of the **`Dimensions` box**, not of the footprint. For a `Dimensions: 2,2` actor whose `Footprint` occupies one corner cell — `__ x_`, the shape most of the mod's trees use — the centre lands on the box's interior corner, **724 units (0.71 cells) diagonally off the occupied cell's own centre**. Anything anchored at `CenterPosition` inherits that offset: `ProximityExternalCondition` registers its trigger at `self.CenterPosition + Info.Offset` (`Traits/Conditions/ProximityExternalCondition.cs`, `INotifyAddedToWorld.AddedToWorld`, re-anchored in `ITick.Tick`), so a radius **smaller than the box offset covers no cell centre at all** and the aura fires for nobody. The shipped instance: `^TreeHusk` carried `Range: 384` (its `ProximityExternalCondition@ObjectProximity` in `husks/husks.yaml`) against a 724-unit offset and granted `object-proximity` on 0.0% of cells until it was corrected. **When siting an aura on a multi-cell building, either add the box offset to the radius or cancel it with `Offset:`** — the per-husk `Offset:`/`Range:` pairs on the `T##.Husk` actors in `husks.yaml` and the derivation in the header comment of `rules/ingame/decoration.yaml` are the worked examples. Related: radius on a cell-placed population is a **step function**, since only discrete separations exist, so a radius mid-plateau is free robustness over one sitting on a plateau edge.
- Changing a cell from `+` to `=` **removes the post-service bounce.** A unit parked on a transit-only
  cell is pushed off by `Mobile.OnBecomingIdle`; on an `=` cell it stays. Anything that relied on that
  shove to free a dock needs an explicit vacate — see
  [`architecture.md` §Resupply docking](architecture.md#resupply-docking-an-even-dimensioned-host-needs-a-declared-dock-cell).

**There is a THIRD way to consult occupancy, and it fails in a way neither of the first two does: `ActorMap.FreeSubCell`.** A cosmetic full-cell actor cannot be filtered out of its *result*, because it does not occupy one subcell — it registers as `SubCell.FullCell`, and `AnyActorsAt` matches a FullCell occupant against **every** subcell query (the static `AnyActorsAt(InfluenceNode, CPos, SubCell, bool)` overload in `Traits/World/ActorMap.cs`). One such actor therefore makes the whole cell report full and the plain overload returns `SubCell.Invalid`. **The predicate overload (`FreeSubCell(CPos, SubCell, Func<Actor, bool>)`) is the only way through**; `CellOccupancy.FreeBlockingSubCell` wraps it, as `CellOccupancy.AnyBlockingBuildingAt` wraps the `BuildingInfluence` half.

- **`SubCell.Invalid` is NOT the value a non-sharing unit legitimately gets, and the enum reads backwards.** `SubCell : byte { Invalid = byte.MaxValue, Any = byte.MaxValue - 1, FullCell = 0, First = 1 }` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Traits/TraitsInterfaces.cs`, `enum SubCell`) — so `FullCell` is **zero** and a `… : 0` fallback branch is the *valid* one, which reads as "Invalid" to anyone assuming the usual ordering. A unit handed an out-of-range subcell keeps it for the rest of the match (`Mobile` pins `FromSubCell`/`ToSubCell` to the init value) and does not crash, because the offset lookup falls back to cell centre.
- **A PARTIAL sweep can be worse than no sweep.** When one end of a producer/consumer pair is taught to see through a class of actor and the other is not, "X never happens here" becomes "X happens here and is then destroyed". Convert both ends or neither.
- **Widening one "is this cell free" test can make a NEIGHBOURING one newly reachable.** Ask what the cell can now contain that it could not before — a predicate that was vacuously satisfied while placement was refused becomes live the moment placement succeeds.
- **`Transforms.CanDeploy` tests a footprint CENTRED on the vehicle, not the cell under it** (`Traits/Transforms.cs`, via `Transforms.Offset` and the target building's `Dimensions`/`Footprint`), and every `=` or `+` character counts because `BuildingInfo.Tiles()` returns both — so a single blocker anywhere in the target footprint refuses the deploy, which reads to a player as a refusal *beside* the obstruction rather than on it. **It also consults neither prerequisites nor buildable area**: it calls only `World.CanPlaceBuilding`, so a `RequiresBuildableArea` on the target does not gate a transform deploy.

**Shipped examples:** `SUPPLYROUTE` is 3x3 `=+= +++ =+=` (its `Footprint:` in `structures.yaml`)
— all four corners stoppable, five transit-only cells. `LOGISTICSCENTER` is 2x2 `++ =+` (same file) with
exactly one stoppable cell, `(0,1)`, under the crane. Neither has ever blocked movement.

### `Dimensions` is a BOUNDING BOX, not the shape — a `CenterPosition`-anchored trait on a padded Building fires off-centre

*(Geometry over read code and authored YAML — never launched.)*

A Building's `CenterPosition` is the centre of its **`Dimensions` box**, not of its authored footprint:
`BuildingInfo.CenterOffset` is `(CenterOfCell(dim) - CenterOfCell(1,1)) / 2` = `512 × (dim - 1)` per axis
(`Traits/Buildings/Building.cs`), applied once in `Building`'s constructor. Any trait that anchors at
`self.CenterPosition` therefore acts from the box centre — and **when the footprint carries an empty padding
column, the box centre is displaced into the padding, away from the mass a player sees.** The error scales
with the padding, not with the actor's size.

The live case was forest cover. `ProximityExternalCondition` anchors its trigger at `self.CenterPosition`
(`Traits/Conditions/ProximityExternalCondition.cs`, `AddedToWorld`), while the forest *shadow* reads `Building.Density`,
which `Map.SetDensityLayer` stamps per footprint cell at `location + d.Key`. Cover and shadow — the two halves
of forest concealment — were therefore anchored on different points, and the clump actors (`TC01` footprint
`==_ xx_`, `TC04` `x==_ xx=_ x___`, `Density: 0` in the padding) put only 27–50 % of their own authored density
inside their own 1-cell cover circle, against 100 % for the single-trunk trees the radius was chosen from.
The single-trunk case was the one the original reasoning was checked on; the clumps are the bulk of the forest.

**Fixed by a per-actor `Offset` on the trigger, not by a bigger `Range`** — `Offset: -512,0,0` on TC01–TC03,
`-1024,-512,0` on TC04, `0,512,0` on TC05 (each actor's `ProximityExternalCondition@ObjectProximity` in `ingame/decoration.yaml`, with the
derivation in that file's own header under `CLUMP TRIGGER OFFSETS`). Two things to carry:

- **The field is ADDED, not subtracted.** `ProximityExternalCondition` does `position += Info.Offset` at both
  call sites; the `[Desc]` used to say "Offset to subtract from center" and was backwards. It now states the
  box-centre trap in place (the `Offset` field's `[Desc]`).
- **The proximity test is STRICT** — `HorizontalLengthSquared < range.LengthSquared` — so a cell at exactly
  `Range` is **not** covered. Size an offset to leave margin rather than to land on the boundary.

A one-cell-radius emitter cannot span a 3-cell-wide mass whatever offset it is given (TC04/TC05 cap at 63.6 %
of their own density); that needs a second emitter. `T01.Husk` already carried an `Offset` workaround, so this
had been hit once before and not generalised.

### Disabling a string field: bare colon, not `""`

To clear a widget/chrome string field (`Background`, `Decorations`, `Separators`, `TooltipText`, …), use a **bare trailing colon**, never empty quotes. `FieldLoader.ParseString` returns the value verbatim, so `Separators: ""` parses as the literal two-character string `""`. That passes the `!string.IsNullOrEmpty` guards in the widgets, and the code then tries to load a chrome collection literally named `""` — e.g. `Sprite ""/separator was not found`. `Separators:` (bare colon) parses as null, `IsNullOrEmpty` fires, and the feature is skipped as intended.

**That holds only where nothing is INHERITED under the same key — in a merge, a bare colon KEEPS the parent's value.** `MiniYaml.FromLines` maps an empty value to `null` (`MiniYaml.cs`), and the merge leaf rule is `new MiniYaml(overrideNodes.Value ?? existingNodes.Value, …)` (the `MiniYaml`-pair `MergePartial`), so a null override never replaces an inherited value. That is a feature when re-pointing one leaf of an inherited block — `Warhead@FireballLight:` with only `Light:` under it still resolves as `LightEvent`, which is how the nuclear exchange variants override one field without restating the warhead type (pinned in `OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/ExchangeVariantTest.cs`) — and a trap when the intent was to clear it. **To clear an inherited field, remove the node with `-Key:` (and re-add what you need), not with a bare colon.**

### Maps must declare `Rules: rules.yaml`

A map's `rules.yaml` is loaded **only** when `map.yaml` names it under the top-level `Rules:` key. The engine maps that key to `Map.RuleDefinitions` (the `"Rules"` entry in `Map.cs`'s `YamlFields`, `required: false`); if it's absent, `MapField.Deserialize` returns early, `RuleDefinitions` stays `null`, and the map loads only mod defaults. The map still renders and actors still spawn, so the omission is **silent** — LuaScript references, AutoTarget overrides, and every rule tweak are simply ignored. The same applies to `Weapons:`, `Sequences:`, etc.

**Name the class, not the instance: there are FOUR ways a map YAML can be silently wrong, they live in four places, and no gate catches any of them.** Each is a *well-formed* document that means something other than what the author intended, so the file loads, renders, spawns every actor and reports nothing — to the player, to the log, or to `--check-yaml`. The only symptom is that the thing you wrote does not happen.

| Silent mode | Documented at |
|---|---|
| `map.yaml` omits `Rules:` / `Weapons:` / `Sequences:`, so the whole file is ignored | this section |
| A rules override mis-cases a top-level key (`e3:` against a defining `E3:`) and contributes a new actor instead of overriding | [§"The override isn't taking effect"](#the-override-isnt-taking-effect--blank-lines-are-only-the-first-of-three-causes) cause 2 — **silent only for `^Template` keys**; the other shapes throw, and loudly |
| A missing blank line between top-level entries, silently merging them | same section, cause 1 |
| `Inherits@` / `-Key:` ordering inside an actor, where a later parent overwrites the field you set | same section, cause 3 |

**The reason a worker who has met one has no reason to suspect the other three: three of them are causes of a section titled around the phrase *"the override isn't taking effect"*, which is a symptom you only search for once you already suspect an override.** The `Rules:` mode does not share that symptom at all — nothing is overriding because nothing was loaded — and a demo that is merely "too bright" gives no reason to type either phrase.

**The two halves also have different detectors, which is the trap inside the trap.** `--dump-balance-json` resolves the default ruleset and will surface a mis-cased key or a bad inherit, but it **never reads a map's `rules.yaml` at all** — so the one failure this section is about is invisible to the detector the section next door recommends. Before believing a map-scoped YAML edit took effect, confirm in this order: the file is named in `map.yaml`; the top-level key matches the defining file's casing byte for byte; blank lines separate top-level entries; the `Inherits@` above your override is not putting it back. All four are cheaper than one launch. A near-miss worth quoting: `demo-light-events` needs `Weapons: weapons.yaml` as a *separate* declaration from `Rules: rules.yaml` — declaring only `Rules:` would have loaded seven light envelopes and dropped the one weapon carrying the warhead emitter, i.e. a demo 6/7 correct, with the seventh looking like a bug in the warhead.

**THE CHAIN ABOVE IS ONLY HALF THE TAX: a bodiless actor also needs `AlwaysVisible:`.** `CheckDefaultVisibility` counts
`IDefaultVisibilityInfo` traits on every actor and wants **exactly one** — zero and two are both errors
(`Lint/CheckDefaultVisibility.cs`, message ``Actor type `X` does not define a default
visibility type``). `AlwaysVisibleInfo` is the trait that supplies it (`Traits/Modifiers/AlwaysVisible.cs`),
and nothing about an actor that is never created, never drawn and never in the world suggests a
*visibility* trait is required. **This is the more dangerous of the two faults because it is the quiet
one:** fixing only the `Interactable` chain removes the loud ``not constructible`` line and leaves the
other error on every actor on every map. Four buy proxies shipped that way drew **2,555 lint errors across
326 maps** from two faults per actor.

The two faults are one checklist, and the way to find the rest of it is to read the pass list once and ask
which passes take `foreach (var actorInfo in rules.Actors)` with no filter: `CheckDefaultVisibility`,
`CheckTraitPrerequisites`, `CheckTooltips` (Buildable only), `CheckInteractable` and
`CheckConflictingMouseBounds` all do, and between them they are the whole tax on a bodiless buildable
actor. **A trait's `Requires<>` is half of what the gates enforce, and the `ILintRulesPass` set is not a
single rule either** — satisfying the one pass a document names says nothing about the other passes
in `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Lint/` (one file each; `ls` it for the current set). Both faults are reproducible in ~30 ms with no gate and no mod load,
extending the `ActorInfo(string, params TraitInfo[])` technique above: `TraitsInConstructOrder()` answers
the first and `TraitInfos<IDefaultVisibilityInfo>().Count` answers the second, and the test should build
the trait set **by reflection from the shipped YAML with `Inherits:` resolved** rather than from a
hand-mirrored list — a hand-mirrored list stays green through exactly this failure.

### Which YAML gate catches a misspelled trait field — the fast ones do not

`FieldLoader.UnknownFieldAction` is bound near the top of `CheckYaml`'s `Run` (`UtilityCommands/CheckYaml.cs`), **before** the branch on whether a map argument was supplied, so the handler is armed in both modes. What differs is what each mode then *evaluates*:

- **Pathless** (`./utility.sh --check-yaml`, no map argument) calls `CheckRules(modData, modData.DefaultRules)`. Evaluating `DefaultRules` forces the ruleset load, constructing an `ActorInfo` for every non-abstract actor (`Ruleset.LoadDefaults`) and therefore every `TraitInfo` — **including the `Player` actor's bot modules**. A misspelled field on an `ai.yaml` module block *is* reported here. **`make test` runs exactly this form** (the `Makefile`'s `test` target), so the repo's own gate does cover it.
- **Map-scoped** (`--check-yaml <map>`) never makes that call; it reaches `CheckRules(modData, map.Rules)` only when the map defines custom rules (the `map.RuleDefinitions != null || …` guard). It lints the map, not the default ruleset.
- **`--dump-balance-json`** never binds `UnknownFieldAction` at all (grep `DumpBalanceJsonCommand.cs`: zero hits).

**Practical rule:** the per-map shortcut and `--dump-balance-json` are not substitutes for `make test` when you have added or renamed a trait field, or removed an inherited trait. They remain worth running — `--dump-balance-json` forces the full `ResolveInherits` pass in seconds and catches load-order and resolution failures neither of the others reaches, which makes it the cheap smoke test for an **inheritance** edit (a `-Trait:` removal that finds nothing throws *"There are no elements with key … to remove"* from `MiniYaml.ResolveInherits`, so an exit-0 dump is positive evidence a removal bit). But a delta that touches AI YAML has not been linted until the pathless form has seen it; if you must skip it, cross-check the block's keys against the `Info` class's `public readonly` fields by hand.

**No YAML gate validates chrome collections at ALL.** Nothing under `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Lint/` references `ChromeProvider` or `ImageCollection` — the grep returns zero files — so a misspelled `ImageCollection:`, a region naming a sheet that does not exist, or a rectangle running off the canvas is caught by no lint, no `make test` and no `--dump-balance-json`. It surfaces as a throw from `ChromeProvider.GetImage` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Graphics/ChromeProvider.cs`) **the first time the widget draws**, which for a rarely-opened panel can be long after the merge. Chrome edits are verified by rendering them, not by linting them — see [`DOCS/recipes/SCREENSHOT.md`](../recipes/SCREENSHOT.md).

### `Tooltip` + `Buildable` + `Interactable` are a locked chain on any bodiless actor

A support-power buy proxy has no
`IOccupySpace` — that is the point of it, and `Production.Produce` short-circuits the exit requirement for
exactly that case (`Production.Produce`, `Traits/Production.cs`). Three constraints then close on each other:

1. `TooltipInfoBase : ConditionalTraitInfo, Requires<IMouseBoundsInfo>` (`Traits/Tooltip.cs`). A
   positionless actor has no `Selectable`, no `IsometricSelectable` and no `Interactable`, so `Tooltip`
   alone makes it **unconstructible**.
2. Dropping `Tooltip` trades one red gate for another: `CheckTooltips` emits *"The following buildable
   actor has no (enabled) Tooltip"* for any actor carrying `BuildableInfo` (`Lint/CheckTooltips.cs`),
   and a buy-menu item must be `Buildable`.
3. Which leaves a bare `Interactable:` as the only trait a bodiless actor can supply to close the chain:
   `Buildable =(CheckTooltips)=> Tooltip =(Requires)=> IMouseBoundsInfo <= Interactable`.

**The bare `Interactable:` is inert**, which is worth stating because it looks like decoration on an actor
that can never be moused over: `Bounds` is null, `MouseoverBounds` falls through to `AutoBounds`, which
reduces over an empty `IAutoMouseBounds` set to `default(Rectangle)`, and `ScreenMap.TickRender` drops an
empty bounds rather than indexing it. `CheckInteractable` only rejects bounds that are *present and*
non-positive (`Lint/CheckInteractable.cs`, `HasInvalidBounds`), so it is clean.

**The general shape:** a trait's `Requires<>` attribute is only half of what the lint gates enforce. The
other half is the `ILintRulesPass` set, which can require the *presence* of a trait that itself requires
something else. Checking one and not the other is how a fix passes locally and fails the gate a second time.

**A constructibility failure is reproducible without the gate, in ~20 ms.** `ActorInfo` has a
`(string name, params TraitInfo[])` constructor and `TraitsInConstructOrder()` is the exact method whose
exception `CheckTraitPrerequisites` reports, so an NUnit test can build a candidate actor's trait set
by hand and get the gate's verdict with no mod load, no `World` and no launch slot
(`OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Mods.Common/BuyLoopProxyTest.cs`).

### The lint baseline matches on `<scope> | <message>`, so a NEW map can add unrecorded errors while a whole-run grep still looks clean

`mods/ww3mod/lint-baseline.txt` is the floor `make test` fails against, and **the unit it compares is a signature, not a line and not a count.** `LintBaseline.Signature` is `$"{scope} | {firstLine}"` (`LintBaseline.Signature`, in `UtilityCommands/LintBaseline.cs`), where `firstLine` is the message truncated at the first newline — several lint errors carry a multi-line explanation under a one-line summary, and only the summary is keyed on.

Consequences worth knowing before you touch the file:

- **The gate is two-directional.** `Judge` fails on any signature not in the baseline, **and also fails when a recorded signature stops occurring** unless `LINT_BASELINE_PRUNE=true` — that is the "you fixed something, so the floor must drop with it" rule from `CLAUDE.md` made mechanical. Pruning (`Prune`) can only remove entries, never add.
- **`scope` is usually a map, so adding a map can add unrecorded errors.** Because the same rules lint re-runs once per map without deduplicating, a new map contributes its own signatures under its own scope. A grep over the whole run for a *message* can look unchanged while the baseline diff is not — **diff the signature LIST, never the error count.**
- **`ClassOf` blanks backtick-quoted specifics** when grouping for the amnesty output, so the grouped text printed to you is deliberately *not* the signature that was matched. Do not copy a grouped line into the baseline and expect it to match.
- `Judge` prints `"{errorCount} errors emitted, {seen.Count} distinct signatures."` followed by `NEW:` and `FIXED:` lines — read those two lists, not the leading number.

**Never read the baseline as evidence that ART is missing.** Most of its lines are `sprite file \`X.shp\` not found` (`grep -c 'sprite file' mods/ww3mod/lint-baseline.txt`), including `mig.shp`, `badr.shp` and `u2.shp` — stock Red Alert sprites that ship inside `conquer.mix`, which `mod.yaml`'s `Packages:` mounts as the optional `~conquer.mix`. **Those lines are what a baseline recorded on a machine without the downloaded RA content produces, because the check cannot see inside mixes that are not there** — an artefact of the recording environment rather than a finding (inferred from the content of the lines, not confirmed on a machine with content). Ruling "the sprites do not exist" off this file is a live trap. The authoritative question is answered by `Mod=ww3mod ./utility.sh --check-missing-sprites` **on a machine with content installed** — read-only, takes seconds.

**Never hand-add a line to the baseline to turn a red run green without saying why in the commit message.**

### Scenarios and the map-shaped gates

**The YAML lint covers every autotest scenario as well as the shipped maps.** `make test` and `.\make.ps1 test` both run the pathless `--check-yaml`, which prints one `Testing map:` line per linted map, and the lint passes iterate `rules.Actors` — the scenario's whole merged ruleset, not only the actors it places. **A scenario's rules override is linted as rules, whether or not anything uses them**: the gate has caught *Actor `powerproxy.strike` is not constructible* in a new scenario's `rules.yaml` for an actor that scenario never spawns. The linted-map total equals the shipped-map directories plus the scenario directories; recount both rather than trusting any figure written down — `ls -d mods/ww3mod/maps/*/ tools/autotest/scenarios/*/ | wc -l`. On Windows, count the lint's own headers with:

```powershell
$o = .\make.ps1 test 2>&1 | Out-String
($o -split "`n" | Where-Object { $_ -match '^Testing map:' }).Count
```

**Work on the assumption that the gate reaches your scenario.** Of the two ways to be wrong this is much the safer: assuming coverage you do not have costs a surprise later, while assuming a lint error in a scenario cannot reach the merge gate puts a red gate in front of a finished branch.

**Why the pathless lint reaches them, when the classification looks like it should not.** `mods/ww3mod/maps` is declared `System` in `mod.yaml`'s `MapFolders` (`ww3mod|maps: System`) and `tools/autotest/scenarios` is declared `Unknown` (`~^EngineDir|../tools/autotest/scenarios: Unknown`). `CheckYaml` takes its list from `MapCache.EnumerateMapDirPackagesAndNames()`, whose `classification` parameter defaults to `System`, and the folder filter is `classification.HasFlag(packageClassification)` (`MapCache.EnumerateMapDirPackages`) — but `MapClassification.Unknown = 0` (`MapPreview.cs`), and `HasFlag(0)` is true for every value, so `Unknown` folders pass every classification filter. The lint logic is otherwise ordinary: `TestMap` reports `map.InvalidCustomRules`, which `Map.PostInit` sets by catching whatever `Ruleset.Load` throws. Consequences under [§Engine code rules](#engine-code-rules-enforced-by-toolsgit-hookspre-commit) ("The mirror case").

**Other gates are scenario-blind, and that is why this section exists.** `make nav-guard`'s baseline is `mods/ww3mod/maps` only — see [`tools/nav-guard/README.md`](../../tools/nav-guard/README.md). **Before trusting any gate to cover a scenario change, find the line where it builds its file list and check which classification or directory it names.** A green run is otherwise byte-identical whether your scenario is perfect or unparseable.

**`Unknown` is deliberate, so do not "fix" it upstream.** The comment above the scenario `MapFolders` entry in `mod.yaml` explains it: the classification is what keeps scenarios out of every UI tab (lobby, missions, main-menu chooser) while the engine still resolves them by folder name, because `Game.LoadMap` matches on Uid or package folder name and reads neither `Class` nor `Visibility`. Reclassifying them would put every test scenario in the player's map lists.

**The targeted form lints one scenario, and the path is relative to `engine/`.** Who may run it is set in `CLAUDE.md` §"Who runs what" (a worker dispatched by a manager does not run the YAML lint; it names the files it touched and what lint should say if it got them wrong):

```sh
./utility.sh --check-yaml ../tools/autotest/scenarios/<name>      # from the repo root
```

`CheckYaml` accepts `args[1]` as a package path under `new Folder(Platform.EngineDir)`, and the effective root is `<repo>/engine` because `utility.sh` `cd`s there before its `dotnet bin/OpenRA.Utility.dll` line — so the repo-root-relative path everyone writes first resolves to `<repo>/engine/tools/…` and fails.

**Run it from the repository ROOT. Do NOT `cd engine` first**, however natural that looks once you know the path is engine-relative: there is a *second* `engine/utility.sh`, tracked mode `100644` and therefore never executable, so invoking it there dies instantly with `permission denied` and **exit 126**. Redirect that to a file and you get a **zero-byte log with the only diagnostic on stderr** — which reads exactly like a tool that has started and not yet produced output. **A zero-byte output plus a fast non-zero exit is a launch failure, not a slow tool; read the exit code before concluding anything about queueing.** **A second launch failure of the same family, and the one that bites first on Windows: `utility.sh` requires `make` on `PATH` before it touches dotnet** (its opening `command -v make … || exit 1`). Git Bash on a machine with no `make` therefore fails with exit 1 and a one-line stderr before any lint runs; `.\make.ps1 test` is unaffected. The underlying command still works by hand from the repo root: `cd engine && MOD_SEARCH_PATHS="<repo>/mods,<engine>/mods" ENGINE_DIR=".." dotnet bin/OpenRA.Utility.dll ww3mod --check-yaml ../tools/autotest/scenarios/<name>` — i.e. `utility.sh`'s `dotnet` line without the wrapper (use Windows-style paths from `pwd -W`). Two further differences from the pathless run: it skips the baseline judgement entirely (`if (args.Length >= 2)` returns before `LintBaseline.Judge`), so it fails on *any* error rather than against the recorded floor; and it lints the map's own rules rather than `DefaultRules`, so it does **not** catch a misspelled trait field on a mod-level block — see [§Which YAML gate catches a misspelled trait field](#which-yaml-gate-catches-a-misspelled-trait-field--the-fast-ones-do-not).

**What is loud and what is silent.** A path that does not exist is **loud in every shape**: `Folder.OpenPackage` reaches `ZipFileLoader.TryParseReadWritePackage`, whose `File.OpenRead` is unguarded (`ZipFile.cs`), so a missing parent directory throws `DirectoryNotFoundException` and a missing name inside an existing directory throws `FileNotFoundException`. `CheckYaml` catches both, prints `Failed with exception:` and exits 1. So a typo in the scenario name cannot masquerade as a pass. The silent `if (package == null) continue;` is reachable only when the path **exists as a file that is not a parseable package** — i.e. pointing at `…/<name>/map.yaml` instead of at the scenario directory. **Confirm you saw `Testing map: <title>`** — belt-and-braces against a typo, load-bearing against a path aimed one level too deep.

**`Testing map:` sits at the TOP of the output, so checking for it with a `tail` produces a FALSE NEGATIVE.** The targeted lint emits its header first, then hundreds of lines of `grants conditions that are not consumed` warnings. `| Select-Object -Last 25` or `| tail -25` therefore shows the warning block and **no `Testing map:` line** — which reads exactly like the "silently not linted" failure the check exists to catch, and has sent a reader to conclude a correct scenario was malformed. Filter the **whole** output instead:

```powershell
$o = .\utility.cmd --check-yaml "..\tools\autotest\scenarios\<name>" 2>&1 | Out-String
($o -split "`n") | Where-Object { $_ -notmatch 'grants conditions that are not consumed' -and $_.Trim() -ne '' }
```

For a clean scenario that prints exactly one line: the header. **The general form is the part worth carrying — when a rule says "require string X to prove the tool ran", grep the WHOLE output for X.** Sampling either end can only produce false negatives, and a false negative on a proof-of-execution check is indistinguishable from the failure being checked for.

**And do not pipe the run.** The utility's exit code is the evidence; a `| grep` appended to it reports the pipeline's status instead.

**The general form is broader than piping: the verdict-producing command must be LAST in its chain.** `tail` is only the famous instance. Anything appended after a verdict — `cat`, `grep`, `echo`, `tail` — substitutes its own exit code, and it can flip the result **in either direction**: a passing `run-test.sh` followed by a `cat` of a mistyped path reports as a failure, and the reverse is equally available. Either put the verdict command last, or run `set -o pipefail` first, or read `result.json` and ignore `$?` entirely.

**`make lua-gate` proves a binding's NAME resolves; whether its RETURN VALUE can cross back into Lua is a separate check.** `ScriptTypes.ToLuaValue` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Scripting/ScriptTypes.cs`) converts `LuaValue`, `null`, `double`, `int`, `bool`, `string`, `IScriptBindable` and `Array`, and **throws on everything else**. C# `is int` does not match a boxed `uint`, so a binding declared `public uint Foo()` reaches the final `throw` and takes the whole script down. lua-gate checks this as a `warn`-severity denylist of unconvertible primitives (the comment above that list in `tools/lua-gate/lua_gate.py` explains it) — a name-resolution check alone would have passed such a scenario.

**Read the gates as a set: `make nav-guard` is green-by-construction on scenarios, and `make lua-gate` resolves names, not behaviour.** Assume a further gate has the same kind of hole until you have read its file list.

**A fatal Lua error is an outcome no in-script arm can reach.** A scenario written with GREEN / defect-FAIL / VOID arms still has a fourth outcome underneath all three: the script dies before any arm evaluates, and the run reports a bare `AUTOTEST_VERDICT outcome=FAIL` with no verdict line. **No arm can cover this, because arms are code in the process that just died** — the countermeasure has to live one level up, statically in lua-gate or in the harness. **When triaging a FAIL, check the log for a fatal before believing the verdict is about the code under test.** Worst case observed: the one caller of the broken binding reached it *only* on its own failure path, so the landmine was armed to detonate precisely when the scenario most needed its diagnostic. **A latent defect reachable only from a failure branch is invisible to every green run, forever.**

**The NUnit option, and why nobody has taken it.** An `engine/OpenRA.Test/` fixture that loads every scenario's rules would close the gap in CI, and it is possible but not cheap: **nothing in the test project constructs a `ModData`**, so the mod-filesystem bootstrap would have to be built from scratch. The one `Ruleset` a test does build is hand-assembled in memory from synthetic `ActorInfo`s with no YAML behind it (`OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Game/ShadowCacheKeyTermsTest.cs`), so it is not a starting point. The cost is the bootstrap, not the ruleset.

**A scenario's `rules.yaml` is not a private namespace to lint, and it is not a private namespace to the shipped actor either — re-role or re-tune a scenario-local CLONE, never the shipped type.** `CheckYaml` runs every `ILintRulesPass` over every map's resolved ruleset, scoping errors to the map's package (`CheckYaml.TestMap`), so a scenario writing `abrams: AIUnitRole: Role: Logistics` failed `CheckUnitRoleTable`'s per-actor `Expected` pin for that map — only the set-equality half of that lint is gated to `DefaultRules` (its `ReferenceEquals(rules, modData.DefaultRules)` branch). Overriding the TYPE also re-roles every instance the bot BUYS during the run. The clone form fixes both:

```yaml
casualtyabrams:
	Inherits: abrams
	RenderSprites:
		Image: abrams      # sprites resolve by ACTOR NAME, not through Inherits -- without it, world load dies
	AIUnitRole:
		Role: Logistics
	-Buildable:           # or the clone sits in the live production queue all run
```

and a mis-cased `Inherits:` value is loud (`Parent type ... not found`, thrown in `MiniYaml.ResolveInherits`), so the clone also converts the silent case-merge failure into a loud one. **The converse census: a change to a shipped actor reaches scenarios by TWO routes** — `map.yaml` placements of the type, and `rules.yaml` clones declaring `Inherits: <ACTOR>`. When PBOX's vision was gated (`70e63582`), the placement grep found 18 scenarios and missed three clones (`fogmarker`, `shademarker`, `probebox`). Grep both. For a vision change, ask first whose side the scenario stages on: `Vision.ValidRelationships` defaults to `Ally` (`Traits/Vision.cs`) and nothing in the mod overrides it, so only scenarios that rely on the actor's OWN side seeing can be invalidated — exactly one of 23 was.

### A duplicate child key is LATENT until another file overrides that actor — the override is the detonator

*(Extends the section above, which says what the merge *does*; this says when it is *reported*.)*

**Inside a single source, repeated child keys are folded together and nothing is reported.** `ResolveInherits` hands each child to `MergeIntoResolved` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/MiniYaml.cs`), which keys the list with a `HashSet` and, on the second sighting, replaces the first node **in place** with `MergePartial(existing, override)` of the two trait *values* — so the conflict check inside that call sees each trait's own fields, never the actor's trait list.

**The conflict check that throws lives in the `MiniYaml`-pair `MergePartial` overload** — and nothing hands that overload an actor's trait list until a **second source defines the same top-level key** (the local `MergeNode` in the list overload of `MergePartial`, reached from `MiniYaml.Merge`). So the duplicate is the charge and an ordinary override is the detonator. The failure names neither the file that was edited nor the field that is wrong.

Three details, each of which changes what you would conclude:

- **`IntoDictionaryWithConflictLog` clears its output dictionary first** (`output.Clear()`, `engine/OpenRA.Game/Exts.cs`). The two calls at the top of the `MiniYaml`-pair `MergePartial` are therefore **two independent checks, one per side** — not one check over the union. An override redefining a key the base also defines is fine; only a list that repeats a key *within itself* throws. Read without that `Clear()`, the code looks like it would reject every override ever written.
- **`Inherits` is exempt from the fold but NOT from the check.** `ResolveInherits` branches on `Inherits`/`Inherits@` before reaching `MergeIntoResolved`, so two plain `Inherits:` lines both apply, in order — and are still two nodes with the same key, so they still throw under an override. This is the one shape where collapsing the duplicate would change behaviour; labelling it (`Inherits@X:`) is the only correct fix.
- **The diagnostic is stricter than the merge it guards.** The conflict check throws on a shape the rest of `MergePartial` would then have merged deterministically (`overrideNodes.Value ?? existingNodes.Value` — **second wins, per key, with the union of children kept**). The exception is a correctness tripwire, not a failure of the merge algorithm — which is why the fix belongs in the *shipped actor*, not in the overriding file, and why turning the override off only re-hides the defect.

**Operational consequence: every new override file is a detonator for whichever latent duplicates it happens to touch.** A sweep found ~20 blocks with duplicate direct children across the loaded manifest; one (`A10`) detonated the first time any file overrode it. The permanent guard is `engine/OpenRA.Test/DuplicateChildKeyTest.cs` (~90 ms, in the merge gate), which resolves the manifest lists plus every shipped map's declared rules and fails on any offender, naming the actor and every `file:line`.

### The set of actor names you may override is mod.yaml's `Rules:` LIST, not the rules DIRECTORY

**A top-level key in a loaded file that matches nothing already present is not an error and not a no-op.** MiniYaml has no notion of "override an existing actor": `Merge` simply adds the node, and **the mod gains a NEW actor** whose entire definition is whatever the override file wrote. With no `Tooltip`, no `Interactable`/`RenderSprites`, such an actor fails `Actor type 'x' does not define a default visibility type` and `The following buildable actor has no (enabled) Tooltip` — **on every map, forever.** One override file naming five dead actors produced 3,650 unrecorded lint errors (ten messages × 365 maps) against a baseline of 22; the per-map multiplication is the tell that the defect is in one actor definition rather than in one map.

`mods/ww3mod/rules/` and the manifest's `Rules:` list differ by every file in `weapons/` and `sound/`, the `campaign/` tree, `campaign-palettes.yaml` and `ingame/old.yaml` (recount by diffing `mod.yaml`'s `Rules:` list against `find mods/ww3mod/rules -name '*.yaml'`). **Any tool that builds an actor universe with `os.walk` over the rules directory is counting actors that are not in the game**, and is simultaneously *missing* loaded ones: the manifest merges seven lists through `MiniYaml.Merge` — `Rules`, `Weapons`, `Voices`, `Notifications`, `Music`, `ModelSequences` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/GameRules/Ruleset.cs`) and `Sequences` (`SequenceSet.cs`) — so `weapons/` and `sequences/` are loaded and are not under `Rules:`. **Scanning by directory is wrong in both directions at once.**

Two corollaries, both paid for:

- **A generator and its checker that share a wrong universe agree with each other**, and no amount of running them proves anything. The checker must derive its universe from a *different* authority than the thing being tested, or both must be corrected together and the check must name what it rejects.
- **An unloaded `rules/` file is still visible to every directory-walking NUnit fixture.** `VaporizeScopeTest` went red on a tree whose C# was byte-identical to the last green build, because a new unloaded file declared `SUPPLYROUTE` a second time and the fixture's first-match `Find` returned the wrong node. The walk **over-approximates** the loaded rules, so a fixture asking a per-actor question must union the declarations — and **a branch that changes anything under `mods/` still needs `dotnet test`**, because several fixtures assert on files rather than on code.

### A MiniYaml template of PURE `-Key:` removals cannot exist, and the obvious workaround throws too

Both failure modes are loud — an exception at rules load naming the exact key — so this costs minutes, not a session. It is recorded because the broken shape is the one anyone would try first.

1. **Removal-only template.** `ResolveInherits` consumes a `-Key:` against the accumulator of the node it is *written in*, before that node's result is folded into the child (`MiniYaml.cs`). A template whose only children are removals runs them against an empty list and dies on the first: `There are no elements with key 'X' to remove`.
2. **Giving the mixin `Inherits: ^Parent` so the removals have something to bite** moves the throw to the *child*, which now reaches `^Parent` twice — once through its own chain, once through the mixin — and the duplicate-parent guard fires with `Parent type '^X' was already inherited by this yaml tree`, also from `ResolveInherits`. **That guard is per TREE, not per node**, so it catches diamond inheritance however deep the two paths are.

**The shape that works is a SUBCLASS, not a mixin**: `^UnarmedHelicopter: Inherits: ^Helicopter` with the removals below it, and actors naming *that* as their single parent. **MiniYaml inheritance is single-path subtyping, not composable mixins** — any "remove some of what my parent gave me" template must *be* a subtype of that parent; you cannot factor removals out sideways and apply them to several unrelated parents. `-Key:` is positional (see §"There is no 'own beats inherited' rule") **and** scoped to the node it appears in, which is the half that is easy to miss because only the positional half is documented.

### A trait declared under the wrong system actor is not IGNORED, it is ADDED — and a lobby option then makes the SERVER reject the client

**MiniYaml does not validate `TraitLocation` at merge time.** A scenario `rules.yaml` writing `World: MapLayers: ExploredMapCheckboxEnabled: true` does not override the Player-actor `MapLayers` (declared on `^BasePlayer` in `mods/ww3mod/rules/player.yaml`) and is not ignored: it creates a **second** `MapLayers`, on the World actor. `MapLayers` is `[TraitLocation(SystemActors.Player | SystemActors.EditorPlayer)]` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Traits/Player/MapLayers.cs`) and its Info implements `ILobbyOptions`, so the session then offered the option id `explored` **twice**.

**Where it fails is three layers from the edit, and on the other side of the wire.** `LobbySettingsNotification.ClientJoined` builds a dictionary keyed on the option id, threw `ArgumentException: An item with the same key has already been added. Key: explored`, and the server dropped the joining client (the catch-all in `Server.ValidateClient`). The client logged `Attempted to read past the end of the stream`, stayed on the main menu and never built a world — so: **no `lua.log`, a twelve-line `debug.log`, no exception file, and a full watchdog timeout.** Every one of those is the signature of "the rules failed to load", which is what the runner's own timeout branch greps for, and none of it is what happened: the rules loaded fine.

> **When a run produces `TIMEOUT-FAIL` with no `lua.log` and a tiny `debug.log`, read `server.log`.** The failure was on the server, and the client-side logs the harness reads cannot contain it. `run-test.sh` now polls `server.log` for `Dropping connection` and reports `outcome=LAUNCH-FAIL exit=3` — the same "nothing ran, so this is not a test result" family as the exit-127 and zero-byte-log traps in `CLAUDE.md`.

**The authoring-time check is seconds:** every trait a scenario declares must sit under the system actor its `TraitLocation` names, and grepping the trait's `.cs` for `[TraitLocation(` is the whole of it. `--check-yaml` also catches it — this scenario was authored under an instruction not to run that gate.

### A lobby option has TWO spellings, and converting a trait field into one SILENTLY DISABLES every `rules.yaml` that set it

**1. Grepping for the option ID finds the minority of the places that set it.** A map or scenario can pin an option two ways: by the wire id in its Lua, or by overriding the **trait Info field the option is BUILT from** in its `rules.yaml`, which contains the id nowhere. **Before scoping any lobby-option change, grep for BOTH the id and the `*Info` field names that feed `new LobbyOption(...)` / `new LobbyBooleanOption(...)`** — and grep for the field being **assigned** (`^\s*Field: `), not merely named, because `grep -l` on the field over-counts: most hits are comments *discussing* it. The two errors run in opposite directions — the id grep under-counts and the field grep over-counts — and both are in the direction of carelessness.

**2. Converting an Info field into a lobby option keeps compiling, keeps loading, and stops being read.** `LobbyCommands.LoadMapSettings` registers **every** option a trait yields with that option's own `DefaultValue`, for every match, whatever a map or scenario `rules.yaml` says (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/ServerTraits/LobbyCommands.cs`) — a `rules.yaml` can set trait *fields* and has no way to set a lobby *option*. So the instant a constructor switches from `info.SomeTicks` to `settings.OptionOrDefault(id, …)`, the registered default wins and the scenario's carefully-pinned field is never consulted again. **Nothing warns: the field still exists, the YAML still parses, the lint gate still passes, and the scenario still runs — at the shipped default instead of at the value it asked for.** That is exactly the shape that survives a test run: a demo pinning a clock to 300 ticks because a capture has ~200 s would compile, lint, launch, draw and capture its frames during the full five-minute shipped clock — a demo of its own subject not happening, with a green exit code.
