**The escape hatch has to be a second field, and the idiom is already in-tree.** `TimeLimitManagerInfo.TimeLimitTicks` (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/World/TimeLimitManager.cs`, read in the `TimeLimitManager` constructor *before* any minute conversion) is exactly this: an absolute override in ticks, non-zero meaning "overrides the lobby dropdown entirely", documented as intended for scenario `rules.yaml`. Copy it rather than inventing one. **And choose the SENTINEL PER FIELD, not per file:** `0` is the natural "not in play" and is right for a duration that cannot legally be zero, but it is **wrong** for any field where zero is a real setting — and unifying two sentinels for tidiness is how a documented setting gets deleted in silence. **A cheap check before you convert:** `grep -rn '<FieldName>' tools/autotest/scenarios/ mods/*/maps/`. Every hit is a caller about to be silently ignored.

**3. Deleting a lobby option does not freeze it at its default — it INVERTS it whenever the registered default is `true`.** `GrantConditionOnLobbyOption` computes `OptionOrDefault(Option, !GrantWhenOptionDisabled)` and then `shouldGrant = GrantWhenOptionDisabled ? !optionEnabled : optionEnabled` (`Traits/Conditions/GrantConditionOnLobbyOption.cs`, `INotifyCreated.Created`), so **the REGISTERED default and the UNREGISTERED fallback are independent values.** Every WW3MOD gate uses `GrantWhenOptionDisabled: true`, which makes the unregistered fallback grant the condition — matching the shipped behaviour only for options whose registered default is OFF. Delete an option that defaults ON and the gate flips. **The safe lever for removing a lobby ROW is always visibility; deletion is only safe for an option whose registered default is already `false`.**

## Launch args and the autotest harness: traps that produce a confident wrong answer

Each of these returns a clean-looking result that is about something other than what was asked.

### `--hidden` autotest runs write NO screenshots, while `result.json` still lists them

`--hidden` (`tools/autotest/run-test.sh`, "the unattended/tournament profile") exports `OPENRA_WINDOW_HIDDEN=1`, and `Sdl2PlatformWindow` then creates the window with `SDL_WINDOW_HIDDEN` and sets `IsSuspended = true` explicitly. The main loop in `Game.cs` calls `RenderTick()` only when `!Renderer.WindowIsSuspended`, and a pending capture is taken only at the end of `RenderTick()` (`if (takeScreenshot) … TakeScreenshotInner()`). So under `--hidden` **no frame is ever rendered and no PNG is ever written**. Meanwhile `Test.Screenshot` → `TestModeScreenshots.Capture` records the planned path and rewrites `manifest.json` *before* any file exists, and `TestGlobal.ExitWhenCapturesFlushed` waits at most 5 s for the files, then writes the verdict anyway — so `result.json` and `manifest.json` list PNG paths with `captured_at` timestamps while the run directory holds none. An observed run reported `PASS (3 screenshot(s))` with only `debug.log`, `lua.log`, `manifest.json` and `result.json` on disk; the identical run with `--background` wrote all three files.

**Consequence:** `--hidden` is right for an assertion scenario and **wrong for any scenario whose answer is a frame.** Use `--background` (the default) for captures. **Treat a `result.json` that lists screenshots as a claim, not as evidence, until `ls` shows the files.** `--minimized` is wired through the same suspended-render path, so expect the same of it wherever the platform actually reports the window as minimized; [`DOCS/recipes/SCREENSHOT.md`](../recipes/SCREENSHOT.md) covers what each window mode yields for captures.

### A single-client autotest launch seats ONE slot, so a second `Playable: True` side has no `Player` at all

`CreateMapPlayers` builds a `Player` for every **non-playable** map player, then one per lobby slot — with `if (client == null) continue` (`CreateMapPlayers.CreatePlayers`). `run-test.sh` launches a single local client (`Launch.Map=${LAUNCH_MAP}`), so it seats exactly one slot. A scenario authoring two `Playable: True` sides gets one `Player` and one empty slot, and `Player.GetPlayer("<the other one>")` returns **nil** — not a broken player, not a defaulted one, nothing. No bot fills it either: an autotest server is `ServerType.Local`, so `SkirmishLogic.ClientJoined` early-returns and **nothing is ever auto-seated.**

Three consequences, in increasing order of expense:

- **The shape every passing two-sided scenario already uses** is exactly ONE `Playable: True` seat, for the client, with the other side a bare map player or a `Bot:` map player. `Enemies:` on a map player still makes the relationship, so nothing about the match is weaker for it.
- **A scenario that passes is not evidence its `Players` block is sound** — only that nothing read the part that is broken. `test-crate-rearm` and friends carry two `Playable: True` seats and are not counterexamples: nothing in them resolves the second player by name.
- **The trap behind the trap.** The obvious fix — drop `Playable: True` from the second side — silently changes what any consumer filtering on `Player.Playable` can see, and can turn an assertion **vacuously green** rather than red. `Playable` is a lobby-slot fact (see [`architecture.md` §"`CountsAsASide`, NOT `Playable`"](architecture.md)); the honest filter for "who is in this match" is `CombatantSides.CountsAsASide`, or at minimum `!p.NonCombatant`, which also drops the synthetic `Everyone` player (created in `CreateMapPlayers.CreatePlayers` with `NonCombatant = true`). **When a scenario fix changes a player flag, grep the code under test for that flag before committing.**

Also: a `Test.Fail` on the first line of `WorldLoaded` produces an **empty `lua.log`**, which is the documented tell for "the game never launched". One `print` above the guard costs nothing and separates the two.

### A launch-arg setting is PERSISTED, not session-only — `Settings.Save()` runs on every server join

`Settings.Save()` writes back every field differing from its *compiled* default and **deletes** nodes that match it (`Settings.Save`, `engine/OpenRA.Game/Settings.cs`), and it runs on ordinary paths — including on every server join (the `HandshakeRequest` handler in `engine/OpenRA.Game/Network/UnitOrders.cs`). Two consequences that bite in opposite directions:

- A command-line override (`Section.Field=value`, applied by the "Override with commandline args" loop in the `Settings` constructor) is **written to `settings.yaml`** and sticks until explicitly turned off. `Debug.SyncCheckUnsyncedCode=true` passed once to a launcher is still true next week.
- A hand-edited `settings.yaml` value that happens to equal the compiled default is **silently erased** on the next join.

So hand-editing `settings.yaml` was never a workable substitute for a launch arg, and a launch arg was never a safe way to make a one-off measurement. A harness that changes settings for a run must back them up and restore them (`run-tournament.sh` does, and so does `run-test.sh`'s `restore_settings`).

### A test-mode lobby flag is a SHARED dependency — grep the scenario tree for who forces it on

Several autotest scenarios force `PowersSandboxCheckboxEnabled: true`
+ `Locked: true` in their own `rules.yaml` (it is the only route to `powers.event`, which no faction
provides; recount with `git grep -l -E 'PowersSandboxCheckboxEnabled:[[:space:]]*true' -- tools/autotest/scenarios`).
**So any new behaviour hung on that flag is automatically live in every one of them** — and extending it to
zero a launch delay would have landed on the one scenario whose assertion is precisely that the shipped delay
IS applied, failing the only guard in the tree against the value it removes, **on correct behaviour**. The
resolution is a per-sub-behaviour opt-out in that scenario rather than a weakened assertion, which keeps the
guard pointed at the shipped value. **Before hanging anything new on an existing lobby option, grep the
scenario tree for who forces it on.**

**Corollary on the authority order, which the C# header did not state:** checkbox defaults in a
`*LobbyOptions` trait are ordinary `TraitInfo` fields, so YAML *can* override them and those scenarios do. At
runtime `LobbyInfo.GlobalSettings.OptionOrDefault(id, csharpDefault)` means **host lobby tick > registered
default (= the C# field as overridden from YAML) > the C# field as `OptionOrDefault`'s fallback**, the last
reached only when the option is not in `GlobalSettings` at all (trait stripped, old saved session).
`Locked: true` removes the host from that ordering, which is why the scenarios set both. "These are C#
checkbox defaults with no YAML override" is true of `mods/ww3mod/rules/world.yaml` and false of the scenario
tree.

### `Launch.Benchmark` forces the SERIAL relight path, so a benchmark does not measure the shipped one

`PerfHistory.Sampling = Settings.Debug.PerfGraph || Settings.Debug.PerfText || benchmark != null` (`Game.RenderTick`, `engine/OpenRA.Game/Game.cs`), and the parallel terrain-relight sweep refuses to run while it is true (the `var parallel = … && !PerfHistory.Sampling` guard in `TerrainLighting.cs`) because `PerfHistory.Increment` is not thread-safe — mechanism in [`architecture.md` §"The terrain relight sweep parallelises by VERTEX row"](architecture.md).

**So the three instruments that make a relight measurable are the same three that switch off the thing being measured.** Any profile with `Launch.Benchmark`, the perf graph, or the perf text overlay is timing the serial sweep. That is deliberate and it is also stable — a benchmark profile keeps measuring what it always measured — but **a figure taken under `Launch.Benchmark` is not a figure about shipped play**, and an A/B whose arms differ in whether sampling is on is comparing two different code paths rather than two configurations.

**The per-tick cost is readable WITHOUT the flag.** The `tick_time` `PerfSample` in `Game.InnerLogicTick` is unconditional, and `Benchmark.Tick` only reads `PerfHistory.Items["tick_time"].LastValue`; `Test.GetTickTimeMs()` (`Scripting/Global/TestGlobal.cs`) reads the same property in test mode, so a populated-match timing can be taken on the shipped parallel path. **The two readings lag differently:** a `Trigger.OnTick` callback runs inside `world.Tick()` before `PerfHistory.Tick()` publishes (both in `Game.InnerLogicTick`), so Lua reads the cost of tick **N−2**; `benchmark?.Tick` runs after both, so a CSV row labelled N holds tick **N−1**. Never line a Lua reading up against a CSV row by tick number.

### A staleness bound derived from a THRESHOLD preserves the threshold, not the behaviour — and they can be orders of magnitude apart

A refresh gate that tolerates a quantity drifting by `Threshold` before it acts looks like a licence to be `Threshold`-stale in general. It is not. That tolerance is real only **on a plateau**, where the delta never reaches the threshold and no refresh is ever issued. In the *moving* regime the staleness the shipped code actually accepts is `interval × rate`, which on the nuclear fireball envelope is `5 × 0.515 = 2.6` intensity units against a `TerrainRefreshThreshold` of `0.04` (`LightEventDefinition.TerrainRefreshThreshold` in `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Lighting/LightEventDefinition.cs`, consumed by the refresh gate in `Traits/World/LightEventManager.cs`) — **65× the threshold.**

The lever this was found on derived its eligibility condition as `rate × tailInterval ≤ Threshold`, i.e. `rate ≤ 0.002` intensity/tick, and **never fired once**: `NukeSarmatRV`'s `Warhead@FireballLight` table (`rules/weapons/weapons-nuclear-arsenal.yaml`) has its slowest segment at `0.13 / 49 = 0.00265`, and every other segment is 3× to 200× above the limit. The derivation was sound as a *sufficient* condition and useless as a *practical* one. Reverted in full.

**Two rules, and the second is the cheap one.** *When deriving a safety bound from a constant, check what the code already does at runtime before assuming the constant describes it.* And: **before shipping a gate whose predicate depends on authored data, evaluate the predicate against that data by hand.** The eligibility condition here was a pure function of a static YAML table — minutes to check, no build, no launch — and it would have shown the lever inert before it consumed a serial A/B slot on a machine where launches are the scarce resource. This is the [§"A change believed made, documented as made, and inert"](#a-change-believed-made-documented-as-made-and-inert) shape, caught by arithmetic rather than by a run.

### `--speed` and a compressed clock are NOT the same lever, and only one of them keeps the simulation honest

`TestModeSpeedMultiplier` implements **`IWorldLoaded`** and divides `world.Timestep` there (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/World/TestModeSpeedMultiplier.cs`). A trait constructor that reads `self.World.Timestep` to convert a lobby's **minutes into ticks** runs during world setup, i.e. **before** `WorldLoaded`. So under `--speed N` the tick counts are unchanged and only the wall-clock length of a tick moves.

**`--speed` buys wall clock without touching a single simulated quantity.** A scenario that wants shipped timing inside an unattended slot should reach for it and never for an override field. **The inverse is the trap: an override field genuinely changes what is simulated, so a scenario that compresses a clock is no longer evidence about the clock it compressed.** That is right for a single-mechanism probe and fatal for a scenario whose subject *is* the clocks.

**And a multiplier is not the divisor you asked for.** `world.Timestep = Math.Max(1, oldTimestep / multiplier)` is **integer** division, so `--speed 8` on a 60 ms timestep gives 7 ms — a real multiplier of 60/7 = **8.57x**. Derive expected wall clock from `ticks * newTimestep`, never from `ticks / (16.67 * N)`. `--speed 4` is exact (15 ms); 8 and 16 are not.

### A CRASH AFTER a verdict is reported as that verdict — the crash hunt is scoped to the no-result branch

`run-test.sh` finds `exception-<utc>.log` files newer than the run marker and reports `OUTCOME=CRASH`, but the entire hunt sits **inside `if [ ! -f "${RESULT_FILE}" ]`** (the top-level `if [ ! -f "${RESULT_FILE}" ]` block in `tools/autotest/run-test.sh` that sets `OUTCOME="CRASH"`). So a scenario that writes `Test.Pass` at tick N and then dies at tick N+100 comes back **PASS**, with the exception log on disk and nobody looking at it. This is invisible to short single-mechanism scenarios, which pass at the end of what they measure; it bites a long scenario that passes in the middle of a match still running. **The countermeasure is structural and costs nothing: never write a verdict earlier than the last thing the scenario cares about**, so there is no window left behind a green.

### `ConquestVictoryConditions` is INERT IN TEST MODE AT EVERY ENTRY POINT, so `WinState` can never become true

`ITick.Tick` returns on `TestMode.IsActive` (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/Player/ConquestVictoryConditions.cs`) — deliberately, and the comment says why: the harness owns the verdict, so victory tracking would only race the test's own assertions. But `objectiveID` is assigned **only inside that same method** (it is declared `= -1`), so it stays `-1`; and `INotifyTimeLimit.NotifyTimerExpired` opens with `if (objectiveID < 0) return;`. **The two facts compose into something neither states: a test-mode match can run its time limit, open an endgame, fly a whole salvo and annihilate the map, and every player's `WinState` will still read `Undefined`.** A scenario asserting on `WinState` is asserting on something that cannot become true — it fails identically whether the ending worked perfectly or never happened at all.

**Two consequences beyond the flag.** First, **anything else living inside `NotifyTimerExpired` is equally unreachable from a scenario** — including this trait's team arithmetic, which no map, no Lua and no length of run can execute. When a bug is reported at N call sites, *establish reachability per site before promising a test per site*: one site can be live-but-unreachable-from-tests, one already dead, and only one actually biting. Second, **prefer a projection of the MECHANISM over a projection of its CONSEQUENCE.** `WinState` is downstream of an objective system test mode deliberately disables; the endgame trait's own phase value is the ending itself. *If the thing you are asserting has no direct projection, that absence is itself the finding.*

> **And write the fail text so it cannot accuse a component the same readings exonerate.** A poller that samples a state machine every few ticks, sees level 3 then level 1, and reports *"the level never left 3"* has named the one component provably correct — the intermediate phase can be **one tick long**, because it ends on the first qualifying event rather than on a clock. Sampling is only sound for states with a guaranteed minimum duration, and "this phase always lasts a while" is an assumption about gameplay, not about code: **if a state machine has a transition you want to assert on, expose the transition TICK, not just the current state.** A fault that misattributes costs more than a fault that fires late, because it sends the next reader to the wrong file.

### "Copy the nearest working scenario" imports departures you did not choose

Scenario `rules.yaml` files carry deliberate deviations, and a comment naming one is not a statement about the default. The worked instance: a tournament scenario sets `PlayerResources.DefaultCash: 7500` *"because the default 5000 is fine for normal games"* — and 5000 is not the default. `mods/ww3mod/rules/player.yaml` leaves `DefaultCash` **commented out** (`# DefaultCash: 20000`), so `PlayerResourcesInfo.DefaultCash = 20000` applies (`Traits/Player/PlayerResources.cs`) and is also what the `startingcash` dropdown defaults to. The tournament's value is a **62% cut presented as a raise**, and a scenario copying it while claiming shipped defaults inherits that silently. **When a scenario's stated goal is "shipped defaults", read the DEFAULT from the C# field or the uncommented YAML, not from the comment in the file you are copying.** Commented-out YAML is the specific shape that misleads: the value is right there in the file, one `#` away from being true.

## A semantic change to a YAML field is a MIGRATION, and a migration is only complete against the tree at the instant it lands

`ShakeScreenWarhead.Intensity`
changed meaning from the numerator of an inverse-square falloff to **peak screen displacement in pixels**
(`Warheads/ShakeScreenWarhead.cs`, the field's `[Desc]`). The commit that did it retuned every call
site - every call site that was on `main` at that moment. A weapons file created the same day on a branch
merged afterwards, and a later commit copied the un-migrated blocks into four more warheads, so twelve
weapons spent two days asking for 35-150 px of camera throw against the retuned references' 13 and 20.

**Nothing could catch it, and each reason is general.** `--check-yaml` validates that a field EXISTS on a
trait; the field name did not change and still took an int. The unit suite had a dedicated fixture over the
same weapons covering blast physics, flash envelopes and smudge radii - and no assertion on shake, because
shake is not physics anyone had written a law for. **And the clamp that stops a bad number from being
unplayable is the same clamp that stops it from being obvious:** `ScreenShaker` soft-clamps the SUM of live
effects through a `tanh`, so a 7x error and a 1.6x error render as the same picture.

**Two defences, neither of which existed:** rename the field when its meaning changes, so the old spelling
fails to load; or have the retuning commit add a check that enumerates call sites **from the files** rather
than from a list typed at the time. Any branch open across the instant a semantic change lands
reintroduces the old semantics on merge, silently, because the spelling is unchanged.

**Splitting a shared template scalar into per-actor values is the same migration, and the consumers that break never name the key.** When every civilian building inherited `Cargo: MaxWeight: 10` from `^CivBuilding`, `demo-garrison-lineup` encoded that value as the **length of six Lua lists**, where no grep for `MaxWeight` can find it; the per-building table then gave the oil pump `V19` two and the demo died at `LoadPassenger`, while `RUSHOUSE` rising 10 → 12 under-filled silently. The question when splitting is not only "is each new value right" but **"who had memorised the old one"**, and the answer is never in the file being edited. The repair there deleted the literal rather than correcting it: the generator resolves `Cargo.MaxWeight` through `Inherits` (`max_weight` in `tools/autotest/scenarios/demo-garrison-lineup/generate.py`) and the demo re-reads the live figure through `Test.CargoCapacity` (`Scripting/Global/TestGlobal.cs`). The inbound twin — 21 of 38 civilian buildings were "concrete, 60000 HP" because nobody had typed anything — is in [`architecture.md` §Garrisoning](architecture.md#garrisoning-is-an-ownership-transfer-and-that-one-fact-rewrites-the-rest-of-the-system) (the undeclared `Health`/`Armor` paragraph): **a value shared by twenty actors is a fact about the RESOLVED rules that no single actor's YAML contains**, so resolve and look at the distribution.

## A change believed made, documented as made, and inert

**The most expensive defect shape in this codebase is not a missing mechanism. It is a mechanism that is present, documented, wired up, lint-clean — and evaluates to nothing.** The first six instances below were all this shape, and none of them was found by reading the code, because reading is exactly what these survive: the comment beside them is accurate about intent, the citation is real, the field is genuinely set, the trait is genuinely mounted. What is false is the *value the expression produces at the inputs the mod actually supplies*.

**Operational rule: when something has absorbed several fixes that each looked correct and changed nothing, stop hunting for a missing mechanism and start computing what the present one evaluates to.** Substitute the mod's real configured numbers into the expression and reduce it. Four of the first six below were found that way and could not have been found any other way. A companion habit, cheap and high-yield: when a field is set in YAML, grep for every *reader* of the field, not just its declaration — three of those six are fields with zero effective readers.

**Both habits have a blind spot, and instances 7–10 all sit in it.** Reducing an expression needs an expression to reduce, and grepping for readers needs a field that was written down. Instances 7 and 8 are fields **nobody set**, where the default did the damage; instances 9 and 10 are correctly-named call sites whose *callee* does something else. None of the four is visible in the diff, the YAML, or a reader census. What finds them is reading the body of the thing you are about to trust, one level down — which is the same corrective as the rest of this section, applied to a callee rather than to a value.

This is the code-side twin of [`README.md` §"Four shapes of confident correctness claim with nothing behind it"](README.md), which covers the same failure in prose claims. Both reduce to the same corrective: **name the line you would delete to break the property, and check that deleting it would change anything.**

### Six shapes, fourteen instances

The instances keep their numbers — they are cited by number elsewhere in this file and in `WORKSPACE/DISCOVERIES.md` — but they sort into shapes, and **the shape is the reusable part**:

| Shape | Instances | What to run |
|---|---|---|
| **The expression reduces to nothing at the mod's real inputs.** Integer division, a threshold outside its own domain, a count × interval too small to see. | 1, 2 | Substitute the shipped numbers and compute. |
| **A gate cannot bind what it names.** Disjoint sets, an exclusion that covers the whole grant, a narrowing that leaves another channel open, an interface that is never enumerated. | 3, 4, 5, 6 | Enumerate what can satisfy *both* sides — and, per instance 3, over *time* as well as over inputs. |
| **An unset field's DEFAULT does the damage.** Nothing is written in YAML, so nothing looks wrong; the default is a live value that silently rewrites the result. | 7, 8 | For every field a warhead/trait reads, ask what it equals when nobody sets it — and whether that value is *reachable* on your delivery path. |
| **The right trigger wired to the wrong action.** The condition is correct and fires on schedule; what it then does is not what the comment beside it says. | 9, 10 | Read the body of the thing the condition calls. Do not infer it from the call site's name. |
| **The value is in the wrong UNIT.** The field is set, read, and arithmetically used — but denominated differently from the thing it is compared or summed against, so it is either enforced elsewhere or too small to matter. | 11, 12 | Write the unit and the realistic magnitude range beside every term it meets. Cells vs `WDist`, and "how big is this next to its neighbour" are the two questions. |
| **The CONSUMER was deleted and the configuration surface survived.** The `Info` field, its `[Desc]` and the player-facing lobby entry all still parse and render; the code that read them is gone. | 14 | For any long-dormant feature, grep for a *reader* of each config field — not for the field. |

Shapes three and four **cannot be found by reducing an expression** — there is no expression, only a name that does not describe its body. Shape five is the one that survives *all* the earlier detectors: the expression reduces fine, the field has readers, and the callee does what its name says — the arithmetic is simply being done in two currencies.

### The instances

1. **An effect mounted, tuned to imperceptibility.** `WithHealFlash` is on `^ExistsInWorld` (`defaults.yaml`). Its C# defaults are `Alpha = 0.3f`, `Count = 2`, `Interval = 2` (`Traits/Render/WithHealFlash.cs`). `FlashTarget.Render` tints only when `tick % interval == 0` (`Effects/FlashTarget.cs`) and the effect dies at `++tick >= count * interval` — so `Count 2, Interval 2` is **two single-tick tints 120 ms apart**, not 240 ms of tint. The user reported "no effect when medics heal" about a feature that had shipped, working, for months. `Interval: 1` is the only way to get a continuous glow; the mod sets `Count: 6, Interval: 1` (with `Brightness: 1.4`) on `^ExistsInWorld`'s `WithHealFlash`.
2. **A scoring term that is integer-zero across its entire domain.** The medic's patient scorer carried `score += dist / 10240` under the comment *"slight distance tiebreaker (1 point per 10 cells)"*. That is integer division on a WDist length, and `^MEDI`'s `SearchRange` is `8c0` = 8192 (`infantry.yaml`). **8192 / 10240 == 0 at every reachable distance**, so distance never affected patient choice at all and ties fell to enumeration order. Now `DistancePenaltyPerCell` in cell units, with the trap preserved in its `[Desc]` (`HealerAutoTarget.cs`).
3. **A mutual-exclusion lockout between two things that can never contend — and the one entry in this list that was itself wrong.** The Tunguska's 9M311 armament (`Armament@2`, `secondary`) is paused by `firing-primary` (`vehicles-russia.yaml`, `tunguska:`). `firing-primary` is granted by `GrantConditionOnPreparingAttack@1` with `ArmamentNames: primary`; `primary` is the AG gun (`30mm.Tunguska`, `ValidTargets: Infantry, Vehicle, Defense`), while the 9M311 is `ValidTargets: Air`. **No actor in the mod advertises `Air` alongside `Vehicle`/`Infantry`/`Defense`** — aircraft split those by the `airborne` condition — so the two armaments can never be valid against one target. `Armament.CanFire` also rejects on `IsValidAgainst`, *before* `INotifyAttack.PreparingAttack` is notified, so the AG gun cannot grant the condition **while engaging air**.
   Both observations are true. **The conclusion first drawn from them — "the whole condition evaluates to nothing" — was wrong, as a playtest report showed**, and that reading committed the exact error the section exists to warn about: it reduced the expression for *one target at one instant* and never asked how long the condition outlives the shot that granted it. The two armaments never need to contend for a single target, because **the pause is not simultaneous — it is a residue.** The gun grants `firing-primary` while engaging **ground**, where it is entirely valid; the helicopter that arrives afterwards meets a Tunguska whose missiles are still paused. `PreparingAttack` pushed a token **per shot with no `MaximumInstances` cap** while `Tick` pops **one per `RevokeDelay`** (50 ticks, `RevokeAll` defaulting false), so emptying the 180-round pool left 180 tokens draining over 9000 ticks — **540 s of missile lockout at Timestep 60**, with a full missile pool. Note which quantity drives it: *shots fired*, not the empty magazine. Running dry clears `ammo-primary` and thereby *stops* the granting, so exhaustion is merely the most visible moment at which the count is guaranteed large. It compounds with the `AutoTarget` scan-radius collapse ([§Engine behaviors that surprise](#engine-behaviors-that-surprise-debugging-gotchas)): with the guns dry (`!ammo-primary`) **and** `firing-primary` stuck, all three armaments are paused, `GetMaximumRange()` returns `WDist.Zero`, and the Tunguska stops *seeing* aircraft as well as shooting them. Fixed by capping the stack in `GrantConditionOnPreparingAttack.ShouldGrantInstance`, pinned by `PreparingAttackTokenStackTest`; the condition now clears one `RevokeDelay` after the last shot however many were fired. **Reduce the expression over *time*, not only over inputs — a condition's value includes how long it persists.**
4. **A re-gate that shipped as a comment and did not take effect until something else changed.** `12.7mm.Hind` carries a dated, specific, confident comment (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`): *"GROUND ONLY as of 260815 — Helicopter moved to 12.7mm.Hind.AA."* The edit it describes was really made — `Helicopter` is absent from its `ValidTargets: Infantry, Unarmored, Light` and `12.7mm.Hind.AA` exists. **It did nothing at first**, because while the bare armour class leaked into flight the gun kept reaching light helicopters (littlebird, TRAN, HALO) through the `Light` it still lists. It became true only when `15b6201a` gated `Targetable@Armor` to `!airborne`, so a flying helicopter now advertises `AirLight`, not `Light`. The artefact left behind was *more* convincing than no artefact would have been, and is exactly what stops the next person checking.
5. **A declared capability cancelled by an exclusion list.** `^5.56mm` declared `ValidTargets: … Helicopter` and `InvalidTargets: Light, Medium, Heavy`. Exclusion wins (`WeaponInfo.IsValidTarget`), every helicopter is light or heavy, so the declared anti-helicopter capability was **100% dead** while armour classes leaked into flight. Fixed by moving `^5.56mm` to `AirLight` with `InvalidTargets: Medium, Heavy`. Full detail in [§`InvalidTargets` beats `ValidTargets`](#invalidtargets-beats-validtargets-so-an-armour-class-can-silently-cancel-a-declared-capability).
6. **A load-time guard that was never enumerated, and compiled clean.** `Ruleset.cs` iterates `a.TraitInfos<IRulesetLoaded>()` — the **non-generic** interface, which is the only one carrying `ITraitInfoInterface` (`TraitsInterfaces.cs`). A `TraitInfo` that declares only `IRulesetLoaded<ActorInfo>` implements an identical method signature, **compiles with no error and no warning, and is never invoked** — `TraitInfos<T>()` cannot match it. The correct form is the bare `IRulesetLoaded` on the class with the method implemented explicitly as `void IRulesetLoaded<ActorInfo>.RulesetLoaded(…)`; the interface named on the class and the one named on the method are deliberately different, which is precisely why the wrong version looks right. `AmmoPoolInfo` (`AmmoPool.cs`) is the corrected reference. **A validation that never runs is worse than no validation, because everyone who reads it afterwards banks it as safety.**

7. **An omitted field whose default silently divides the damage away.** `DamageWarhead.Penetration` defaults to **1** (`Warheads/DamageWarhead.cs`), and `DamageWarhead.ApplyPenetration` returns `damage * penetration / thickness` whenever penetration is under the victim's armour. So a `TargetDamage` warhead that simply **does not mention** `Penetration` delivers `damage / thickness` — against a 280mm tank, **0.4% of the number written in the YAML**. Both ballistic missiles shipped this way: `IskanderExplosion` and `HIMARSExplosion` were the only large `TargetDamage` warheads in the mod with no value at all, while every comparable weapon sets one (`^12.7mm` 15, `^30mm` 70, `ATGM` 100, `RPG` 500, `^TankRound` 800, `Ataka` 900, `^ArtilleryRound` 1000, read from the caliber templates). **Nothing in the YAML looked wrong, because nothing was written.** The reviewable artefact of an omission is absent by construction — which is what makes this shape different from instances 1–6, where there was always a line to read. Sizing note: the thickest armour in the mod is **2000** (`MSLO`, `structures-defenses.yaml`, no `Distribution` so never discounted by hit direction), not the 280 of a T-90 — a "kills anything in one shot" warhead has to clear 2000.
8. **An unset field whose default is a plausible value, in the wrong coordinate space.** `WarheadArgs.ImpactPosition` was assigned by **projectiles only** — seven projectile types (`Bullet`, `Missile`, `GravityBomb`, `LaserZap`, `Railgun`, `AreaBeam`, `InstantHit`) plus the `WarheadArgs(ProjectileArgs)` constructor (`WeaponInfo.cs`). The **projectile-less** path, `WeaponInfo.Impact(in Target, Actor firedBy)`, built its args with an object initializer that never mentioned it, so it stayed `WPos.Zero` — **the map origin**, a perfectly valid `WPos` that no guard rejects. Everything delivered by `Explodes` / `SpawnedExplodes` takes that path, including both ballistic missiles (`Explodes.Killed`). `TargetDamageWarhead` then scales damage by `CenterProximityPercent(victim, args.ImpactPosition)`, measured from the map corner: against a T-90's 1030-unit half-diagonal that is **−2782%**, and `Health.InflictDamage` clamps rather than floors at zero — `(hp - damage).Clamp(floor, maxHp)` (`Health.ApplyDamageToHp`) — **so the direct-hit warhead healed its target.** `SpreadDamageWarhead` reads the same field for impact *orientation*, so every `Explodes` splash was also computing hit direction from the origin. Fixed in `WeaponInfo.Impact` (`ImpactPosition = target.CenterPosition`); `TargetDamageWarhead.ProximityDamagePercent` additionally floors the percentage at zero, because `Spread` admits victims by distance from the hitshape **edge** while `CenterProximityPercent` normalises against the **centre-to-corner** distance, and on a long thin hull those disagree. **A negative damage number is not a rounding artefact in this engine; it is a heal.**
9. **The right trigger wired to the wrong action.** `Armament.CheckFire` tested exactly the right condition for restarting an interrupted burst — and then called `UpdateBurst`, which is the **per-shot advance**. It ran `--Burst`, so the "reset" removed a shot: a burst broken off with R shots left came back delivering **R−1**. At `R = 1` the decrement hit 0, which *completes* a burst and resets the counter to full — so one case in the family was accidentally correct, which is exactly why the bug read as intermittent rather than systematic. It also emitted a spurious `INotifyBurstComplete.FiredBurst` and scheduled an `AfterFireSound` with no shot fired. **The call site named the intent and the callee did the opposite; nothing about the line was reducible.** Now `Armament.ResetBurst`, with the reset clock keyed off `BurstSequence.StaleTick` — see [`missiles.md` §9](missiles.md).
10. **A value computed, then overwritten on the very next line — while still consuming a synced RNG draw.** `Armament.ResetBurst` called `SharedRandom.Next(...)` into `Burst` for `WeaponInfo.BurstRandomize`, and then assigned the modifier-adjusted `Burst` over it immediately. Three weapons set the field (`5.56mm.AR`, `25mm.Bradley`, and one further `^30mm` derivative), so **the draw really was being consumed and the value really was discarded** — the field has never had an effect, and the balance of those three was tuned with it inert. The dead computation is gone; the field is kept, with its `[Desc]` (`WeaponInfo.BurstRandomize`) saying NOT IMPLEMENTED, because implementing it would put `SharedRandom` back in the firing path and change those weapons' output. **Grepping for readers would NOT have found this one** — the field had a reader, on the correct line, doing the correct arithmetic. Only reading the next line finds it.

11. **A leash declared on the MASTER and enforced on the SLAVE, in different units.** `CarrierMasterInfo.MaxSlaveDistance` has **zero readers engine-wide** — as do `MaxSlaveDistanceCheckInterval` and `SpawnIsMissile` (all in `CarrierMaster.cs`; every other hit in the tree is a comment or a test). `^DR` set `MaxSlaveDistance: 20c0`, so the YAML asserted a 20-cell leash while the game enforced the slave-side `CarrierSlave.MaxDistance` — **denominated in CELLS, not `WDist`**, so the two fields are not even the same kind of number. The dead line has been removed and all three engine fields now carry `[Desc("UNREAD - setting this does nothing…")]` naming the field that actually bites. **The general shape: in a master/slave trait pair, the half you are configuring is not necessarily the half that enforces** — grep the *other* class before tuning either.

12. **A scoring term denominated in a different unit from the term it competes against — inert by construction, and invisible to every gate the project owns.** The drone's task score was `((long)revealedStaleSquares * 1000) + contactBonus - poiDistanceCells`, with `ContactBonus: 2000` in YAML. Because the term it competes against is multiplied by 1000, the whole bonus was worth **two revealed squares** — against a query whose own corner artefact is up to **228** squares (the sampled box is 29×29 = 841 and the inscribed disc is 613; both numbers are asserted in `DroneTaskingMathTest.cs`). A 0.9% nudge inside a 228-square noise floor cannot change an outcome, so a knob that looked like a major weighting had never once moved a decision. **Nothing in the project could see it:** it compiles, the field has a real reader on the correct line, lint has no opinion on units, and the NUnit tests assert *orderings* — which a negligible-but-nonzero term satisfies perfectly. Fixed at `46a226fa` by folding the contact evidence into the same currency (`DroneTaskingMath.cs`, `var worth = revealedStaleSquares + intelSquares;`), with the reasoning kept as an in-code comment above it; the `ContactBonus` parameter is gone. **The detector is dimensional, not logical: for every additive term in a score, write down its unit and its realistic magnitude range beside the terms it is summed with.** An ordering assertion is structurally unable to catch this — see [`README.md` §"Four shapes"](README.md) #4.

13. **A step that resolved to zero at every repair in the game, whose second-order effect was worse than the missing heal.** `Resupply.RepairTick` derived its step as `repairable.Info.HpPerStep > 0 ? repairable.Info.HpPerStep : repairsUnits.Info.HpPerStep` — and **both defaults are 0** in this engine (`RepairsUnitsInfo.HpPerStep`, `RepairableInfo.HpPerStep`, where upstream OpenRA has 10), with nothing under `mods/` setting either. So `InflictDamage(new Damage(-0))` healed nothing, and the `Math.Max(1, …)` on the line below applies to the *cost*, so the player was charged 1 credit per no-op tick. Meanwhile a `PercentageStep` was declared on **both** info classes, set to 3 on `^Vehicle` (`rules/ingame/vehicles.yaml`) — and read by nothing; the only code in the repo reading a field of that name was `ChangesHealth`'s own unrelated one. The YAML side of the change was written and the engine side never finished; it came in with `1f6ea0d4` ("2022 integration"). **The wedge is the part that made it expensive:** `activeResupplyTypes` keeps its `Repair` flag until `health.DamageState == DamageState.Undamaged`, which a zero step can never reach, and the activity's only exit is `activeResupplyTypes == 0` — so a damaged vehicle sent to a Logistics Centre rearmed and then sat there indefinitely until something else gave it an order. Fixed by a percentage fallback gated on `hpToRepair <= 0`, so any host that *does* set `HpPerStep` is byte-identical (`Resupply.RepairTick`). **Detector note: a defect whose symptom is "nothing happened" hides inside a system whose normal state is also nothing happening** — repairs are rare enough that "the tank did not heal" reads as "it was not damaged enough".

14. **An upstream merge deleted the consumer and left the whole configuration surface standing.** `SupportPowerInfo.LobbyChargeIntervalId` (`Traits/SupportPowers/SupportPower.cs`) has **zero readers repo-wide** — the only other hits are two commented usages in `player.yaml`. `PowersLobbyOptions.AirstrikeCooldown` is likewise assigned and read by nothing. `git log -S "LobbyChargeIntervalId" -- .../SupportPowerManager.cs` returns exactly two commits: `6f2191be` added the parsing, and **`71687440` ("Upstream merge: fix OpenRA.Game compilation + resolve duplicate types") removed it.** The `[Desc]`, the field and a five-value player-facing lobby dropdown all outlived their implementation, and **nothing lints for that** — a never-read `readonly` field is not a warning, and a lobby option resolving to a value nobody uses renders normally. Riding along: the removed parser used 25 tps and the surviving `[Desc]` still asserts it, so the shipped `ChargeInterval: 6000` is **6 minutes, not the 4** the dropdown default implies — the same 1.5× family as the section below. **When re-enabling any long-dormant feature, grep for a reader of each config field before trusting it.**

### The same failure in comments: an asserted duration the code does not produce

**RA-era 25 ticks/second is the default assumption of anyone writing a duration comment, and it is wrong for this mod everywhere.** WW3MOD's `default` game speed is `Timestep: 60` (`mod.yaml`, `GameSpeeds: DefaultSpeed: default`) = **16.67 tps**, so a tick count annotated "N seconds at 25 tps" really runs **1.5× longer**. It recurs rather than being an incident: `SupplyRouteContestation`'s `BaseTicks: 1500` was annotated "60s" (really 90 s), `MinTicks: 500` "20s" (30 s), `BaseRecoveryTicks: 3000` "120s" (180 s). **Grep for `25 tps` before trusting any duration comment**, and prefer expressing tick-domain quantities in ticks with the seconds figure derived, not asserted. The engine, the Lua `DateTime` global and the autotest harness all share the one 16.667 base (§"`Timestep` is MILLISECONDS PER TICK" above). **A seconds-literal deadline in a scenario written while the harness constant was still 25 was authored against 25 tps, so it now allows a third less real time than its author measured — treat it as un-audited.**

**A rule of the form "aim at X" is a claim about a defect that is still live, and it becomes an instruction to re-create the defect the moment the defect is fixed.** The live example: while the harness ran at 25 and the engine Lua converter truncated to 16, the advice was "if the harness constant is ever corrected, the target is 16". Both are now 16.667, and following that advice would re-open a 4 % divergence; `AutotestTickRateTest` fails on a 25 or a 16 alike.

**Census, from `git grep -nIiE '25 ?tps|25 ticks ?/ ?s|25 ticks/sec|25 ticks per sec' -- engine mods tools ':!*.md'`. Recall is unverified: a silent division contains no phrase to grep for.** Most hits are comments warning *against* 25 tps. The ones whose seconds figure is still wrong:
- `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/SmartMove.cs`, `UnderFireDuration`'s `[Desc]`: "~3 sec at 25 tps" for 75 ticks, really 4.5 s.
- `engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/SupportPowers/SupportPower.cs`, `LobbyChargeIntervalId`'s `[Desc]`: "Parsed at 25 ticks/second" (the field has no reader — instance 14 above).
- `mods/ww3mod/scripts/scenario.lua`: `briefingDuration = 375, -- ~15 seconds at 25 tps`, really 22.5 s.
- `test-missile-hellfire-probe.lua`: "BurstWait: 1000 — 40 seconds at 25 ticks/s", really 60 s.
- `test-stance-anchor-move.lua` and `test-stance-redirect-midadjust.lua`: "Deadlines (25 ticks/sec)" — the harness base is 16.667.
- Scenario comments asserting "TestHarness.TicksPerSecond is 25", which stopped being true when the harness moved to 16.667: `git grep -n 'TicksPerSecond is 25' tools/autotest/scenarios`.
- `engine/OpenRA.Game/Map/Map.cs` ("well within budget at 25 tps") is a throughput note where the rate is not load-bearing.

`tools/autotest/parse-floor-denominator.py` buckets ticks into game minutes at 25 ticks/s; that is right for the tournament configs it reads, which run at `GameSpeed: fastest` (40 ms), and wrong for anything at the default speed. A related stale line cite: `SupplyRouteContestation.cs`'s timing comment points at a `mod.yaml` line that no longer holds the default `Timestep`.

**Worked instance of the diagnostic direction:** a scenario comment divided `TimeToBeStill: 200` (`infantry.yaml`) by 25, got 8.0 s, and concluded a capture request's "12.0" was mistaken. 200 ÷ 16.67 is exactly 12.0 — the request was right and the comment argued the reader out of a correct number.

**It is not only code comments — grep `DOCS/` too.** This error has appeared inside this curated bank (`game-model.md` once described the clearing `CaptureDelay: 1000` as "~40 s"; it is **60 s**) and in a recipe's log-volume sizing, and neither instance contained the string `25 tps`: one had done the division silently and only the *result* was wrong. The reliable tell is a seconds figure sitting beside a tick count; recompute it rather than scanning for a phrase. The `world.Timestep`-vs-`GameSpeed.Timestep` mechanism is under [§Engine behaviors that surprise](#engine-behaviors-that-surprise-debugging-gotchas).

### A loop with no FIXED POINT cannot be damped — damping bounds an excursion's amplitude, it cannot create a fixed point

*(The worked instances are a supply-truck oscillation; the lesson is about the shape of the fix, not about trucks, and three review rounds of damping failed before it was reached.)*

**The diagnostic question that separates this from ordinary jitter: does the loop have a fixed point at all?** Dwell, hysteresis, deadband and leash all bound how far an excursion goes. **If the input that selects a destination is regenerated identically as soon as the action's effect decays, the loop is periodic by construction and no damper terminates it** — it only sets the period.

- **A hysteresis/dwell can only damp a loop that is CLOSED.** The worked failure: a retreat decision read danger *at the mover*, and the action it triggered moved the mover — closing that half — while the other disjunct read danger *at a destination the mover's retreat did not affect*. Open. **Name the term that does not respond to the action before you reach for a damper.**
- **The structural move is to make the destination an ACTOR-INDEPENDENT, STATIONARY thing.** Against a static target the approach activity's stop condition becomes reachable, a retry counter measured in approach attempts stops being consumed by aura entry/exit flips, and the selector cannot re-select a target that has moved. **Re-issuing an identical order every scan is only a bug when the destination is derived from the mover** — every previous fix in that area had to add memory (`lastVia`, an evac state, a leg model) purely because the destination was computed from the truck's own position.
- **Keep the remaining memory on the DESTINATION, not on the DECISION.** Hysteresis applied to a *position* cannot latch a branch — the worst it can do is keep aiming at a slightly stale cell.
- **Check that the action switches its own precondition off, and prefer that it do so through SEVERAL independent terms** — then a bug in one does not produce a repeat-fire loop. **But "every term responds to the action" is not enough: ask how long the response TAKES relative to the decision cadence.** Terms that respond to a *completed* action, evaluated in one loop over unchanged world state, all hold on the first scan — which is how a first cut dumped an entire fleet's load at one cell.
- **A re-issue dedup keyed on "already dispatched" silently disables every retry the design was relying on.** It converts self-correcting refusals (arrival on an occupied cell; a destination that became unreachable after issue) from *"retries next scan"* into *"parks forever"* — a latch introduced by the fix for a stutter. The repair is a responsive term, not a memo.
- **Choose a damper's asymmetry from the COST OF EACH ERROR, and re-derive it per site — do not copy the nearest existing one.** Damping *leaving* an evac and never *entering* one is right when a delayed withdrawal kills the unit; a latch whose wrong setting is the expensive direction needs the mirror image. And **"value band vs dwell" is only a real choice when the signal is a scalar at all**: a verdict produced by a *set scan* over an aura has no single quantity to band, and banding the aura instead re-opens whatever bug made selection and delivery agree on one boundary.
- **A comment asserting irreversibility is worth checking against the GATE, not against the intent** — "this transition cannot be undone" is a claim about every path that could undo it, and the paths are usually idle-gated somewhere far from the comment.
- **Two test traps that kept these green.** A pin can be a tautology over its own signature (short-circuiting on the first argument) while its comment describes a block the pin never reaches — **check each pin can actually go RED before counting it.** And a suite that only ever exercises the **zero** value of the term whose *non-zero* behaviour is the bug proves nothing about it.

**A comment that JUSTIFIES a workaround by naming an engine limit is trusted the way a derivation is, and
only half of it is checkable from the file.** A weapons-file
header stated that past `ScalePercent 1600` a sprite "cannot be magnified further, so the extra size is
spent on a ring of sprites instead", and a weapon drew five copies of one animation on exactly that
reasoning. **There is no ceiling:** `scale` is a plain float multiplied into the quad
(`Graphics/SpriteRenderable.cs`, handed to `SpriteRenderer.DrawSprite` as `scale * s.Size`) and nothing in
that path clamps anything. One grep would have settled it; two passes quoted the claim back as established
fact instead. **The "therefore we do Y" half is right there in the YAML and verifiable; the premise sits in
engine code nobody re-reads**, and "fix verifiably-wrong statements on sight" does not help when the
statement is only wrong somewhere else.

Two tells worth carrying. The workaround's own arithmetic often refutes it: that ring applied its offset as
`pos + Offset * ScalePercent / 100`, which moved the copies under 5 % of the sprite's radius — so the stated
purpose (size) was not what it delivered (five additively-blended copies, i.e. brightness). And **when a
comment admits a divergence, measure the divergence in the comment.** A note reading "the copies are NOT
equivalent today — this one omits the fog palette's own alpha" sat accurate and honest in
`WorldRenderer` while a user reported the symptom as a bug; the same note carrying *"4.35x at the commonest
visibility"* reads as a defect rather than a nuance. The number is what decides whether the next reader
fixes it or walks past.

### A field added to fix a defect DEFAULTS to the old behaviour, so its absence is indistinguishable from the pre-fix value

This is a property of the class, not of any one field: for the
default path to stay bit-identical, a new field's default has to be the behaviour the fix replaced. So
`StartRadius` unset and `StartRadius: 0` reach the engine identically, and a weapon authored from a
pre-fix template inherits the pre-fix physics with **no lint error, no failing test and no log line**.
`--check-yaml` catches a field that does not exist; nothing catches a field that is absent and defaults
back.

The failure is silent, at a distance, in someone else's commit. Eight nuclear weapons were added in a new
file the day after a shockwave-birth fix landed, every one of them a point source at a flat sound speed -
the precise defect just fixed - and nothing went red, for two compounding reasons worth recognising
separately. The fixture read `MiniYaml.FromFile(...weapons-superweapons.yaml)`, one hard-coded filename, so
the new file was invisible to it; and every test iterated a two-name array inline. **A hard-coded fixture
list is not a bug when it is written, because at that moment it IS the population.** It fails in the
safest-looking direction: the two weapons it names are still correct, so the suite is honestly green about a
shrinking fraction of what it claims to cover.

**So: when you add a field to fix a defect, the test that pins it must assert over a list DERIVED FROM THE
FILES** - or over a list whose staleness is itself an assertion - **and must assert the field is set at
all** (`Assert.That(startRadius, Is.GreaterThan(0))`), so "defaulted back" fails loudly rather than reading
as an unset field nobody looks at. `NuclearYieldTest.AllNukes` is **not** an instance of
either form: it is a hand-typed roster (its own doc comment says anything added to either weapons file "has to
be added here", and `ExchangeVariantTest` lists it among the hand-maintained rosters), so it widens coverage
beyond the old two-name pair but still carries the staleness hazard described above.

### Every quantity checked against its own law, and nothing checking the RELATIONSHIP between them

*(Four instances from one week's nuclear-presentation work, kept as
one rule because the shape recurred four times and the instances did not.)* The recurring failure is a
suite in which **every** test compares a value to its own derivation, every test passes, and the property
the player actually perceives belongs to neither subsystem and is asserted nowhere:

- **A presentation ramp measured in ticks, sitting in front of a simulation phase also measured in ticks,
  with nothing tying them together.** A shockwave ring's fade-in was 20-154 ticks while the supersonic
  phase it was meant to reveal lasted 1-55, so the whole fast sweep rendered at 0.3-3 % opacity. The
  simulation was right, the YAML-reading tests were right, and the player saw a ring that appeared late and
  crawled.
- **Two quantities in different subsystems whose RATIO is what is seen.** A light radius on one exponent
  and the sprite drawn over it on another: neither number wrong, the ratio swinging an order of magnitude
  across the ladder, and below a threshold the lit ground sat entirely under the sprite. **When a user
  reports an effect is invisible, ask what is DRAWN OVER IT before asking how bright it is** - and the tell
  that this class is in play is that the complaint is about a magnitude, the magnitude is provably correct,
  and the two cases the user says look right are the extremes of some *other* ordering.
- **A per-weapon law check passes on a ladder with a hole in it** whenever the hole is a deliberate
  hand-tuned exception. **Assert MONOTONICITY across the sorted ladder as well as each value against the
  law** - they are different assertions, they fail on different bugs, and the monotonicity one catches
  every hand-capped exception that inverts the order without needing to know the law.
- **An absolute tolerance on a quantity spanning five orders of magnitude is a percentage tolerance that
  gets STRICTER as the number grows**, so it passes the small cases that could hide a real error and fails
  the large ones over rounding. Use a relative tolerance (`Within(0.5).Percent`) whenever the subject is a
  ladder.

**The generalisation: "the physics is correct and the player cannot see it" is a class of bug that green
simulation tests are structurally unable to report.** Ask separately what is on screen during the ticks the
new behaviour occupies. And when a rendering constraint and a physical derivation collide on this mod's
maps, **the map is the binding constraint and the physics is the decoration** - expect visual exponents to
end up compressed, and say so where a file would otherwise keep claiming a derivation it no longer has.

### Detectors worth running before you believe a mechanism works

- **Reduce the expression at the mod's real inputs.** Integer division, a threshold compared against a range, a percentage of a small base — substitute the shipped numbers and compute. `distance / 10240` against an 8192 radius is zero; `Count × Interval` at 2×2 is two frames.
- **Grep every reader of a field you are about to tune**, not its declaration. Zero readers is common and silent, and a reader on a path that never runs is no better: `InaccuracyPerProjectile`, `DamageAtMaxRange` and `MovementInaccuracy` all have readers, are all set in shipped YAML, and are all inert where they are set (see [§"Engine behaviors that surprise"](#engine-behaviors-that-surprise-debugging-gotchas)). **A reader is not enough — read the line AFTER it too.** `BurstRandomize` (instance 10) had a reader, on the right line, doing the right arithmetic, into a variable the next statement overwrote.
- **Ask what each field a code path reads equals when NOBODY sets it.** The dangerous default is not zero-as-disabled; it is a live value that looks legitimate — `Penetration` defaults to 1 (a divisor, not a no-op), `ImpactPosition` defaults to the map origin (a valid coordinate). Then ask the second half: is that default *reachable on my delivery path*? An `Explodes` payload never goes near the projectile code that would have set it.
- **When a conclusion rests on a flag never being reset, grep every ASSIGNMENT of that flag — and then ask whether the notification that would re-trigger it still fires when the value it carries is zero.** Reading the latch is not reading the mechanism: what decides the behaviour is who *else* clears the flag, and whether the handler is even called at zero damage. Both questions are static, neither needs a launch, and either alone catches the error. The live instance: `Cargo`'s emergency-bail latch is cleared by `Load()` as well as by the damage-state reset (`Cargo.Load`, with the reason in-file), so an audit that traced only the reset concluded the bail could fire once and never re-arm. The zero-damage half has teeth because `Health.InflictDamage` runs `notifyDamage` unconditionally — there is no "applied damage was zero, skip the notification" branch — while some `INotifyDamage` handlers open with `if (e.Damage.Value <= 0) return;` and others do not.
- **When a comment names an intent, open the function it calls.** Instance 9 was a correct trigger calling a decrement named as a reset. The call site read exactly as intended and there was nothing to reduce.
- **When the fix IS "correct a factual claim", grep the FILE for the claim — never your own diff.** One such correction cost four rounds: the original statement was wrong, the reviewer's replacement SHA was wrong, and the commit that fixed both **left the same false phrase stale ten lines below**, because the author reviewed the change they had made rather than searching for the sentence they had just declared false. **A factual claim is usually repeated near itself**, so the diff is the wrong search scope. This is not hypothetical for the reference bank either: a stale `^E6` claim was found sitting in *two* places in `economy.md`, with three different and equally wrong line citations between them, and fixing only the first would have left the doc still asserting it.
- **A curation pass is also a doc audit, and the two wrong statements it finds will not be the ones you were looking for.** Both `economy.md` defects above surfaced while verifying an unrelated DISCOVERIES entry against code — not by reading `economy.md`, which nobody had reason to re-read because its claims are the ones agents are told to trust. **Verifying a candidate claim against source is the only routine activity that re-reads the bank at all**, so when a promotion check touches a doc section, spend the extra minute on the surrounding paragraph.
- **For a gate or exclusion, enumerate what can actually satisfy both sides.** A lockout between disjoint target sets, or an exclusion covering every member of a granted capability, is a no-op with a convincing name.
- **For a guard, prove it FAILS against the thing it forbids before trusting it** — hand-author a violation and watch it error. A green build is not evidence that a validation ran. (Same RED-first discipline the behavioural work already follows; see [`DOCS/recipes/AUTOTEST.md` §"A green run is not evidence unless something could have made it RED"](../recipes/AUTOTEST.md).)
- **When a re-gate works by narrowing `ValidTargets`, narrowing is not sufficient** — confirm the target no longer advertises anything *else* the weapon still accepts. Neither the lint nor the type system checks for a `ValidTargets` entry that can never match, or for a removal that changes no behaviour. The only reliable instrument is a reachability matrix built from the parsed corpus and diffed across the change. **Sampling will not find these, because the sample you would skip is the one the comment tells you is already handled.**

**Removing an over-eager terminator exposes every downstream path it was accidentally protecting you from.** A guard that fires too early is not merely a false positive — it is an *unearned safety net*: it terminates the subject before it can reach the code downstream of it, so those paths accumulate defects that nothing ever demonstrates. Fix the guard so it fires only when it should and every one of them becomes reachable **in the same commit**. Expect the report to arrive as *"this is new since your change"*, expect that to be literally true, and expect it still not to mean the change was wrong. **What to do instead of scoping review to the predicate: enumerate what ran *after* the terminator and ask which of those paths has never been exercised in anger** — statically, by grepping every assignment to the state the terminator sets and auditing each site as newly-reachable code.

**Two instrument rules, both of which turned a confident wrong answer into a finding:**

- **When instrumenting to test a hypothesis, count every exit on the path, not just the one you suspect.** A counter on the suspected gate alone returns zero and leaves you with a falsified prediction and no answer — same run cost, none of the information. (Worked instance: a module's silence was attributed to an order gate; instrumenting all three of its silent exits showed the gate had refused **nothing** and the real exit was a different one entirely.)
- **Read the raw stream, not the derived counter.** A derived aggregate encodes the question its author was asking and will silently return the wrong answer to an adjacent one — a counter of `false → true` *edges* answers "did this re-latch?" correctly and is structurally blind to "did it ever clear?". When your question is adjacent-but-not-identical to the one a counter was built for, go back to the per-record data.

## PITFALL comments (full spec: [`pitfalls.md`](pitfalls.md))

Recurring traps get a one-line `// PITFALL:` (`# PITFALL:` in YAML) **at the temptation site** — the line a careless reader is looking at when about to fall in. List all: `git grep PITFALL`. Add them during bug fixes when the root cause would surprise a reader; prune when stale (a wrong PITFALL is worse than none). Not for one-shot fixes, generic best practice, or universal anti-patterns (those go in a hook or the engine code rules below).

## Engine code rules (enforced by `tools/git-hooks/pre-commit`)

- **`[Sync]` on a trait that does not implement `ISync` hashes nothing and warns about nothing.** The type test is `trait is ISync`, where `Actor` builds its `syncHashesList` (`Actor.cs`). Adding the attribute is not adding sync coverage — see architecture.md §"`[Sync]` is inert without `ISync`" for the rule, the effects equivalent, and the NUnit guard that now catches it.
- **No `Console.Write`/`WriteLine` in tick-path code** — use `Log.Write(channel, ...)`. Console output fires every tick and floods stdout. Allowlisted directories: `UtilityCommands/`, `UpdateRules/`, `/Lint/`, `OpenRA.Server/`, `OpenRA.Test/`, `OpenRA.Utility/`, `tools/`.

Hook install (once per clone): `ln -sf ../../tools/git-hooks/pre-commit .git/hooks/pre-commit`

### A green analyzer gate means what the TARGET GRAPH reaches, not what the solution lists

**Release builds have no analyzers at all.** The `DisableAnalyzers` target in `engine/Directory.Build.props` removes every `Analyzer` item when `Configuration=Release`. So any target that routes through a Release build contributes **zero** analyzer coverage, however many projects it compiles — and a `make` target that depends on it still goes green.

That is not hypothetical: it was the whole of the long-standing Windows/Linux gate asymmetry. Both platforms read the same solutions; they diverged on which **engine target** the mod's `check` depends on. `make.ps1` routes `check` into `engine\make.cmd check`, a Debug `-warnaserror` build of `engine/OpenRA.sln`; the `Makefile` has `check: worldactor-gate engine`, and the engine's `all` target is **Release**. Linux/macOS was not missing projects from a solution — it was building the right solution in the wrong configuration.

**`OpenRA.Test` is NOT BUILT BY ANY SOLUTION BUILD, IN EITHER CONFIGURATION — so a green `all` is no evidence that the test project COMPILES.** `engine/OpenRA.sln`'s configuration block gives `OpenRA.Test` (`{6CB8E1B7-…}`) an `ActiveCfg` for Debug **and** Release and **no `Build.0` for either**. In MSBuild solution semantics `ActiveCfg` without `Build.0` means *a member of the solution that is not built*, so `dotnet build` on that solution skips it in EVERY configuration — it is the only project in the file in that state (`OpenRA.WindowsLauncher` is missing one deliberately, Release only, because `Directory.Build.props` sends every `OutputPath` to `engine/bin` and the packaging scripts copy `bin/*.dll` by wildcard; adding one would ship NUnit inside the Linux and macOS packages). **So this is NOT an instance of the Release/analyzer split above: switching to Debug does not help, because the configuration was never the reason.** `make all` builds `engine/OpenRA.sln` (skips it) and then `WW3MOD.sln`, which is `OpenRA.Game` + `OpenRA.Mods.Common` and nothing else; `make test` depends on `all` and inherits exactly that. **The ONLY thing in the tree that compiles the test project as a gate is a hand-added csproj line** — in the `Makefile`'s `check` target and `make.ps1`'s `Check-Command`, both naming `engine/OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Test.csproj -c Debug -warnaserror`, each under a comment stating this fact. **That line is invisible from the solution files**, so a reader checking "is the test project covered?" by opening `OpenRA.sln` concludes it is, because the project is right there in the list. It is the right shape, not a workaround waiting to be tidied. **The gap is widest exactly where it is most dangerous** — a merge, a rename or an extract-and-share refactor, where the compile break lands in a fixture rather than in shipped code and the author has no reason to think they touched tests at all.

**Generalise this: when two CI lanes disagree about what they catch, diff the target graph, not the solution files.** `make.ps1` and `Makefile` are independent implementations of the same target names in this repo and have drifted before.

**`.\make.ps1 check` can be RED on a clean `main`, and the cost is the time each worker spends establishing that the errors are not theirs.** Because it is the one Debug `-warnaserror` build, it fires analyzers that no other target sees, so pre-existing violations accumulate there unnoticed — typically `RCS1226`, `SA1514` and `CS0419` — and the ones who meet them are exactly the ones who just changed engine C# and have every reason to read a red gate as their own. Whether it is red at your base ref is a measurement, not something to cite: run it on the base. **The one-command answer: `git diff --name-only main`, then grep the gate's log for your own filenames — if none match, it predates you.** Capture the log with `.\make.ps1 check 2>&1 | Out-String -Width 500`; the wide `Out-String` is what makes the log greppable at all, and without it console line-wrapping silently drops `file(line,col): error ID` lines out of any per-file tally, so a per-file count taken from the narrow console is a floor, not a census.

**The trap inside the trap is `RCS1226`, and it is why this keeps being reintroduced.** A multi-paragraph `/// <summary>` is the natural way to explain a non-obvious class, reads correctly in every editor, and is **invisible** in `make.ps1 all` (Release, no analyzers) and in `dotnet test`. You only learn about it from `check`. The mechanical fix is to demote the prose to plain `//` comments above the declaration and leave a one-line `<summary>`. **Do not "fix" a red `check` by adding to a baseline: there is no analyzer baseline in this repo** — `mods/ww3mod/lint-baseline.txt` is the YAML lint's and is unrelated. Either fix those files in their own change, or leave them and know why the gate is red.

**`check` FAILS IN STRATA, so its error list is a floor and never a total.** The `Makefile`'s `check` recipe is **three** sequential `dotnet build` lines — `WW3MOD.sln`, then `engine/OpenRA.sln`, then `engine/OpenRA.Test/OpenRA.Test.csproj` — and make aborts the target at the first that returns non-zero. A red `check` therefore reports the errors of **line 1 only**; lines 2 and 3 never compile, and the test assembly needs its own line at all because upstream gives it an `ActiveCfg` and no `Build.0` in `engine/OpenRA.sln`. (`make.ps1 check` has the same strata in a different order: `engine\make.cmd check` for `engine/OpenRA.sln`, then `Check-Command`'s `WW3MOD.sln` build, then the `OpenRA.Test` csproj.) **Re-measure after every fix round; never plan against the first number, and never report "N errors remain" without saying which stratum you have reached.** One measured pass went 33 errors → line 1 green → 6 more in `OpenRA.Test`.

Two analyzer-ID traps that each cost a wrong edit:

- **`RCS1226` is "Add paragraph to documentation comment", NOT "add exception to documentation comment".** It fires when a doc element's content has blank-line-separated paragraphs not wrapped in `<para>` — which this repo's multi-paragraph `<summary>` house style walks into by construction. The fix is presentational and cannot change a claim. The name invites a *semantic* edit ("document the exception this method throws"), and `git grep '<exception cref'` returns nothing in the whole tree, so there is never an exception to document.
- **`SA1612` with a SINGLE `<param>` is not an ordering problem and cannot be fixed by reordering.** The analyzer compares each `<param>`'s index among the `<param>` elements against that parameter's index in the signature, so documenting only the 3rd parameter reports "should be at position 3" with nothing to swap it with. `SA1611` is `none` here (`engine/.editorconfig`), so partial parameter docs **are** legal — but only as a *prefix*: they must start at parameter 1 and run contiguously. Fix by **adding** accurate docs for the preceding parameters.
- **Check for a comment explaining a "redundant" concatenation before you join it.** A deliberately-split string literal can be load-bearing: `DebugVisualizationDefaultsTest.cs` assembles its blocker marker from parts precisely so the file does not itself match a naive grep for the marker it polices. Joining the literals as `RCS1190` asks would defeat the guard; hoisting the middle literal to a named `const` silences the rule (an identifier operand is not a joinable literal) and keeps the assembled marker absent from the file.

**No lint WARNING can fail `make test` as the gate is run — it counts errors only, and this is structural rather than a property of any one check.** In `UtilityCommands/CheckYaml.cs` the two emitters are asymmetric by construction: `EmitError` prints, does `++errors` **and** records `LintBaseline.Signature(scope, e)`, while `EmitWarning` prints and does nothing else — it touches neither the counter nor the signature set. The exit decision is `LintBaseline.Judge(modData, Signatures, errors, …)`, which sees only those two. **A warning is invisible to the gate at every step, not merely amnestied by `mods/ww3mod/lint-baseline.txt`.** The one switch is `TREAT_WARNINGS_AS_ERRORS=true` in the environment, which routes every lint pass's warnings to `EmitError` instead; neither `make test` nor `.\make.ps1 test` sets it, so the statement holds for the gate as shipped. The worked consequence: `CheckFluentReferences` reports a missing key through `emitWarning` at both of its missing-key sites (map ftl and mod ftl) and has **no** `emitError` path for a missing key at all — so a `[FluentReference]` field carrying a raw non-key string (e.g. a `LabelWidget.Text` used as a literal) cannot turn `make test` red however many there are. A whole-mod run reports `Warning: Missing key` by the thousand and `Error: Missing key` never. **Read the flip side before treating this as safety: there is effectively no gate on translation coverage**, so adding the next missing key costs nothing and is indistinguishable from adding none. The risk runs in one direction only — this gap cannot break the build, it can only ship a UI string that renders as its raw key.

Two related traps in the same area:

- **Effective severity in `engine/.editorconfig` is the LAST assignment for an id, not the one under the explanatory comment.** `RCS1058` is set to `warning` and later to `suggestion`, both inside the same `[*.{cs,csproj,yaml,lua,sh,ps1}]` section — a whole block of `SA`/`RCS` rules is silently downgraded this way. **Compute the final value** (`awk` over every `dotnet_diagnostic.<id>.severity`) before concluding a rule is enabled, disabled or broken, and especially before picking a rule to prove a gate is live with: a rule you believe is a `warning` may be reporting as a suggestion and failing nothing.
- **`RUNTIME=mono` is not a build path.** The `Makefile` refuses it outright (`ifeq ($(RUNTIME), mono)` → `$(error RUNTIME=mono is no longer supported; this SDK builds net6 only…)`), `engine/Directory.Build.props` targets `net6.0` only with unconditional StyleCop/Roslynator package references, and `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs only the `Linux (.NET 6.0)` and `Windows (.NET 6.0)` jobs. The single build configuration that drops analyzers is Release (above).
- **`NuGetAudit`'s transitive reach is a target-framework effect, not an SDK effect.** The knob is `NuGetAuditMode`, which defaults to `direct` and only flips to `all` at `net10.0`. Measured on one package, one SDK (10.0.400), bare `dotnet restore`: `NU1902` is not raised at `net6.0` or `net8.0` and is raised at `net10.0`; forcing `-p:NuGetAuditMode=all` reproduces it at `net6.0` on demand. So an SDK pin is not what hides a transitive advisory — it surfaces on the TFM bump commit itself.
- **The mirror case — a gate covering MORE than you think: `Class=Unknown` does NOT hide a map folder from `--check-yaml`, because `HasFlag(0)` is always true.** `mods/ww3mod/mod.yaml`'s `MapFolders` registers the autotest scenario tree as `Unknown`, which does keep it out of every UI tab. But `CheckYaml` calls `EnumerateMapDirPackagesAndNames()` with its default `MapClassification.System`, and the folder filter is `classification.HasFlag(packageClassification)` (`MapCache.EnumerateMapDirPackages`) — with `MapClassification.Unknown = 0` (`MapPreview.cs`), `HasFlag(0)` is `(x & 0) == 0`, true for every value. So the scenario maps are linted exactly like shipped content, and there is **no classification value that excludes them** — the sentinel makes it unfixable by reclassification alone. Two consequences: a per-map defect in the scenario tree is indistinguishable from one in shipped content unless you attribute each error back to its `Testing map:` header, and **error counts multiply by map** — one rules-level defect reports once per linted map, so a total can fall by hundreds without a defect being fixed. **Compare error classes, never totals.**
- **Method note: a `dotnet build` reporting `0 Warning(s)` may simply have been up-to-date.** Check for the `-> ….dll` line and a plausible elapsed time before believing a zero; `-t:Rebuild` forces `CoreCompile` to actually run.

### A grep census is a SAMPLE whose recall nobody checks — use the compiler when completeness is load-bearing

*(The 47-site result below is one measurement, not re-run since.)*

Three independent censuses of `FrozenActor.Owner` — a hand-built table, a subagent grep, and a deliberately widened union grep — returned **9, 17 and 27**. The compiler returned **47**. Each was produced by someone trying to be thorough; none carried any statement of its own method or recall, so all three were written as counts and read as measurements, and the largest was believed purely for being largest.

**Why an identifier grep cannot be fixed by widening it.** One missed site reads `consideration.GetAttractiveness(scrutinized, firedBy.RelationshipWith(scrutinized.Owner))`. The binding is named `scrutinized`; no identifier pattern anyone would think to write matches it, and the space of binding names is unbounded. **A grep census finds only consumers whose author shared your vocabulary.**

**The recall-complete instrument, and it costs one build.** Mark the member obsolete and read the warnings:

```csharp
[Obsolete("ZZCENSUS")] public Player Owner { get; private set; }
```
```bash
make all 2>&1 | grep "warning CS0618"     # exit 0; every compiled assembly reports
```

Four things decide whether this works, and three of them are traps:

- **Use `make all`, never `make check`.** No project file in this repo sets `TreatWarningsAsErrors`, so under `make all` `CS0618` stays a warning and every assembly compiles and reports. But `make check` passes `-warnaserror` on the command line (every `dotnet build` line of the `Makefile`'s and `engine/Makefile`'s `check` targets and of `make.ps1`'s `Check-Command`) — under it the census becomes an error, the build stops at the first failing project, and you get a partial answer that looks complete. **Renaming the member instead has the same failure** for the same reason; it is the obvious first idea and it returns ~37.
- **`make all` covers 8 of the 11 C# projects, and NOT because of solution membership.** `engine/OpenRA.sln` lists ten projects, but `OpenRA.Test` has no `Build.0` in EITHER configuration and `OpenRA.WindowsLauncher` has none for Release, so a Release build skips them (see the bullet above); `tools/fp-determinism/FpDeterminism.csproj` is in no solution at all. Grepping the `.sln` for `Project(` returns 10 and reads like a refutation of this — **the exclusion is in the solution's configuration block, not its project list.** Build those three by hand or the census silently under-reports, and one of them is the test assembly. Same family as the target-graph rule above.
