- **Non-C# surfaces are outside the instrument entirely.** Before claiming a member has *N* readers, state that you also checked YAML, Lua and chrome, and that the type is not `FieldLoader`/`Info`-reflective or exposed through a `ScriptPropertyGroup` — the compiler cannot see any of those bindings.
- **Revert by restoring the one line**, and confirm with `git status --porcelain` before committing.

**What it does NOT answer.** It enumerates consumers of a *member*. It cannot answer "which classes implement interface `I` but not `J`" — for that, "implementors" is not even a well-posed count: at one commit `INotifyOwnerChanged` gave 58 files containing the string, 106 total mentions, and 52 declaration-shaped lines. Three defensible numbers for three different questions.

**The rule: write an enumeration as a claim with its method attached, never as a bare number.** Not *"17 sites"* but *"17 sites; grep `\bfa\.Owner\b` over `engine/**/*.cs`; recall unverified"*. A bare number is indistinguishable from a measurement at the point of reuse, and it will be reused. **If a design decision depends on an enumeration being complete, buy the recall-complete instrument** — one attribute and one build is cheaper than the design being wrong.

**The one instrument stronger than the compiler census, and it is free: check whether the member's own
ACCESSIBILITY already bounds the search space.** `World.nextAID` is `private` to a **sealed, non-partial** `World`, so the language confines
every access to that one file (`World.cs`: the declaration, the post-increment in `NextAID()`, and the
read-only `NextActorID` property). `NextAID()` is `internal` with **no `InternalsVisibleTo` anywhere in `engine/`**, so its callers are
confined to `OpenRA.Game`, and there is exactly one. `new Actor(` has exactly two sites tree-wide, both on
the simulation path. **That is a recall-complete enumeration rather than a grep census** — not because the
greps were thorough, but because `private` in a sealed non-partial class and `internal` without
`InternalsVisibleTo` are *language guarantees* about where a hit could possibly be. The reasoning is carried
in-code in `World.cs` above `NextAID()`, including the warning not to reach for `SyncHash` as the justification
instead. **Ask this before reaching for the `[Obsolete]` instrument; when it applies it costs nothing and is
stronger.**

**A testing-mechanics trap that hides half of a red run: NUnit stops at the FIRST failed assertion in a
`[Test]`, so two subjects in one test means one verified subject.** A guard-deletion run over a `[Test]`
asserting two effects reported `Failed: 1` naming only one of them — which read as success, when in fact the
other half was never evaluated in that run at all. Split, the same mutation reports `Failed: 2` and names
both. **A red-first run only proves what it NAMES; if one test covers N subjects, a red run proves 1 of N.**
(`Assert.Multiple` is the other fix, where splitting is not wanted.)

**And the ceiling of the IL-scan technique, which should be stated wherever it is used.** `IlScan` is a
linear byte walk over `call`/`callvirt`/`newobj` tokens: **it proves a call token appears in a method body.
It does not prove the result is used, used correctly, or reachable.** A guard asserted that way survives
inverting its own `!`, and survives transposing two same-typed `uint` arguments — which *compiles*. Three
repairs, in order of strength: give the value its own type and make the comparison an instance method so
there is nothing to transpose; hand the caller an already-filtered sequence so no branch exists at the call
site; and **assert the ABSENCE of the dangerous call rather than the presence of the safe one** — a wrapper
that swallows the raw sweep makes `Has.None` on it strictly stronger than "the filter is called", because a
filter call can be present and its result discarded. (Demonstrated by doing exactly that: the positive
assertion passed and the negative one caught it.) Pair any `World`-free rule test with a wiring test, or you
have pinned an algorithm nobody is obliged to call.

### Reflection over the loaded assemblies answers "implements A but not B" — grep structurally cannot

The `[Obsolete]` + `CS0618` trick above finds
consumers of a **member**. It cannot answer "which classes implement interface A but not interface B", because
the interface may be inherited from an abstract base and **never named in the file** — there is no text to
match. A throwaway NUnit case answers it exactly, in ~40 lines:

```csharp
foreach (var t in asm.GetTypes().Where(t => t.IsClass && !t.IsAbstract)) {
    var i = t.GetInterfaces();   // transitive closure — inherited interfaces included
    if ((i.Contains(typeof(INotifyAddedToWorld)) || i.Contains(typeof(INotifyRemovedFromWorld)))
        && !i.Contains(typeof(INotifyOwnerChanged)))
        rows.Add(t.FullName);
}
```

`GetInterfaces()` returning the **transitive closure** is what makes this a regression check rather than a
census: once a base class gains the interface, every subclass drops out of the result set — direct evidence the
base handler reaches them all. Anchor each assembly with `typeof(SomePublicType).Assembly`. Three gotchas, each
of which cost a run or a compile:

- **`TestContext.Progress.WriteLine` does NOT survive `dotnet test`'s default console logger.** The test passes
  and the output vanishes. Write to a file from inside the test.
- Many Cnc trait classes are **internal**, so `typeof(TSResourceLayer)` fails `CS0122`. Use a public type such
  as `ChronoshiftPaletteEffect` to anchor `OpenRA.Mods.Cnc`.
- **It narrows the reading list; it does not do the reading.** Of 37 hits on the run that produced this, 12 died
  to a YAML-presence grep and the other 25 had to be read for actual owner-dependence.

### `A() ?? B` in a decision path — check that both arms answer the same QUESTION

Nothing type-checks that the two arms of a `??` are the same *kind*
of answer, nothing lints it, and the difference only surfaces on the maps or configs that populate the left
arm. **When you see `A() ?? B` on a decision path, ask whether A and B answer the same question; if they do
not, whatever flips which arm runs is silently a gameplay switch.**

The worked case. Ground evacuation used to resolve its search origin as
`FindClosestSpawnAreaForOwner(self) ?? self.Location` — left arm a property of the **owner** (the `spawnarea`
nearest that player's Supply Route, `RotateToEdge.FindClosestSpawnAreaForOwner`), right arm a property of the
**unit**. Only `river-zeta-ww3` authors `spawnarea` actors, so one shipped map ran the owner-anchored arm and
nine ran the unit-anchored one. Measured with [`tools/evac-edge-math/`](../../tools/evac-edge-math/README.md)
over a stride-2 grid of passable cells, for `tracked` units within 20 cells of an opponent spawn, on the pre-fix code: river-zeta exited through the owner's own back wall on **100.0%** of cells at a median
74.8-cell drive; the other nine did so on **14.4%** at a median 9.0 cells.

**That split no longer exists.** The fallback is `RotateToEdge.FriendlyEvacuationOrigin(self)`: nearest friendly `ProductionFromMapEdge` → `Owner.HomeLocation`
→ `self.Location`, so both arms are owner-side and the site says so in a comment. Carry the **shape**, not
the map split. Note the third fallback is load-bearing rather than belt-and-braces: a player occupying no
lobby slot has `HomeLocation == CPos.Zero` (the `PlayerReference.HomeLocation` default), and anchoring there would send every
such evacuation to the map's top-left corner — a coordinate default masquerading as "their own side".

**One deliberate exception, commented as a decision in `RotateToEdge.Tick`:** the blocked-path retry
re-picks with `ChooseClosestEdgeCell(self.Location)`, unit-anchored and unfiltered. It runs only after a
`MoveTo` has already ENDED with the unit four or more cells from any border, so re-anchoring on the Supply
Route would re-pick the wall that just failed and burn all three retries on it.

**Related and easy to conflate: `Map` has TWO edge choosers and they are not interchangeable.**
`Map.ChooseClosestMatchingEdgeCell` is an exact filtered argmin over the perimeter list;
`Map.ChooseClosestEdgeCell` is an unfiltered half-plane projection and can return a cell one
*past* `Bounds.Right`/`Bottom`, which are exclusive. In `RotateToEdge.ChooseEdgeCell` ground evac uses the first,
aircraft evac and the retry above use the second. They name a different **wall** for well under 1%
of cells, so "nearest wall by perpendicular distance" is a sound intuition — but the off-by-one is real and
anything comparing the two must normalise it. In the ground call the sort origin and the reachability
predicate also read **different actors' positions**: the key is `searchOrigin`, the predicate paths from
`self.Location` (`CanReach(self, mobileInfo, c)`).

### A value whose contract is "what the PLAYER asked for" must be STATED at the site holding the input, never read back out of the machinery that consumed it

Inference is safe only while the machinery is a pure carrier, and silently wrong the moment it applies a
**per-unit** transform — and it fails invisibly, because every unit's value is individually plausible.

The worked instance. `AttackMoveActivity.OriginalDestination` was derived by running the queued move
closure once at construction and reading the resulting `Move.Destination`. At the player-order site that
closure relocated through `Mobile.NearestMoveableCell` **first**, so the recorded value was the relocated
cell, not the click — and `NearestMoveableCell` is per-unit by construction (it tests
`CanEnterCell`/`CanStayInCell` against the unit's own locomotor and gates on `CanReach`, the unit's own
pathfinding domain). **Two different harms, and which one appears depends on the selection's shape, so
testing one reads as clean:**

- **Homogeneous selection** — `CanEnterCell` at `BlockedByActor.Immovable` ignores movable units, so every
  unit relocates to the *same* cell. The group consistently marches somewhere the player never clicked.
  Self-consistent and silent.
- **Heterogeneous selection** (the standard infantry + armour push) — the units disagree, a consumer that
  compares chains by `(Cell, OrderType)` finds no common suffix, and one click dedupes into N waypoints
  with the selection split between them.

**The cheapest available evidence is a same-shaped sibling that is correct.** Plain Move never had this and
the difference is one argument: `Mobile.MoveTo` takes `evaluateNearestMovableCell` and
`Mobile.ResolveOrder` passes it `true` in both its Move and ForceMove branches, so relocation happens later in `Move.OnFirstRun`
and the construction-time destination is still the click. Shift-G was therefore honest for Move and
dishonest for AttackMove, on the same screen, for the same click. Look for that sibling before arguing from
first principles. The fix is scoped by the same reasoning: of the `AttackMoveActivity` construction sites,
only the player-order one pre-relocated (`AttackMove.ResolveOrder`, with the reasoning carried as an
in-file PITFALL comment at that site); rally point, paradrop, resupply, Hunt, Patrol, AttackWander, Guard,
Reservable, aircraft and the Lua binding all pass a raw cell, so the inferring constructor is correct for
them and stays.

**Neither test suite reaches this class.** The harm is a divergence *across* a selection, held in an internal
activity field: NUnit has no `World`, and a Lua binding that exercises the behaviour cannot assert on the
field. Pin the two structural facts that together forbid it instead of trying to observe the divergence.

## `make.ps1 all` can silently NO-OP after a checkout, leaving the previous SHA's binaries

*(The observation is established; the cause below is a hypothesis and is marked as one.)*

**Observed 2026-09-04**, in a reused worktree: detached at one SHA, built, gate run; then
`git checkout --detach <other SHA>` and `.\make.ps1 all` again. It printed **"Build succeeded, 0 Warning(s),
0 Error(s)"** in **1.53 s** with **no per-project output lines at all**, and `engine/bin/OpenRA.Mods.Common.dll`
still carried its mtime from the previous build 1h45m earlier. The checkout had *added* a `.cs` file whose
mtime read older than the DLL, despite git having just written it. Touching the two changed sources and
re-running rebuilt in 5.87 s with the project lines back.

**Why it matters: a gate run on those binaries is a gate on the OLD commit while reporting the new one, and
nothing in the output says so** — "Build succeeded" is identical either way. This is strictly worse than the
missing-build case, which fails loudly (`launch-game.sh` gates on `OpenRA.dll` present and `VERSION` matching).

**Tells that a `make.ps1 all` did nothing:**
- elapsed under ~2 s **and** no `-> …dll` project lines in the output;
- `engine/bin/OpenRA.Mods.Common.dll` mtime older than the checkout you just did.

**Remedy before any gate or launch after switching SHAs in a shared or reused worktree:** `touch` the files
the checkout changed (`git diff --name-only <old> <new> -- '*.cs'`), or check the DLL mtime, before trusting
the build.

**Leading hypothesis, NOT verified:** git's index stat-cache restored a previously-seen file with a preserved
mtime rather than stamping it with the checkout time, so MSBuild's source-newer-than-output test failed and it
skipped the project. Confirming that means inspecting `core.trustctime` and the index stat data across that
specific checkout pair, which nobody has done.

## A build that fails and launches anyway indicts the WRONG code, every time

When a launcher runs the game after a failed build, the engine loads new YAML against a stale `engine/bin` and reports **the first trait it cannot resolve** — which is a trait added *after* the last successful build, i.e. whatever the reader edited most recently. The error is maximally misdirecting: it points at real, freshly-written code and says something false about it, and there is no route from the symptom back to the cause. **A `Cannot locate type: <recently added trait>` stack trace is a stale-binary tell first and a YAML bug second** — check that the build actually succeeded before reading it. The concrete incident cost a session to a 60-line trace naming a trait that was fine.

Three mechanisms in this repo made that reachable; all are now closed, and the reasoning is carried in-code at each site.

- **`find … -exec` is never a safe way to run something whose exit code matters — `find` exits 0 regardless of what the command it ran returned** (verified: `find . -maxdepth 0 -exec sh -c 'exit 7' \;` yields `$? = 0`). The `Makefile`'s `all` target used it to build the mod solution, so a failed build reported success to every consumer including `make test`'s `all` prerequisite. It is now `@set -e; for sln in $(MOD_SOLUTION_FILES); do …` in the `all` target, with the reason in a comment above it. **This never bit anyone only by luck** — `WW3MOD.sln` currently holds just `OpenRA.Game` and `OpenRA.Mods.Common`, both built earlier by the `engine:` prerequisite through a sub-`make` that *does* propagate, so a compile error always failed sooner. Add one mod-specific project to that solution and the hole reopens. `clean` uses the same `set -e` loop.
- **A launcher must test the build's exit code explicitly.** `launch-game.cmd` invokes `make.ps1 … all` and now gates on `if %errorlevel% neq 0 goto buildfailed`. Deliberately `neq 0` rather than `if errorlevel 1`: the latter is a signed `>= 1` test, so a `powershell.exe` dying on an access violation (`-1073741819`) would slip through it and launch on stale binaries.
- **"The muxer is on `PATH`" is not "the pinned SDK is installed".** `global.json` pins `6.0.428` with `rollForward: latestFeature`, which **cannot cross a major version**, so a machine carrying only 8.x/10.x resolves `dotnet` fine and still fails every project. Do not reimplement the `rollForward` matrix in a shell script — it drifts away from what `dotnet` really does. `dotnet --version` honours `global.json`, costs nothing, and fails with exactly the error a build would hit, which makes it an authoritative pre-flight that cannot rot. Both entry points ask it and nothing else: the `Makefile`'s `check-dotnet-sdk` (a prerequisite of `all`) and `make.ps1`'s `CheckForDotnetSdk` (called for `all`/`clean`/`check`). Running it before anything compiles reports the one real cause instead of burying it under a per-project wall of identical muxer errors.

**Generalising past this repo: any pipeline that *can* run the game after a failed build will eventually produce a misdirecting crash, and the person reading it has no way to know.** The cheap guard is at the launcher, not at the build.

## Shell scripts run under zsh — an unquoted `$var` is NOT word-split

The repo's scripts and the agent shell both run zsh, where `prog $args` passes the variable as **one** argument rather than splitting it on whitespace the way bash does. So the common bash idiom

```bash
for p in "--perturb 0" ""; do ./probe.sh $p; done     # zsh: passes ONE arg "--perturb 0"
```

hands `probe.sh` a single glued token that no flag parser matches. Use an array (`args=(--perturb 0); prog $args`) or `${=var}` to force splitting.

**Why this earns a place here rather than being generic shell trivia: the failure is silent and it inverts results.** The flag never parses, so the feature under test never activates, and the run completes normally — reporting IDENTICAL / PASS for a comparison that was never perturbed. It cost two false results in one day, including a self-test that passed *while believing it was perturbed*. **Prove the flag arrived (echo the received argv) before trusting a null difference or blaming a stale build.** Same family as the `| tail` verdict-swallowing trap in [`../recipes/AUTOTEST.md`](../recipes/AUTOTEST.md): the shell reports success for a thing that did not happen.

**`utility.sh` and `utility.cmd` both inject the mod id, and the whole value of that is that one documented command string works on both platforms — so do not add a mod id yourself.** `utility.sh`'s last line ends `… OpenRA.Utility.dll "${LAUNCH_MOD}" "$@"`, and `utility.cmd`'s argument-passing branch ends `call bin\OpenRA.Utility.exe %MOD_ID% %*`. Write `./utility.sh --regen-shadows PATH` and `.\utility.cmd --regen-shadows PATH`; **`.\utility.cmd ww3mod --regen-shadows PATH` is WRONG** — `OpenRA.Utility/Program.cs` skips argv[0] as the mod and reads argv[1] as the command name, so a second `ww3mod` raises `NoSuchCommandException("ww3mod")`.

> **The `.cmd` half is UNVERIFIED on Windows** — it was written and committed on macOS, where `.cmd` cannot run. The one command that settles it is `.\utility.cmd --build-fingerprint`: it should print a fingerprint hash and a `mod rules:  ww3mod …` line and return to the prompt. If instead you land in the interactive `Please enter a command:` loop, the flag was read as a mod name and the fix did not take.

Two `utility.cmd` traps that are fixed but worth carrying, because both are **failures wearing the costume of progress**, the same family as the exit-code traps above:
- **Bare `find` in the VERSION check.** With Git-for-Windows on PATH it resolved to GNU `find.exe`, errored, fell through to `:noengine` and sat on `pause` **forever at ~0% CPU** — indistinguishable from a slow computation; an agent waited ten minutes believing shadows were being generated. Now `%SystemRoot%\System32\find.exe` by absolute path.
- **`cmd.exe` parses redirection operators on `@REM` lines.** An angle bracket inside a batch comment is a live redirect, not documentation — a bracketed path in a comment on the argument-passing branch once did exactly that. Keep redirect, pipe and escape characters out of `.cmd` comments.

## An MSYS absolute path handed to a NATIVE Windows process resolves against the current drive

`REPO_ROOT=$(cd ... && pwd)` yields `/c/Users/...` under MSYS/Git
Bash. Hand that to anything that is not an MSYS program — `dotnet`, a native `python3`, any `.exe` — and the
leading `/` is resolved against the current drive instead, so the path silently does not exist. Two
instances sat in one script: a `python3 -c` sanity check that raised `FileNotFoundError` (reported), and a
`MOD_SEARCH_PATHS`/`ENGINE_DIR` pair handed to `dotnet OpenRA.Utility.dll` that made the mod search come up
short and wrote a **zero-byte** dump (not reported, and invisible because the first fault fired and took the
blame). **When a script fails on a path, check every path it hands out, not the one in the traceback.**

**The fix is relative paths, which is already the house form in this tree.** `utility.sh`,
`tools/impact-scar/extract-palettes.sh` and `engine/utility.sh` all pass `ENGINE_DIR=".."` with
`MOD_SEARCH_PATHS="../mods,./mods"` after cd-ing into `engine/`; relative paths are drive-agnostic, need no
`cygpath`, and behave identically under MSYS, native Windows, Linux and macOS. For the Python side the
equivalent move is to pipe the file in on **stdin** (`python3 -c '...' < "$FILE"`) so the SHELL opens it —
path translation stays where it already works, and the Python source can then be single-quoted, which
removes every escaping hazard as a side effect.

**And a zero exit from `OpenRA.Utility` does not mean the mod loaded.** With no env vars set,
`cd engine && dotnet bin/OpenRA.Utility.dll ww3mod --dump-balance-json` prints `The available mods are:` —
**empty** — and exits **0** with a usage line. That is a successful exit with no mod loaded, which is
exactly what a hand-check reads as "works". **Require the leading `{` before believing a dump**, the same
shape as the zero-byte-log and exit-126 traps recorded for `utility.sh` in `CLAUDE.md`.

**Related, and it turned a portability wart into data loss: a validator must never delete its subject.** The
failing check above carried `rm -f "${OUT_FILE}.tmp"` on its failure branch, so a broken CHECK destroyed a
good 454 KB DUMP and printed a message naming the wrong party. **That branch is reached both when the
artifact is bad AND when the validator is bad, and it cannot tell which** — so it may not destroy evidence,
and its message may not assert which side failed.

## Engine behaviors that surprise (debugging gotchas)

- **`Actor.Location` LEADS a moving unit by one cell, and unit speed does not widen the window.** `Actor.Location` is `OccupiesSpace.TopLeft` and for a `Mobile` actor `TopLeft => ToCell` (`Mobile.TopLeft`) — **the cell being moved INTO**. `ToCell` is assigned when a move BEGINS, not when it completes (`Mobile.IsLeavingCell` encodes the same semantics: `ToCell != location && FromCell == location`). So the reported cell runs one ahead of where the unit physically is, and **a Speed-25 rifleman's reported cell jumps exactly as fast as a tank's** — the intuition that a slow unit gives a poll a wide margin is backwards, because the first tick of the queued move is enough. Anything reading where a unit *arrived*, *spawned* or *stopped* by polling `Actor.Location` is reading its destination. Use a notification hook — for a Supply Route delivery that is `Trigger.OnProduction`, which fires from `INotifyProduction.UnitProduced` inside the same frame-end task, after `CreateActor` and after the MoveTo is queued but **before any activity has ticked**, so `ToCell` is still the spawn cell — or `CenterPosition` when an approximate position will do. **The independent tell that such a reading is an instrument fault rather than an engine one:** a reported "entry cell" that is not on the map perimeter cannot have come from `ChooseClosestMatchingEdgeCell` at all.

- **`INotifySold.Sold` means "this actor got home alive"; `Selling` is only the INTENT.** `Sold` fires from exactly two places — `RotateToEdge.DoSell`, reached by an evacuating actor that leaves alive (normally at the map edge; also in place when no edge cell exists or after three blocked retries), and the `Sell` activity for a building selling in place. `Sellable.Sell` raises `Selling`. So a unit ordered to evacuate and killed en route raises `Selling` and never `Sold`, and reading the wrong one turns *"recovered"* into *"tried to recover"*.

- **`Cargo.MaxWeight` is a weight BUDGET that the tooltip prints as a headcount.** `CargoInfo`'s `ProvideTooltipDescription` renders `$"{MaxWeight} infantry"`, which is only a passenger count while every passenger weighs 1 — true today because `Passenger.Weight` defaults to 1 (`PassengerInfo.Weight`) and is overridden nowhere in `mods/` (`git grep -nE '^\s+Weight:' mods/` is empty). **The first `Passenger.Weight: 2` authored in this mod silently makes every transport tooltip overstate its capacity, and nothing fails.** Found from the reverse error: `pbox`/`hbox` descriptions claimed "Garrisons 2 soldiers" against a `MaxWeight` of 4, so the hand-written figure and the generated row disagreed on screen.

- **`FindTilesInCircle` / `FindTilesInAnnulus` THROW above 56 cells rather than clamping, and nothing at the call site says so.** `Map.FindTilesInAnnulus` rejects any `maxRange` past `MapGrid.MaximumTileSearchRange` (ceiling **56**), so a radius over 56 cells takes the game down at the instant of the call. `FindTilesInCircle` reads like a search helper, its argument is a plain cell count, and there is no clamp, no guard and no `[Desc]` mentioning a maximum anywhere on the traits that reach it. It has caught at least **four** callers in this repo and every one was found by crashing rather than by reading: `Radar.Range: 56c0` via `AffectsMapLayer.ProjectedCells` (note `56c0` resolves to 57, so the effective ceiling is **55c0**), an autotest radar scenario, a support power's `CameraRange: 68c0`, and `TerrainLighting.AddLightSource`'s light radius. **Treat any WDist that reaches a `FindTiles*` call as capped at 55c0 until you have read the callee.**

  **When you hit the ceiling, first ask whether you needed the ordering at all.** The limit exists *only* because these methods return cells **sorted by distance**, by walking the precomputed `Grid.TilesByDistance` jagged array (`MapGrid.TilesByDistance`, built by `MapGrid.CreateTilesByDistance`) whose length **is** the ceiling. A caller that wants "every cell within r", unordered, wants a rectangular sweep over the bounding box with a squared-distance test: no ceiling, no enumerators, no precomputed table, and faster. `TerrainLighting.NotifyCells` is that sweep. Two subtleties it had to get right, both hidden by the annulus call: the containment guard must be **`map.Tiles.Contains(uv)`, not `map.Contains(cell)`** — the subscriber `TerrainSpriteLayer.UpdateTint` indexes its vertex array with no bounds check of its own, and `map.Contains` tests the narrower playable `Bounds`; and the radius must be **widened by 724** (the half-diagonal of a 1024 cell) because tints are sampled at cell CORNERS, so a cell whose centre is outside the light can still have a lit corner.

- **`Viewport.Scroll` is CUMULATIVE, which is why upstream's screen shake used periods of exactly 4 and 5 ticks.** `Scroll` is `CenterLocation += (1f / Zoom * delta).ToInt2()` (`Viewport.Scroll`) — a relative nudge, not an absolute placement. `ScreenShaker` called it once per tick with `sin(2π t/4)` and `cos(2π t/5)`, and **each of those series sums to exactly zero over its own period**, which is what returned the camera to where the player left it. The periods look like an arbitrary aesthetic constant and are a correctness constraint. **So "make the shake waveform less mechanical" is not a self-contained change:** any broadband or noise-driven waveform has a non-zero running sum and, applied as a per-tick delta, random-walks the camera away from wherever the player put it — slowly, invisibly, and only during combat. `.ToInt2()` truncates toward zero as well, so even the shipped sines drifted a fraction of a pixel per tick. **The fix worth carrying: track the ABSOLUTE offset you have applied and scroll by the difference** (`ScreenShaker.appliedOffset` in `engine/OpenRA.Game/Traits/World/ScreenShaker.cs`, its `ITick.Tick` emitting `target - appliedOffset`), so the camera lands on the exact pixel it started from whatever the waveform did in between. That needed `Viewport.ScrollPx(int2)` — an exact integer offset with no zoom conversion — because routing an integer through `Scroll`'s `1/Zoom` round-trip can lose the last unit and leave the camera permanently one pixel off. **Generalisable: before replacing a periodic function, check whether anything downstream depends on its ZERO SUM rather than on its shape.**

- **A `PerfSample` on a per-entity path costs ~15× the work it is measuring, and no gate can see it.** A `PerfSample` is two `Stopwatch.GetTimestamp()` calls plus a string-keyed `Cache` lookup in `PerfHistory.Increment`. Benchmarked at `net6.0`/Release against a faithful stand-in for `TerrainLighting.TintAt`'s no-light body: **3.62 ns** unsampled, **53.47 ns** wrapped, **4.73 ns** behind a static-bool gate that is off — i.e. **14.8×** the cost of the thing instrumented, paid whether or not anyone is reading the number, and invisible in `make.ps1 all`, in `dotnet test` and in every gate. **The generalisable part is the ratio, not the fix: `PerfSample` is only free on a path that runs once per tick or once per frame**, which most of the engine's other call sites do (`git grep -n 'new PerfSample(' engine/`) — `Locomotor.UpdateCellBlocking` (per cell) and `ModularBot`'s `INotifyDamage.Damaged` (per damage event) are the exceptions; the moment one lands on a per-sprite or per-entity path it dominates its own measurement. `PerfHistory.Sampling` (set once per frame in `Game.RenderTick`) exists for hot-path callers to branch on — and note **the third term of that disjunction**: it is `PerfGraph || PerfText || benchmark != null`, because gating on the two on-screen overlays alone would silently empty the CSV `--benchmark` writes (`Support/Benchmark.cs` walks every `PerfHistory.Items` entry with no reference to whether anything is displayed). **An instrumentation gate has to enumerate every reader, and the off-screen one is the easy one to miss.** Worked example in place at `TerrainLighting`'s `ITerrainLighting.TintAt`, which branches on `PerfHistory.Sampling` into `Sampled`.

- **`SpatiallyPartitioned.Add` throws on a zero-width bounding rectangle** (`SpatiallyPartitioned.ValidateBounds`, called by `Add` and `Update`), so an actor or effect whose bounds are built as `2 * range` from a radius of **zero** is a crash from inside the renderer with no YAML in the stack trace. Clamp radius-like fields at the validator *and* at any runtime interpolation, not only at load.

- **Holding a unit at `DamageState.Critical` means fighting a mechanism built to kill it, and EVERY chassis has one — in a different trait each time.**

  | chassis | what kills it | where |
  |---|---|---|
  | helicopter | `HeliEmergencyLanding` — unrecoverable crash descent, always destroyed on impact | `Traits/Air/HeliEmergencyLanding.cs` |
  | vehicle | `ChangesHealth@CriticalDamage` — `PercentageStep: -1`, `Delay: 5`, `StartIfBelow: 50` | `vehicles.yaml` |
  | infantry | `ChangesHealth@BleedOut`, same `StartIfBelow: 50` shape | `infantry.yaml` |

  Only **buildings** have no such drain (the comment on `^Building` in `structures.yaml` spells out why: `critical-damage` is a doom marker, not a damage tier, and the only `ChangesHealth` reaching a building is `@BurnDamage`, gated on an externally-granted `onfire`). A comment in `vehicles.yaml` (`^EffectsWhenDamagedVehicles`) states the design intent outright: *"A vehicle is doomed the moment it drops below 50% HP … with no way back."*

  **The number that matters for test authors: a vehicle parked at 20% of max dies 100 ticks later** (1% of max per 5 ticks, 20 → 0) — comfortably inside an ordinary measurement window. `test-aa-breakoff-critical` already had to delete `-HeliEmergencyLanding` for the helicopter form of this and its comment reads as a helicopter quirk; **it is not, it is universal.** The remedy that survives an unknown fourth drain is to **re-pin the health every tick** rather than only removing the trait you know about — `floor(20%)` never crosses the 25% Critical boundary, so the condition cannot flicker while the pin holds.

  **This is also a design fact, not just a test-rig annoyance:** break-off is declining targets that every chassis is already independently killing, so the ammunition it saves is genuinely wasted, and a doomed target's remaining lifetime is bounded and knowable in each case.

- **A diagonal cell step is produced by THREE independent code paths, and only one of them is the pathfinder — so a new movement rule placed in the graph is not in force.** (a) `DensePathGraph.GetConnections` → `GetPathCostToNode`, the multi-cell case; (b) **`PathFinder.FindPathToTargetCell`, the one that is easy to miss** — for `source.Layer == target.Layer && (source - target).LengthSquared < 3` it returns the two-cell path **without building a graph at all**, and `LengthSquared == 2` *is* a diagonal, so every single-step diagonal (exactly the order a player issues when clicking just past a gap) bypasses the graph; (c) `Mobile.GetAdjacentCell`, the "back up to let others pass" sidestep, which walks all 8 `CVec.Directions` under its own `CanEnterCell`/`CanStayInCell` tests. Any "you may not take this step" rule has to be taught to all three — the diagonal-squeeze rule is: the shortcut re-applies it through `Locomotor.MovementCostToEnterCell`, and `GetAdjacentCell` calls `Locomotor.IsDiagonalSqueeze` on both of its candidate lists.
  - **And a rule whose blockage is NOT in the destination cell will spin `Move` forever unless it is also taught to `PopPath`'s escape** (`Move.PopPath`). The blocked-handling block has exactly one path-clearing exit, and it was a plain `CanEnterCell` test — which returns *true* for a corner-squeeze, because the blockage lives in the two shoulder cells while the destination is ordinary open ground. The comment and the extra disjunct are at the site now. **General form: if your rule is a property of the *edge* rather than of the *cell*, every destination-only predicate in the movement stack is blind to it.**
  - **Both-shoulders vs either-shoulder is load-bearing, not a taste call.** `DirectedNeighbors` prunes each expansion to cells "not guaranteed to be reached more cheaply through our parent", a proof that assumes reachability is direction-independent. It survives a **both**-shoulders rule because for every neighbour the straight-incoming rows discard, the parent's diagonal to it has the currently-expanding (therefore passable) cell as one shoulder — so the parent's move can never be corner-denied. Under an **either**-shoulder rule that neighbour becomes deniable, the pruning silently drops reachable cells, and the search goes incomplete: paths vanish in tight terrain.
  - **Infantry are EXEMPT, deliberately — a solid wall corner does not stop them, and that is an accepted trade, not a bug.** `Locomotor.IsDiagonalSqueeze` returns early for `SharesCell` locomotors, so a subcell-capable actor still walks diagonally between two solid cells — including the two corners of a building, a cliff, or the outside corner of any structure. Full-cell occupants (every wheeled, tracked, walker, naval and landing-craft locomotor) are denied it. **Do not "fix" this** — it is a user decision of 2026-08-08, and the reasoning is in-code at the head of `IsDiagonalSqueeze`. The criterion is occupancy, not unit class: `SharesCell` is not a *proxy* for subcell capability, it **is** the switch (the `Mobile` constructor assigns `ToSubCell` straight from it), so the scoping cannot drift and a future cell-sharing unit is exempted with no list to edit. In this mod it partitions cleanly — `SharesCell: true` appears on exactly the four `foot*` locomotors and nowhere else (`git grep -n 'SharesCell' mods/`). It also had to be a *locomotor* property rather than a per-actor test: `DensePathGraph.DirectedNeighbors` prunes on direction-independent reachability, and a search runs against exactly one locomotor, so a locomotor-level exemption keeps the both-shoulders argument (above) intact for the whole search. Naval was flagged as the live edge and is a non-issue: those locomotors are vestigial RA carry-over and `rules/ingame/naval.yaml` has **zero** non-comment lines, so no ship exists to be blocked.
  - **Scope, as shipped: the rule is tank-traps-only.** It keys on a marker trait `BlocksDiagonalSqueeze` via `CellFlag.HasDiagonalSqueezeBlocker` (set in `Locomotor.UpdateCellBlocking`, read by `Locomotor.CellBlocksCorner`), carried by exactly `TANKTRAP1` and `TANKTRAP2` in `decoration.yaml` (`git grep -n 'BlocksDiagonalSqueeze' mods/`). Deliberately **not** on `^Rock` or `^Tree` — an earlier, broader version keyed on generic impassability and closed tree-corner pockets all over the mod.
- **`CanStayInCell` and `IsBlockedBy` are different predicates, and a unit can legally ARRIVE somewhere it may not REST — which is what "my tank backed up on its own" is.** `Locomotor.CanStayInCell` is purely `!CellFlag.HasTransitOnlyActor`: no terrain, no pass classes, no locomotor state, so it is locomotor-independent. The flag is set only in `Locomotor.UpdateCellBlocking`, from `actor.OccupiesSpace is Building b && b.TransitOnlyCells().Contains(cell)` — footprint cells written `+` (`FootprintCellType.OccupiedPassableTransitOnly`). `Locomotor.IsBlockedBy` explicitly returns *not blocked* for those same cells. When a unit falls idle on one, `Mobile.OnBecomingIdle` re-orders it off with `MoveTo(self.Location, evaluateNearestMovableCell: true)`, searching an annulus of radius 1–10 (the one-argument `Mobile.NearestMoveableCell` overload) — typically a one-cell shuffle that reads to the player as a click they never made.
  - **Exactly four actors in the whole mod declare a `+` cell**, so the trait can fire nowhere else: `SUPPLYROUTE` (`=+= +++ =+=`), `LOGISTICSCENTER` (`++ =+`), `AFLD` (`xxx xx+`), `FIX.Husk` (`husks-buildings.yaml`, same as the SR). Exhaustive — `git grep -n 'Footprint:' mods/ | grep -F '+'` lists every one. A unit wobbling in open ground with none of those nearby is something else (`Nudge`, via `Mobile.OnNotifyBlockingMove`).
  - **A plain move order cannot strand a unit — the obvious theory is wrong.** Every player order (the Move and ForceMove branches of `Mobile.ResolveOrder`), every rally move (`ProductionFromMapEdge.BuildWaypointActivity`) and `Move.OnFirstRun` route the destination through `NearestMoveableCell`, which filters on `CanStayInCell`; both give-up branches in `Move.PopPath` sit under a `CanStayInCell(mobile.ToCell)` guard and `Move.Cancel` clears the path only when it holds. **Production cannot either** — `ProductionFromMapEdge` spawns ground units on map-edge cells and never consults `Exit`. The SR's four *vehicle* exit cells (`Exit@Vehicle_1`–`4`) really are `+` while its four *infantry* exits (`Exit@Infantry_1`–`4`) are the `=` corners, but nothing is ever placed on them, so that asymmetry is real and inert.
  - **The one live route is service docking, and it is an override that removes a base-class safety filter.** `MoveAdjacentTo.CalculatePathToTarget` builds candidates through `CanStayInCell(cell) && CanEnterCell(cell)` (`MoveAdjacentTo.CalculatePathToTarget`), but `MoveOnto` **overrides that method** and substitutes a single unfiltered cell (`MoveOnto.CalculatePathToTarget`). `Resupply.Tick` and `DockHost.QueueMoveActivity` both reach it through `Mobile.MoveOntoTarget`. So a vehicle serviced at `LOGISTICSCENTER` — which carries `RepairsUnits` and `SupplyProvider` — would park where it may not stop, except that the Centre is 2x2 `++ =+` and declares a `ResupplyDock` naming its one stoppable `=` cell, so `Resupply` sends clients there and vacates them explicitly. The `MoveOnto` override still removes the filter; no shipped host strands a ground vehicle through it, but a new service host without such a dock would. `LocalMoveIntoTarget` is not on this route: it is constructed only by `Mobile.MoveIntoTarget`/`MoveIntoTargetRaw` and reached only from `Enter`; `Resupply` never calls it.
  - **Do NOT make `LOGISTICSCENTER`'s `+` cells stayable.** The Centre is 2x2 `++ =+`; its service cell is the one `=` cell, named by `ResupplyDock`, and `Resupply` vacates a finished client explicitly (painted in `AutomaticOrder.LineColor`, because the player did not order that move). `TransitOnlyServiceHostTest.ServiceHostDockCellsStayTransitOnlyBecauseTheVacateDependsOnIt` pins the `+` cells, and its failure text is the argument. The mechanism: the transit-only bounce looks purposeless — it does no contention check, `Actor.Tick` raises `OnBecomingIdle` strictly on the `!wasIdle && IsIdle` edge so nothing is queued behind the unit, and the LC carries no `Reservable` (unlike `HPAD` and `AFLD`). It is nonetheless load-bearing: **a host with no queue and no reservation stays free only if something moves the serviced unit off it.** Make a service cell stayable without a vacate and vehicle 1 parks there forever while vehicle 2 stalls at the door — `MoveOnto` returns `NoPath` and *waits* rather than sidestepping (`MoveOnto.CalculatePathToTarget`, comment: *"If we are close to the target but can't enter, we wait"*), `CanEnterCell` defaults to `BlockedByActor.All` so a squatter blocks absolutely, and there is no near-enough fallback: the ammo errand's close-enough is `WDist.Zero` (`AmmoPool`, since no actor in the mod carries `RearmsUnits`) and the repair errand's is `WDist(512)` (`Repairable`) — **both under one cell, so exact coincidence with the dock point is required.** That trades a cosmetic defect for a hard stall. The test's assertion is deliberately the inverse of the one first written there (in-code note above it) — a test encoding a rejected goal reads to the next person as settled policy. The principled alternative, if it ever earns the machinery, is to vacate on *reservation* rather than on idleness (`Reservable` + `DockHost` already exist).
- **Vehicles CANNOT crush or drive through trees or hedges in this mod — a belief that has come up more than once and is wrong.** Crushing here is not the engine `Crushable` trait (no actor uses it); it is modelled through `Passable`, which carries `CrushedByRelationships` (`PassableInfo.CrushedByRelationships`). `SBAG`/`FENC`/`BARB` set their pass class **plus `CrushedByRelationships: Enemy`**, and the heavy vehicle locomotors list those classes in `Crushes` — so tanks really do crush sandbag, wire and barbed wire, and only an **enemy's**. But `^Tree` sets `PassClasses: tree` with **no `CrushedByRelationships` at all** (its `Passable:` block in `decoration.yaml`), and `tree` appears in no vehicle locomotor's `Passes` or `Crushes` list — only the four `foot*` locomotors pass it. Trees are hard blocks to vehicles and walk-through cover for infantry.
- **A locomotor's `Crushes` classes ARE pass classes — `PassableClasses` is the UNION, so a crush-only class is not dead configuration.** `LocomotorInfo.RulesetLoaded` sets `PassableClasses = Passes.Union(Crushes)`, and that union is what `Locomotor.IsBlockedBy` hands to `passable.PassableBy(...)` when deciding enterability; `Passable.PassableInner` just tests `Info.PassClasses.Overlaps(passClasses)`. Crushing itself happens **after** entry, in `Passable.OnBeingPassed`, which re-tests `passerMobile.Info.LocomotorInfo.Crushes.Overlaps(...)` before firing `OnBeingCrushed`. So listing a class under `Crushes` alone both lets the unit in **and** crushes the occupant — `Passes` is the "walk over it, leave it alone" half, `Crushes` is "walk over it and kill it", and neither is a prerequisite for the other. **Do not read `IsBlockedBy`'s `Info.PassableClasses` as "the `Passes` field"** — it is a derived union computed at ruleset load, one indirection away from the YAML key it is named after, which is exactly the README §Four-shapes trap of citing a real line that does not do the work you think it does. A crush-only class such as `Crushes: fence` is therefore live configuration — it does not make placed fence/wire actors solid walls to the vehicles that list it.
- **Pausing `Mobile` is the engine's "halt this order and resume it later" primitive — it does NOT end the activity.** `Move.Tick` returns `false` while `mobile.IsTraitPaused`, so granting a `Mobile.PauseOnCondition` condition stops a unit *mid-order* and revoking it resumes the same activity with no re-issue and no path re-plan. `MobileInfo` is a `PausableConditionalTraitInfo`, so this is available on any actor. Cancelling and re-queuing is the obvious alternative and is strictly worse — the interrupted order is exactly the thing that has to survive. Note `IsCanceling` is tested *before* the pause check in `Move.Tick`, so a paused unit can still be cancelled; a break-off does not have to un-pause first.
  - **The MiniYaml half, and it generalises past this trait: a condition EXPRESSION is a single field, so restating it on a child REPLACES the inherited one rather than adding to it.** The trait merges per-field; the boolean expression inside the field does not merge at all. `^Vehicle` carries `PauseOnCondition: !(!empdisable && !being-captured)` (`^Vehicle`'s `Mobile` in `vehicles.yaml`), so a bare `PauseOnCondition: my-condition` on a derived actor would silently let an EMP'd or mid-capture unit drive away. The inherited terms must be restated — which `TRUK` does (`vehicles.yaml`, with the reason recorded in-file in the comment directly above its `PauseOnCondition`). Applies to every single-field expression, not just this one.
- **THREE weapon fields are globally inert, and all three are set in shipped YAML — so a tuning pass on any of them measures nothing.** `Bullet.InaccuracyPerProjectile` is unreachable: its branch in the `Bullet` constructor requires `lastPosIsSet`, which is declared `readonly bool lastPosIsSet = false` and **never assigned** anywhere — `git grep -n lastPosIsSet engine/` returns exactly those two hits — so the `else` branch always runs and every round in a burst scatters independently instead of walking. Shipped weapons still set it — `git grep -n InaccuracyPerProjectile mods/` lists them (six at the time of writing). `Armament.MovementInaccuracy` never applies to aircraft, because the lookup is `delayedTarget.Actor?.TraitOrDefault<Mobile>()` (in `Armament.FireBarrel`) and no airframe carries `Mobile` — the aircraft files declare `Aircraft` instead (zero `Mobile:` across all three `aircraft*.yaml`). And **`DamageAtMaxRange` does nothing on a `SpreadDamage` warhead**: `RangeDamageMultiplier` is defined on `DamageWarhead` but has exactly one caller engine-wide, `TargetDamageWarhead.DoImpact`; `SpreadDamageWarhead` never calls it, so its damage is flat with range. Four SpreadDamage warheads set it (all in `weapons-ballistics.yaml`; recount with `git grep -n DamageAtMaxRange mods/` and check each hit's enclosing `Warhead@`). **On an `Explodes`-delivered payload the field is worse than inert even for `TargetDamage`** — it divides by a `Weapon.Range` of zero; see [`missiles.md` §10](missiles.md). **Before tuning any weapon field, confirm the branch that reads it is reachable for the projectile/warhead class you care about** — and see [§A change believed made, documented as made, and inert](#a-change-believed-made-documented-as-made-and-inert) for how to compute that cheaply.
- **Bullets do not collide en route, so a non-bouncing round only ever detonates at the end of its flight path.** Bullets *do* lead their target — the lead is applied in `Armament`, not `Bullet`; see §"Bullets DO lead a moving target" above for the mechanism and for what is actually wrong with it. `target = args.PassiveTarget` (`Bullet`'s constructor) is a fixed point, but it is the *already-led* point, and `args.TargetingVector` is the `FirstBurst`/`FollowingBurstTargetOffset` walk pattern, not a lead. The per-tick `AnyValidTargetsInRadius` check in `Bullet.Tick` is gated on `remainingBounces < info.BounceCount`, and `BulletInfo.BounceCount` defaults to 0 with `remainingBounces = info.BounceCount` set at launch — so at default the predicate is `0 < 0`, false from the first tick, and a non-bouncing bullet only ever detonates at the end of its flight path. A Littlebird at its 265 u/tick cruise crosses ~5 cells during a 30mm round's 20-tick flight to `18c0`, against a `Circle Radius 32` hitshape — **but that displacement is what the lead solves for, so it is the size of the problem, not the size of the miss.** The residual miss is the single-iteration solve's error, which is largest against a *closing* target and exactly zero inside one tick of flight.
- **`traitreport.log` counts trait interface QUERIES, not implementors — never read it as a census.** The tell is on its own first line: `IAirborneVisibility: 723287`, and no mod has 723k traits. A line reading `INotifyPlayerDisconnected: 5` means five *lookups* (one world actor + four players, from the `TraitsImplementing<INotifyPlayerDisconnected>()` caches built in the `World` and `Player` constructors), not five implementing traits. **To find out whether a hook is wired up, grep the interface name** — the report cannot answer it. (`INotifyPlayerDisconnected` does have an implementor — `ConcedeOnDisconnect` — and whether any hook has one changes as code lands, which is the reason to grep rather than trust either the report or this sentence.)
- **A predicate about an ACTOR and a predicate about the CELL UNDER IT are not interchangeable — substituting the cheaper one silently deletes every channel of knowledge that is not line-of-sight.** `MapLayers` keeps radar in its own binary counter, `radarCount` (`MapLayers.cs`; written only in `AddSource`/`RemoveSource`, read only via `RadarCover`), which contributes **nothing** to `ResolvedVisibility` — vision writes graded strengths, radar writes a separate yes/no, and the resolution loop (`MapLayers`' `ITick.Tick`) reads `explored` and `visibilityCount` only. `Detectable.IsVisibleInner` ORs the two (via `IsRadarDetectedBy`), so a radar contact draws on screen. But anything asking a **cell** question — `MapLayers.IsVisible`, `IsDetectable`, `World.FogObscures(pos)` — consults `ResolvedVisibility` alone and answers "fogged" for every radar contact that has ever existed. `UnitOrderGenerator.TargetForInput` required `!FogObscures(CenterPosition)` *in addition to* `CanBeViewedByPlayer`, so a helicopter the player could plainly see was dropped from the mouse-target list and the right-click fell through to `Target.FromCell` — a Move order, no attack cursor.
  - **Two channels have now been lost this way** — `FrozenUnderFog` buildings (`22a1ec34`, 2026-05-04) and radar contacts — and both surfaced as user-visible bugs rather than by anyone reading the code. Both came from `8db9da9e` (2026-04-16), which added the cell check as defence-in-depth against a through-fog targeting bug whose root cause it says outright it never found (*"the exact edge case is elusive"*). **An unreproduced guard that overrides an authoritative predicate will keep doing this.** The rule is now hoisted out of four drifted copies into one predicate, `MouseTargetVisibility.IsRevealed` (`actorIsVisible && (isFrozenUnderFog || positionIsUnfogged || isRadarDetected)`) — note the guard was **consolidated, not deleted**. If a third channel turns up, that is the signal to go find `8db9da9e`'s original bug and delete the guard rather than add a fourth exemption.
  - **Corollary that disqualifies the three obvious test assertions.** `Test.IsDetectedBy` reads `CanBeViewedByPlayer`, which is **true on the broken build** — that disagreement *is* the bug. A Lua `Attack` order gates on the same predicate (`CombatProperties.Attack`). And `Test.ClickOrder` builds `Target.FromActor` itself, so it begins one step *after* `TargetForInput`, the method containing the defect. All three go green against it. Use `Test.IsMouseTargetable`, which calls the shipped predicate object directly.
- **If you model an intention as an anonymous increment on a shared counter, you have not built a reservation — you have built a debt.** Overkill prevention claims a share of a target's health before firing so other units see it as spoken for. It was applied as a bare `+=` on one shared int (`Actor.MarkForDestruction` onto `Actor.AverageDamagePercent`) — **no owner, therefore no way back**. The only downward force was the periodic halving in `Actor.Tick`, a *cleanup* mechanism doing a *release* mechanism's job, so commitments accumulated and the tally described history rather than the present: a target under sustained attention read as permanently over-committed, `ChooseTarget` hard-skipped it (its `OverkillThreshold` test), and an AA battery engaged one unit at a time. (A manual order still fired, because `AttackBase.AttackTarget` never consults `ChooseTarget` — which is what made it present as "my AA won't autotarget until I click".) Giving the claim an owner (`Actor.ClaimForAttack`, `engine/OpenRA.Game/OverkillClaim.cs`) yields the release *and* the "one claimant is one claim" invariant for free — the second was never separately designed. Two placement traps worth keeping: **release belongs to the trigger pull, not the outcome** (a shot that misses, expires short or is intercepted never impacts, and the projectile carries no back-reference — so the release (`self.ReleaseAttackClaim()`) sits in the `ScheduleDelayedAction` callback in `Armament.FireBarrel`, deliberately *above* the target re-validation return); and **the decay runs underneath the claim, so release must be clamped** (`OverkillClaimMath.Release` in `OverkillClaim.cs`) or a claim held across two halvings returns −75 and poisons every later comparison. Pinned in `engine/OpenRA.Test/OverkillClaimTest.cs`.
- **Every enabled `Explodes` fires on the same death; there is no arbitration.** `Explodes` is a `ConditionalTrait<ExplodesInfo>` implementing `INotifyKilled`, and `Killed` unconditionally impacts its own weapon, consulting no other instance on the actor. **So a set of `Explodes@Foo` traits whose `RequiresCondition` expressions overlap detonates once per overlapping trait, and a set with a gap kills the actor silently.** Neither is visible to an autotest, which sees the unit die either way — the partition has to be pinned in a unit test (`VolatileLoadBandTest.cs` walks every load value and asserts exactly one band matches). What decides whether gating is needed is **whether the incumbent weapon carries a damage warhead at all**: `BuildingExplode`, inherited by every structure from `^BasicBuilding` (`structures.yaml`), is `CreateEffect` + `LeaveSmudge` only (`weapons-explosions.yaml`) and stacks harmlessly, while `VehicleCookoffLarge` deals 14000 and does not — which is why `grad`/`m270`'s `Explodes@CrewCookoff` had to be gated to `!ammo-primary` (their `Explodes@CrewCookoff` blocks in `vehicles-russia.yaml` / `vehicles-america.yaml`).
- **A `ShockwaveDamage` warhead has TWO independent radii, and the one named `MaxRadius` is the cosmetic one.** `MaxRadius` is the wave's **travel** limit — `ShockwaveEffect.Tick` expands the front by `1024 / WaveSpeed` per tick once it is sonic (faster while still supersonic) and ends the effect once `currentRadius > MaxRadius`. Rendering stops at `warhead.VisualRadius` (`ShockwaveEffect.RenderAnnotation`), which is `ShockwaveVisualRadius` when set and `MaxRadius` otherwise (`ShockwaveDamageWarhead.VisualRadius`; validated non-negative and ≤ `MaxRadius` in its `RulesetLoaded`). **The consequence is the tuning trap:** `progress` is measured against `visualRadius` in `RenderAnnotation`, so **shrinking a ring shortens its visible LIFETIME as well as its size** — the two are not independent, and an A/B capture at a fixed frame will read the change as a brightness difference. The **damage** limit is `(Falloff.Length - 1) × Spread` (when no explicit `Range` list is given): `RulesetLoaded` builds `effectiveRange = MakeArray(Falloff.Length, i => i * Spread)` without ever consulting `MaxRadius`, and `ApplyBlastDamage` **returns outright** past the last step rather than clamping to the final `Falloff` entry — so damage stops dead with no tail. Effective damage radius is `min(MaxRadius, (Falloff.Length - 1) × Spread)`. **Neither term is validated against the other at load, so the smaller one wins silently** — widening `MaxRadius` alone yields a wave that travels further and hurts nothing past the old table, with no error and no lint. `Atomic` happens to be consistent (16 entries at `Spread: 1c0` against `MaxRadius: 15c0`), which is why this has never bitten; `AtomicHighYield` lands `34 × 3072 = 104448 = 102c0` on both sides deliberately. And because the falloff LERPs, a wide blast does **not** need one entry per cell — 35 entries at `Spread: 3c0` is the same curve as 103 at `1c0`, and legible on one line. **That is what lets a shockwave sweep four cells for spectacle while only hurting things within one**, a shape not otherwise reachable by lowering `Damage`, which scales the whole curve uniformly and leaves the long tail intact. Two calibration traps: `GetDamageFalloff` LERPs between adjacent steps (`int2.Lerp`) so the curve is piecewise linear and a coarse `Spread` cannot express a sharp cutoff (tighten `Spread` to sharpen the knee); and the default `DamageCalculationType.HitShape` measures from the victim's hitshape **edge** (`HitShape.DistanceFromEdge` in `ApplyBlastDamage`), not its centre, so a unit "one cell away" takes slightly more than centre-to-centre arithmetic predicts. The ring's drawn thickness is also not `ShockwaveThickness` directly — `RenderAnnotation` scales it by `min(1, progress * (ShockwaveThicknessRampPercent / 100))` (`ShockwaveThicknessRampPercent` defaults to `250`), reaching the configured value at 40% of the **visual** radius, so a thickness near its own `MaxRadius` renders as a filled disc for most of the wave's life; `IskanderExplosion`'s 1024-on-4096 is the 1:4 reference to match.
  - **"Make it fade out at the end" is a statement about terminal SLOPE, not about the end value — and reaching for the end-alpha constant is the wrong lever.** `ShockwaveEndAlphaPercent` already defaults to **0**, so the rings already reach fully transparent and lowering it gains nothing. What controls whether the fade is *perceived* is the exponent: `FadeOutAt` is `endAlpha + (1 - endAlpha) * (1 - progress^k)` with `k = ShockwaveFadeOutExponentPercent / 100` (default **200**). **`1 - p^k` with `k > 1` holds alpha high and collapses late; `(1 - p)^k` would fade early.** A ring tuned "invisible" is almost always a ramp-shape change, not an end-value change. The full rationale is written as the `[Desc]` on `ShockwaveFadeOutExponentPercent` in `ShockwaveDamageWarhead.cs` — read it there rather than restating it — and the default curve is NUnit-pinned against regression by `ShockwaveTuningTest.TheDefaultCurveNeverDarkensAnyFrameAgainstTheOldLinearRamp`. No shipped weapon sets the field — every shockwave takes the default, and the eight volatile-load explosion bands (`weapons-explosions.yaml`) and `TosRockets` (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`) record that choice in-file; recount with `git grep -n ShockwaveFadeOutExponentPercent mods/`.
- **`AttackTurreted.CanAttack` short-circuits before `base.CanAttack`**: it returns `turretReady && base.CanAttack(...)`, and `turretReady` is false while the turret is still rotating onto the target. A trace or breakpoint in `AttackBase.CanAttack` therefore never fires until the turret has finished aiming. When debugging "why won't this unit fire?", check the turret is pointed at the target first — the answer is often just "it hasn't finished turning."
- **`Activity.IsCanceling` is always false inside `OnLastRun`**: `Activity.TickOuter` sets `State = Done` *before* calling `OnLastRun`, and `IsCanceling` is `State == Canceling`. So `OnLastRun` cannot distinguish "ended naturally" from "was cancelled" — the flag is already cleared. Use a different signal: a queued `NextActivity` implies you were replaced, or compare `attack.RequestedTarget` to your own target field.
- **Cell distance (`CVec.Length`, `CPos` subtraction) is Euclidean, not Chebyshev**: `CVec.Length => Exts.ISqrt(X*X + Y*Y)` — rounded straight-line distance, so a diagonal reads ~1.4× farther than the grid distance a player/watcher sees on the minimap. For true chessboard "cells away" gates use `max(|dx|, |dy|)` computed by hand, not `.Length`.
- **A bot `Order` string must match a `ResolveOrder` case, not an activity class name.** `Cargo.ResolveOrder` handles `"Unload"`, `"UnloadCargoPassenger"` and the two eject-rally orders (`SetEjectRally`/`ClearEjectRally`); `"UnloadCargo"` is the *activity* class name (`Activities/UnloadCargo.cs`, queued internally by `Cargo`) and matches no order — issuing `new Order("UnloadCargo", …)` is **silently dropped**. Also, even a correct unqueued `"Unload"` is dropped when `!CanUnload()` (no free adjacent cell on arrival), so a one-shot issue can permanently stall; re-issue when a cell frees. When wiring a bot to drive a unit trait, read the trait's `ResolveOrder` for the exact accepted strings.
- **`UnitDefaultsManager` moves a human-owned unit's stance off the YAML default — by ORDER, not in `Created`.** Applying per-type persisted defaults in `AutoTarget.Created` would read a per-machine file in code that runs on every client for every actor and diverge synced state — see [architecture.md §A per-machine settings file is a desync source](architecture.md). The persisted file is `Platform.SupportDir/ww3mod/unit-defaults.yaml` (`UnitDefaultsManager.cs`) and a human's saved per-type preference overrides the YAML default, so the test-authoring warning holds: a deterministic human-owned test must strip the bare-key `UnitDefaultsManager` World trait (`world.yaml`) or a locally-saved default silently changes behaviour. It lands as an order resolved after world load rather than a field write during construction, so a test sampling stance at tick 0 and one sampling a few ticks later can now disagree. `AutoTarget.Created` is inert on this axis and carries a PITFALL saying so.
- **The `*AI` stance fields are selected by `!Owner.Playable`, not by `IsBot` — so a SCENARIO OPPONENT silently ignores `InitialStance`.** `AutoTarget`'s constructor picks the field with `self.Owner.IsBot || !self.Owner.Playable ? info.InitialStanceAI : info.InitialStance`, and the same disjunction governs `InitialEngagementStance`, `InitialCohesion` and `InitialResupplyBehavior`. A scenario's enemy `PlayerReference` is normally declared *without* `Playable: True` — only the human side gets it — so **every scenario opponent takes the `*AI` field even though no bot is attached**. Writing `AutoTarget: InitialStance: HoldFire` on such an actor sets a field the engine never reads, and the unit keeps the `InitialStanceAI` default (`FireAtWill`). There is no lint error, no warning and no log line. **Set both fields unless you specifically want the human/AI asymmetry.** Combined with the `UnitDefaultsManager` rule above: a unit's starting stance comes from a different source depending on who owns it, and on neither path is it necessarily the YAML field you wrote — a deterministic scenario needs the World-trait strip *and* both fields.
- **Actor types in map YAML must be the lowercased ruleset key, or the density pass throws.** `Map.SetDensityLayer` looks the actor up in `Rules.Actors` with the raw map string (`actorReference.Type`) — and `Rules.Actors` is keyed lowercase, so an uppercased placement like `Truck0: TRUK` throws `KeyNotFoundException: 'TRUK'` at map load. Use `truk`. Normal actor spawning is case-insensitive and hides this, so the same map can spawn actors fine yet crash the density pass — always lowercase actor types in map placements. The density pass runs on the first load of **every** map on every machine, and again after any map or density-rules edit — no map is exempt (see [architecture.md §The LOS cache](architecture.md)).
- **A multi-weapon unit closes to its SHORTEST weapon's range, not its longest — and the activity was inconsistent with itself about it.** `Attack.TickAttack` computed `maxRange = armaments.Min(a => a.MaxRange())` — the shortest reach among every armament valid against the target — so a Tunguska ordered at a helicopter 24 cells out read as *out of range* and drove forward until its 30mm gun (`18c0`) could fire, with all eight 9M311 missiles (`28c0`) loaded. **This looks exactly like a condition lockout and is not** — see [§A change believed made…](#a-change-believed-made-documented-as-made-and-inert) instance 3, the inert `firing-primary` condition on that very armament. Meanwhile `Attack`'s two `lastVisibleMaximumRange` assignments computed it through `GetMaximumRangeVersusTarget`, which takes the **maximum** and skips paused armaments — so the engage path and the range-memory path disagreed. Now opt-in via `AttackBaseInfo.EngageAtLongestArmamentRange` (default `false` so nothing else in the mod moves), routed through the pure `AttackBase.EngagementMaxRange` and pinned by `EngagementMaxRangeTest`. **The longest branch must skip PAUSED armaments, with a fallback when all are paused** (as `EngagementMaxRange` does): a dry armament is *paused*, never disabled, so `ChooseArmamentsForTarget` keeps returning it, and a naive `Max` would strand a Tunguska that had spent its missiles at `28c0` — out of its own gun's reach, firing nothing.
- **`AutoTarget` never re-arms its scan timer on a tick an `IOverrideAutoTarget` answers, so an always-answering override is consulted EVERY idle tick.** `AutoTarget.ScanForTarget` gates on `ignoreScanInterval || nextScanTime <= 0`, consults each override, and **returns from inside that loop**; `nextScanTime` is re-armed only by the `SharedRandom.Next(MinimumScanTimeInterval, MaximumScanTimeInterval)` after the loop, on the ordinary `ChooseTarget` path an answering override never reaches. The code says the omission is deliberate (comment inside the override loop, to keep the `SharedRandom` draw pattern unchanged) but does not state the consequence: once `nextScanTime` first reaches 0, an unconditionally-answering override pins it at 0 forever and `MinimumScanTimeInterval`/`MaximumScanTimeInterval` (16/32 on `^CamoSoldier` in `infantry.yaml`) stop bounding anything for that actor. **Any rate-limiting an override relies on must live inside the override itself** — `HealerAutoTarget` happens to carry its own `ScanInterval` of 8, but nothing in `AutoTarget` was providing that bound, and an override written to lean on the AutoTarget interval would silently run 16–32× more often than intended.
- **When a periodic re-selection feeds a mover, the selection needs hysteresis LARGER than the per-step change the action itself causes — or the action inverts its own ranking.** `^MEDI`'s `Heal` warhead is `DamagePercent: -10` (`weapons-other.yaml`), so each pulse lifts its patient ten percentage points. With no preference for the incumbent, `HealerAutoTarget` re-running a full `FindBestTarget` every 8 ticks hands the case over the moment treating the worse of two comparable patients moves him *past* the other one. **The gap does not need to be a tie: any two patients within one pulse of each other oscillate deterministically.** Because `AutoFollowAlly` walks the medic to whichever patient is currently named, and crossing the ground between two patients takes far longer than the 50-tick heal cadence (`BurstWait: 50` on `Heal` — this mod paces fire with `BurstWait`, not `ReloadDelay`, which `Armament` makes mandatory by throwing `Weapons must define BurstWait` without it), the medic delivers roughly one pulse per crossing and finishes nobody. The fix is to score the incumbent with a `SwitchMargin` (`HealerAutoTarget`, subtracted when `patient == currentTarget`) rather than to lengthen the interval — **and the margin must EXCEED one pulse.** `^MEDI` sets `SwitchMargin: 10`, which now only *equals* the 10-point pulse, and the PITFALL above it in `infantry.yaml` still reasons from a pulse of 5; both are filed in `WORKSPACE/bugs/discovered.md`.
- **`World.SharedRandom` is the synced gameplay RNG — never draw from it to stagger a net-new always-on trait.** `SharedRandom` (a `World` field seeded from the lobby `RandomSeed` in the `World` constructor) is the stream folded into the sync hash (`World.SyncHash` adds `SharedRandom.Last`), so every `.Next(...)` advances it for **all** clients/profiles. A trait that ticks or loads for everyone (not just one gated profile) and calls `world.SharedRandom.Next(...)` in `WorldLoaded`/`Tick` to pick a per-instance stagger therefore shifts the stream for control/benchmark games too — silently breaking replay byte-identity versus any earlier baseline that lacked the trait, even when the trait is otherwise behaviour-inert. Derive a per-instance offset **deterministically** instead (a fixed constant, or a hash of a stable synced identity). Drawing from `SharedRandom` at load is only safe for a trait that is itself part of the baseline being measured. (Contrast `LocalRandom`, which is *not* in the sync hash — see architecture.md "Bot decisions ARE seed-reproducible".)
- **`AutoTarget.ScanForTarget` returns `Target.Invalid` for TWO indistinguishable reasons — "found nothing" AND "scan interval not elapsed".** `ScanForTarget` returns `Invalid` both when a scan ran and found no target, and when `nextScanTime > 0` (and `ignoreScanInterval` is false) so no scan ran at all — the gate is its opening `if`. A scan that *does* run re-arms `nextScanTime = SharedRandom.Next(MinimumScanTimeInterval, MaximumScanTimeInterval)`, decremented one per tick in `AutoTarget`'s `ITick.Tick` — so a unit only actually scans on ~1 of every N idle ticks. Any caller that treats every `Invalid` as "target lost" therefore wipes its per-target state ~N−1 of every N ticks. To tell the cases apart, capture `scannedThisTick = nextScanTime <= 0` **before** the call and, on a gated off-scan `Invalid`, reuse the cached target (re-checking alive + `CanBeViewedByPlayer`) — exactly what `AmbushTickIdle` does (it captures `scannedThisTick` before scanning). This conflation was the concrete bug that kept the Stage-3 ambush cadence counters from ever reaching their sample threshold (see architecture.md §Widened ambush).
- **The logging thread is the engine's only long-lived FOREGROUND thread — keep `IsBackground = true` on it or the process never exits.** Every other long-lived engine thread (Server/Connection/MapCache/graphics) sets `IsBackground = true`; `Log.DoWork` spins until its cancellation token fires, and the token is cancelled **only** in `Log.Dispose()` (cancel + `Thread.Join`), reached via the `finally { Log.Dispose(); }` in each entrypoint (`OpenRA.WindowsLauncher/Program.cs`, `OpenRA.Launcher/Program.cs`). A .NET foreground thread keeps the process alive after `Main` returns, so any exit that bypasses that finally (a signal kill mid-shutdown, or a main-thread hang between `Game.Exit` and the finally) leaves the thread spinning and **pins the process** — the mechanism behind lingering `dotnet.exe`/`OpenRA.exe` after the game closes. WW3MOD sets `IsBackground = true` on the Log thread (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Support/Log.cs`, PITFALL comment inline at the `new Thread(DoWork)`) so the runtime reaps the process on every exit path; clean exits still `Log.Dispose()` (cancel + join + final flush), so no log lines are lost.
- **`world.Actors` includes positionless PlayerActors — enumerate-then-read-position NREs on them.** `world.Actors => actors.Values` yields every `PlayerActor` and the world actor, not just spatial units — each `Player` adds and `Initialize`s a `PlayerActor` into the same dict (`Player.cs`), and a `PlayerActor` has no `IOccupySpace`. both `Actor.CenterPosition => OccupiesSpace.CenterPosition` and `Actor.Location => OccupiesSpace.TopLeft` dereference `OccupiesSpace`, so reading `.CenterPosition` **or `.Location`** on one throws `NullReferenceException`. A relationship filter does **not** exclude it: an enemy `PlayerActor`'s `Owner` is *itself*, so `RelationshipWith(Owner) == Enemy` passes it through — the bug that crashed a bot game to desktop on tick 0 when a HelicopterSquad's `FindClosestEnemy` fell through to the `world.Actors` scan before the ThreatMap had data (`HelicopterStates.cs`, guarded in `FindClosestEnemy` with `a.OccupiesSpace != null`). Any bot code that enumerates `world.Actors` (rather than a spatial query) and later reads a position must filter `a.OccupiesSpace != null` — and because a `FirstOrDefault`/`Where` predicate evaluates left-to-right, the **field-access order matters**: put an occupancy-implying trait test (`HasTraitInfo<…Info>()`, e.g. a hut / `IPositionableInfo` filter) or the `OccupiesSpace != null` guard **before** any `a.Location`/`.CenterPosition` term, or the scan NREs the moment it reaches a positionless actor (re-occurred in `EngineerRouteOpenBotModule.FindBridgeHutAt`, a `Location`-first predicate). Spatial-partition queries (`World.FindActorsInCircle`) are safe by construction — they only ever hold actors that occupy space.
- **`Actor.Trait<T>()` THROWS when the trait is absent, so a null check downstream of it is dead code — and the trait that "cannot be missing" is enforced by `Requires<>`, which is not the same guarantee.** `Trait<T>()` is `World.TraitDict.Get<T>(this)`, and `TraitDictionary.Get` throws `InvalidOperationException: Actor <name> does not have trait of type …` (via `TraitContainer<T>.Get`). The non-throwing accessor is `TraitOrDefault<T>()`. **The shape to watch for is a constructor doing `x = self.Trait<T>()` with `if (x == null)` guards further down the file** — the guards read as defensive and can never fire, so the actual failure mode is a load-time crash, not the degraded behaviour the guards imply. Worked example: `GarrisonProtectionInfo` declares `Requires<GarrisonManagerInfo>` and `Requires<CargoInfo>` but **no `Requires<HealthInfo>`**, so an actor that strips `-Health:` while keeping the garrison stack would throw under `self.Trait<IHealth>()` at construction — which is why `GarrisonProtection.Created` reads `self.TraitOrDefault<IHealth>()` and keeps its `health == null` guards (the in-file comment records why). No shipped actor reaches that path today; `V19.Husk` strips the garrison stack along with `Health`. **`Requires<>` is all the lint can see**: it is what makes an absent trait a load error instead of a runtime one, and a trait you dereference unconditionally belongs in that list.
- **A blocked activity TELLS the blockers to move, so a ring of your own idle units blocks nothing.** `ActorExts.NotifyBlocker` (`engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/ActorExts.cs`) fans a blockage out to every `INotifyBlockingMove` on every blocking actor, and `Mobile.OnNotifyBlockingMove` reads: an actor that `!AppearsFriendlyTo(blocking)` **returns immediately**; an actor that `IsIdle` **queues a `Nudge` and drives out of the way**; only a non-idle friendly falls through to `IsBlocking = true`. So the sim contains machinery whose whole job is to clear the obstruction, and a blocked-path activity is precisely what wakes it. Three stagings that actually block, in descending faithfulness: **enemy-owned** blockers (early-out at `AppearsFriendlyTo`), friendly blockers that are **not idle**, or **immobile** actors. **Second trap in the same staging: infantry do not block infantry.** Exit search goes through `pos.GetAvailableSubCell(c)` (in `Activities/UnloadCargo.cs`) and up to three infantry share one cell, so a ring of eight riflemen leaves free subcells and every search succeeds — only a full-cell occupant, i.e. a vehicle, blocks an infantry exit. Generalises past unloading: **"I placed obstacles" is a claim about the frame you are looking at, not about the tick the code runs on** — before staging any blockage, ask what the code under test does when it finds itself blocked.
- **A Lua `AmmoCount` on a pool the actor does not declare THROWS rather than returning zero.** `AmmoPoolProperties.AmmoCount` raises `LuaException("Invalid ammopool name …")` when no pool matches the name (`Scripting/Properties/AmmoPoolProperties.cs`); `MaximumAmmoCount` is the same shape. A scenario that reads a shared `primary-ammo` + `secondary-ammo` pair across a lane table therefore **kills the whole run** the moment one lane's actor carries a single pool — the failure presents as a dead scenario, not as a missing number. Same accessor-choice family as the `Trait<T>()` / `TraitOrDefault<T>()` rule above.
- **Reading any trait of a Disposed actor throws — so a bot squad module must prune dead members *before* it ticks squad states, not on a slower scan cadence.** `TraitDictionary` guards every lookup with `CheckDestroyed`, which throws `InvalidOperationException: Attempted to get trait from destroyed object` on a Disposed actor — `TraitOrDefault` included, so there is no "safe" trait read. A module whose state-update interval is shorter than its membership-prune interval will therefore hold dead actors in `Squad.Units` for the gap and CTD the first time a state touches a trait (the concrete crash: `HelicopterSquadBotModule` updated squads every 5 ticks but pruned only every 100, so a state tick's `GetRole` → `TraitOrDefault<AIHelicopterRole>` hit a destroyed hind). The engine-standard invariant is **prune-before-update**: `SquadManagerBotModule.CleanSquads()` runs on every `BotTick` before any `s.Update()` (at the top of `AssignRolesToIdleUnits`); `HelicopterSquadBotModule.PruneSquads()` (`RemoveAll` on `IsDead`/`!IsInWorld`/foreign owner) mirrors it and is called at the top of `UpdateSquads`. Enforce list hygiene upstream at the single choke point that runs before every update — don't scatter per-site trait-read guards through the squad states (they rot; the choke point can't be bypassed).
- **A ground unit CANNOT path off the map, and it is structural rather than a tuning limit.** `Locomotor.MovementCostForCell` returns `PathGraph.MovementCostForUnreachableCell` for every cell where `!world.Map.Contains(cell)`, so **every** `Mobile` pathfind terminates at the playable boundary by construction — no destination, cost table or locomotor tuning gets a ground unit past it. Aircraft escape only because `Fly` takes a `WPos` and never consults a locomotor. So "make ground units drive off the edge like aircraft do" is not a matter of picking a farther target cell: the pathfinder refuses it, and the only way out is to stop asking the pathfinder and drive `IPositionable.SetCenterPosition` directly (what `Drag` does — see the `SetCenterPosition` rule below for the cell-vs-position consequences that follow).
- **`Map.GetTerrainInfo(CPos)` / `GetTerrainIndex(CPos)` are UNGUARDED — an off-map cell throws `IndexOutOfRangeException`.** `GetTerrainInfo(CPos)` delegates to `GetTerrainIndex(cell)`, which does a raw `cachedTerrainIndexes[cell.ToMPos(this)]` array index with no bounds check — so any code that can hand it a cell past the map edge crashes hard. The engine idiom everywhere else is to gate it behind `Map.Contains(cell)` first (Aircraft land checks, Wanders, Missile trails, HeliEmergencyLanding, AIUtils all do); the `Rules.TerrainInfo.GetTerrainIndex(string/TerrainTile)` *overload* is safe (no per-cell array). The recurring source of off-map cells is death effects: a `FallToEarth`/`SpawnActorOnDeath` husk or a `LeavesTrailsCA` ballistic-missile trail whose `CenterPosition` drifts past the edge. When adding a per-cell terrain read, either `Contains`-guard it or `Map.Clamp` the cell into bounds first. (Contrast the harmless `Rules.TerrainInfo` string overload.)
