- **Bot-module actor-name config is a case-mismatch trap: `actor.Info.Name` is ALWAYS lowercase, so a case-sensitive config set silently no-matches.** `Ruleset.cs` builds every `ActorInfo` with `k.Key.ToLowerInvariant()` (`Ruleset.LoadDefaults` and `Ruleset.Load`), so at runtime an actor's name is always lowercase. A bot-module Info field that stores YAML actor names verbatim in a default (ordinal, case-sensitive) `HashSet<string>`/`Dictionary<string,int>` and then does `.Contains(a.Info.Name)` / `.ContainsKey(name)` / `== key` **silently no-matches** the moment the YAML value carries an uppercase letter — no warning, no exception, the unit is just never built/classified/priced (this was the "US only ever buys the littlebird" bug: uppercase `A10/F16/…` in `UnitsToBuild`). The tell (grep signature): `= new HashSet<string>()` / `= null` dict with NO `StringComparer.OrdinalIgnoreCase`, consumed by `.Contains(<name>)`/`.ContainsKey(<name>)`. **Hardened at the engine level:** the shared helper `ActorNameCase` (`Traits/BotModules/ActorNameCase.cs`, `NormalizeInPlace(HashSet)` / `NormalizeKeysInPlace(Dict)`) lowercases the collection contents in place, wired into the bot-module Info `RulesetLoaded` methods that hold actor names (list them with `git grep -l 'ActorNameCase\.' engine/OpenRA.Mods.Common/Traits/BotModules/`) — so config case can no longer silently no-op. It lowercases *contents* rather than swapping to `OrdinalIgnoreCase` because `FieldLoader.ParseHashSetOrList`/`ParseDictionary` build each collection via `Activator.CreateInstance(fieldType, capacity)`, a fresh default-comparer instance that discards any comparer set in the `readonly` field initializer. **Do NOT lowercase engine-token sets** that look like the family but compare against non-lowercased tokens — production-queue `Type` sets (`Vehicle`/`Infantry`/`Aircraft`), target-type `BitSet`s (`Submarine`), terrain sets (`Water`/`Tree`), cohesion enums — their capitalization is correct. (`PoiMap.IncomeWeights`, a World trait not a bot module, carries the same hazard but was out of the audit's scope — lowercase-safe today.)
- **An `EnterBehaviour.Dispose` unit is REMOVED at structure-entry, *before* the structure's effect completes — so "actor gone ⇒ mission failed" is a false read.** `RepairsBridges` uses `EnterBehaviour.Dispose` (the `RepairsBridgesInfo.EnterBehaviour` default): the engineer (`e6`) is disposed the instant it enters the `LegacyBridgeHut`/`BridgeHut`, but the bridge stays `BridgeDamageState.Dead` through the whole repair animation while `hut.Repairing` is true (`LegacyBridgeHut.Repairing`, `BridgeHut.Repairing` — both public). A bot mission that checks "bridge still Dead + engineer gone ⇒ failure" therefore records a FALSE failure (with cooldown + a wasted retry) on a mission that is actually succeeding. Gate `Repairing` (repair-in-progress ⇒ hold and wait for the bridge to flip to not-Dead ⇒ success) BEFORE the engineer-alive and timeout checks — the *ordering* is the whole safety property (`EngineerRouteOpenBotModule.TickActiveMission`). The `RepairBridge` order itself is `new Order("RepairBridge", engineer, Target.FromActor(hut), false)` (`Target.Type == Actor`); `bot.QueueOrder` resolves it on the engineer and queues the walk-to-hut + enter + repair activity (`RepairsBridges.IssueOrder` / `ResolveOrder`) — no custom activity needed.
- **`Actor.IsIdle` is `CurrentActivity == null` — and for any actor whose traits queue an activity on going idle, it is never observably true.** **The line that enforces it is `QueueActivity`, not the tick loop** — the obvious citation is the wrong one. `Actor.Tick` does fire `INotifyBecomingIdle` and then re-run the activity **inside the same tick**, but that second `RunActivity` exists to avoid an *empty null tick of motion* and says so in its own comment; deleting it would not open an observation window. What closes the window is `QueueActivity`'s `if (CurrentActivity == null) CurrentActivity = nextActivity;`: the instant any handler queues, `IsIdle` is **already false**, before the tick loop reaches its second `RunActivity`. `Aircraft.OnBecomingIdle` (`Traits/Air/Aircraft.cs`) queues `FlyIdle` under the default `IdleBehavior` (`IdleBehaviorType.None`) — **except** when the aircraft is already at `LandAltitude`, where it returns without queueing `FlyIdle`, so the property is about airborne frames specifically, and `FlyIdle.Tick` returns true only on `ForceLanding`/cancel or with something queued behind it — so **a hovering helicopter's `IsIdle` is false at every tick, forever.** Every `IsIdle` test on an airframe is dead code: a `!IsIdle` guard resets its counter each tick (an idle-timeout window becomes unreachable), an `IsIdle` gate never opens. Use `AIUtils.IsUnoccupiedAirframe` (`IsIdle || (CurrentActivity is FlyIdle && CurrentActivity.NextActivity == null)`) instead. Ground infantry are the *opposite* case and need a different fix: `Mobile.OnBecomingIdle` queues nothing in the common case (layer 0 + `Locomotor.CanStayInCell` ⇒ early return) and WW3MOD combat infantry carry no other idle-queuing trait, so a soldier standing still genuinely **is** idle — but one pushed forward engages through `AutoTarget` and is never idle again, so requiring N *simultaneously* idle soldiers on one sampled tick is unsatisfiable in practice. For infantry the right predicate is availability-for-tasking (in a rear reserve zone, uncommitted in the `PoiGoalGuard` ledger, unreserved by a peer module), not idleness. There is a standing PITFALL on the ground half in `MountedTransportBotModule.TryAssignNewTasks` and on `LayeredDefenceBotModule`'s `ExcludedActorTypes`.
- **`INotifyIdle.TickIdle` fires EVERY tick the actor stays idle, not once on going idle.** `Actor.Tick` calls `becomingIdles` on the `!wasIdle && IsIdle` edge, then `tickIdles` on each subsequent tick while still idle (the `else if (wasIdle)` branch). An idle-driven trait that scans the world is therefore a *per-tick* world scan unless it throttles itself. `INotifyBecomingIdle` is the edge-triggered interface (what `AmmoPool` uses). When throttling, derive the per-instance phase from `self.ActorID % Interval` — **not** a `SharedRandom` draw (see the `SharedRandom` rule above; a trait that loads for every profile would shift the synced stream for control games).
- **NO INVENTORY EXISTS of which traits react to a unit's own state, and the absence of the list is not evidence of the absence of the problem.** Several traits answer "this unit should do something about its own condition" (`AmmoPool`'s resupply dispatch, `AutoSeekSupplies`, auto-follow, auto-rearm), and the recurring failure is that such a trait is wired to an **idle** trigger while a combat unit under orders is never idle — so the reaction never fires for the units that most need it (see the `Actor.IsIdle` rule above). The claim that *every* such trait in the repo is idle-triggered is **not established** and is already false in one case — `AutoSeekSupplies` implements `ITick` and ships enabled. **Nobody has swept for the full set.** If you are adding or auditing a self-state reaction, enumerate it yourself from the `INotifyIdle`/`INotifyBecomingIdle` implementer list rather than assuming either that the problem is universal or that it is confined to the traits named here.
- **`Activity.QueueChild` APPENDS to the child chain — it does not replace.** `if (childActivity != null) childActivity.Queue(activity); else childActivity = activity;` (`Activity.QueueChild`). So a multi-leg activity that cancels its current child and immediately queues the next leg runs the **stale, cancelled** leg first. `Cancel` only sets `State = Canceling` for a child already past `Queued` (`Activity.Cancel`; a never-run child is marked `Done` and skipped), and a `Canceling` child that is never ticked again sits in the slot indefinitely. The fix is to **drain**: after cancelling, tick the child until `ChildActivity == null` *before* queuing the replacement.
- **`Activity.Cancel` tests `IsInterruptible` on ITSELF, never on its child — so `IsInterruptible = false` on a child does NOT make the composition uncancellable.** `Cancel` returns early on its own `!IsInterruptible`, then calls `ChildActivity?.Cancel(self)` and sets its own `State` regardless of what the child did. A non-interruptible child therefore refuses, stays `Active`, and the parent goes `Canceling` around it. **Whether that orphans the child depends on `ChildHasPriority`, and this is the qualification to carry:** under the default `true` (set in the `Activity` constructor) `TickOuter` runs `TickChild(self) && (finishing || Tick(self))`, so the parent's `Tick` is not even reached until the child completes and the child is safe. It is the handful of `ChildHasPriority = false` activities — `RotateToEdge`, `Enter`, `MoveAdjacentTo`, `SmartMoveActivity`, `AttackMoveActivity`, `PickupUnit`, `FlyOffMap`, `FlyAttack`, `SeekSupplyProvider` — whose own `Tick` runs alongside the child and typically opens with `if (IsCanceling) return true;`, dropping it. **If a parent's cleanup depends on a child finishing, set `IsInterruptible = false` on the PARENT** (`RotateToEdge.StartDriveOff` does exactly this before its final `Drag`, with the reasoning inline).
- **`IPositionable.SetCenterPosition` moves the WORLD POSITION only — the actor's CELL does not follow.** `Mobile.SetCenterPosition` assigns `CenterPosition` and fires notifications but never calls `Mobile.SetLocation`; only the two `SetPosition` overloads do both. Since `Actor.Location` is `OccupiesSpace.TopLeft` = `Mobile.ToCell`, **an actor driven by `Drag` can be rendered several cells outside `Map.Bounds` while `Actor.Location` is still a valid in-bounds cell.** Two consequences that are easy to get backwards: a predicate testing `self.Location` to detect "has left the map" is dead code for a `Mobile` actor, and the unit **keeps blocking the cell it left from** for the whole leg, because `AddInfluence`/`RemoveInfluence` only re-run in `SetLocation`. This is `Mobile`-specific — `Aircraft.TopLeft` is `Map.CellContaining(CenterPosition)`, so an aircraft's `Location` *does* go off-map, which is what `FlyOffMap.Tick` terminates on.
- **A QUEUED ORDER BOUNDARY IS A STATE BOUNDARY — splitting a move in two destroys the corner it was supposed to contain.** Two queued `Move` orders do **not** produce a corner arc. The first completes at its waypoint, the unit settles on the cell centre with `FromCell == ToCell`, and the second turns it in place before setting off (`Move.Tick`'s `firstFacing != mobile.Facing` branch, `Activities/Move/Move.cs`). The arc — and with it the `ToCell` retargeting that is the interesting behaviour — happens only between cells of **one** path, in `MoveFirstHalf`'s chained-turn branch, which opens the arc and in the same step calls `mobile.SetLocation(mobile.ToCell, …, nextCell.Value.Cell, …)`. Generalises past movement: **anything that exists only *within* one activity — arc turning, carryover progress, mid-path retargeting — is destroyed by splitting the order, and splitting is the natural way to write the setup.** A run staged that way can drive, stop, turn and finish perfectly happily without ever entering the state under examination.
  - **Corollary: `self.Location` on a dying mobile actor is a RESERVATION, not a position.** `Mobile.TopLeft` is `ToCell`, and mid-corner `ToCell` is already the cell *after* the turn. A husk spawns at `self.Location` (`SpawnActorOnDeath`, `self.Location + Info.Offset`) and then drags to that cell's centre (`Husk`'s `finalPosition`), so the wreck's straight drag line is neither the death facing nor the living unit's heading — it is a third direction, and the three coincide only on a straight leg, which is why anything derived from the wrong one is a no-op on most kills. Any death-time code reading `self.Location` — spawn cells, drop points, score attribution by cell — inherits this and can be up to one and a half cells from the body. *(The husk-facing case itself is fixed: `Husk` now settles onto the drag line via `HuskSettleGeometry.SettleFacing`.)*
  - **Corollary: `Turn` cannot be used on a husk.** `Turn.Tick` advances via `Util.TickFacing(…, TurnSpeed)` and `Husk.TurnSpeed` is `WAngle.Zero`, so the activity never completes — a `Drag` given a `facing` queues a `Turn` (`Drag.OnFirstRun`) and would hang a wreck forever.
- **`ITick` traits KEEP FIRING for an actor removed from the world but not disposed — `IsInWorld` is not a tick gate.** Actors and traits are ticked from two different places: `World.Tick` iterates `actors.Values` calling `a.Tick()`, which drives **activities** only; `ITick` traits are driven on the next line by `ApplyToActorsWithTraitTimed<ITick>` → `TraitDictionary.ApplyToAllTimed`, a plain indexed walk of the trait container with **no `IsInWorld` and no `Disposed` filter**. `World.Remove` sets `IsInWorld = false`, drops the actor from the `actors` dict and fires `INotifyRemovedFromWorld` but never touches the trait container — an actor leaves that only via `World.TraitDict.RemoveActor(this)` inside `Actor.Dispose`'s frame-end task. Removed-but-not-disposed is a **reachable** state, not a theoretical one: a Carryall pickup calls `cargo.World.Remove(cargo)` with no `Dispose` (`Activities/PickupUnit.cs:174`), as does loading into cargo, and the actor is `World.Add`ed back later. Throughout the carry its `ITick` traits run with `CenterPosition` frozen at the pickup point — so any proximity/aura trait keeps operating from a stale position and can even re-grant a condition one tick after `INotifyRemovedFromWorld` revoked it. Any world-position-dependent `ITick` trait needs an explicit `if (!self.IsInWorld) { …release…; return; }` at the top of `Tick` (`SupplyProvider.TickServing`, with the mechanism written up inline). Corollary: `INotifyRemovedFromWorld` is the right place to *release* state but is no guarantee the trait has stopped; the terminal pair for permanent teardown is `INotifyKilled` + `INotifyActorDisposing`.
- **`IsDead || !IsInWorld` is NOT a sufficient liveness test for a host actor, because it does not cover CAPTURE — a captured building is alive, in world, and now hostile.** This bites any multi-tick errand that validates its destination once and then drives to it. The concrete case is the Logistics Centre, which is capturable by inheritance and easy to assume is not: `LOGISTICSCENTER:` → `^Building` → `^BasicBuilding` → `^NeutralOrOccupiedCapturable` (`CaptureManager` plus `Capturable@neutral` / `@occupied`), all in `mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/structures.yaml`, and it additionally carries `OwnerLostAction: Action: ChangeOwner` / `Owner: Neutral`. **So a supply truck can set out toward its own depot and complete the delivery into an enemy's.** The fix ships and is the pattern to copy — add the relationship term: `host.IsDead || !host.IsInWorld || !self.Owner.IsAlliedWith(host.Owner)` (`DeliverSupply.Tick`, reasoning in-file directly above it). **Whenever an activity re-checks a host across ticks, check ownership alongside liveness; the two are independent and only one of them is obvious.**
- **`Actor.GetConditionCount(name)` is a public, cache-backed read of a granted condition by name, and it works for conditions nothing consumes.** `GetConditionCount` reads a cache that `GrantCondition` populates unconditionally through `UpdateConditionState` — `conditionCache[condition] = conditionState.Tokens.Count` is written *before* the consumer fan-out, so the count is recorded even when no trait on the actor declares a `RequiresCondition` for that token. This is the honest way to read a state a C# activity grants: `evacuating` comes from `RotateToEdge` (`self.GrantCondition("evacuating")`) and is otherwise declared only as a bare `ExternalCondition` on the unit templates. Bot code that instead infers such a state from a *cause* (e.g. "all pools empty ⇒ must be evacuating") misses every other trigger. The suppression stack is read the same way — `GetConditionCount("suppressed")`, with the name kept as an Info field rather than hard-coded.
- **A `HitShape`'s `Radius` does not affect AIMING at all — it reaches damage through two different doors, and only one of them can shut.** `HitShape.TargetablePositions` returns `CenterPosition + TargetableOffsets` and never reads `Radius`; `TargetableOffsets` defaults to `{ WVec.Zero }`. So a weapon aims at the actor's centre whatever the radius. The radius is consumed only by (a) `DistanceFromEdge`, which `SpreadDamageWarhead` uses for falloff — for a circle that is `max(0, |d| - Radius)`, so shrinking the radius costs exactly `Radius` world-units of lethal envelope and nothing else; and (b) `CenterProximityPercent`, which `TargetDamageWarhead` uses to scale damage — for a circle that is `100 × (Radius − d) / Radius`, which **hits zero AT the radius**. Door (b) is therefore a hard cutoff and door (a) is a 1:1 trade. **Worked case, because it is counter-intuitive:** `^Drone` sets `Radius: 1` against the `Radius: 32` it would inherit from `^NeutralAirborne` (both in `aircraft.yaml`), which looks like it should make the quadcopter unhittable. It does not — the mod's real AA (`Stinger` and its `9M311`/`Stinger.quad` heirs in `weapons-missiles.yaml`: `Damage: 5000, Spread: 256, Penetration: 20`) is `SpreadDamage`, so the whole cost is 31 units against a weapon whose own `Inaccuracy` is 300. **The one weapon it genuinely shuts out is `20mm_CRAM`** (`weapons-ballistics.yaml`), whose own block declares only a `TargetDamage` warhead; the `SpreadDamage` in its resolved ruleset comes from `Inherits@HitEffects: ^MinimalExplosionEffectsAir` (`weapons-effects.yaml`), which sets `ValidTargets: Air` and **no `Damage:`** — a target-type scaffold so `CreateEffect` renders, not a damage half. CRAM does literally zero damage to a drone. Pinned by `DroneHitShapeTest`. **When a hitshape radius looks decisive, ask which warhead class reaches the victim first** — and note that the drone radius has no recoverable intent: `git log -S` gives one commit (`e62150de`, 2024-01-07) that changed it 8 → 1 while doing something unrelated, and it has never been 32.
- **`world.Timestep` is MUTABLE runtime state; `world.GameSpeed.Timestep` is the invariant.** `World.Timestep` is a plain field initialised from the match speed (`Timestep = ReplayTimestep = GameSpeed.Timestep` in the `World` constructor) and then **written at runtime** by three consumers — the debug speed button (`SpeedControlButtonLogic`), `TestModeSpeedMultiplier`, and `BotVsBotMatchWatcher`. `World.GameSpeed` is `public readonly`. **Any display of ELAPSED or REMAINING match time must format with `world.GameSpeed.Timestep`** — formatting an accumulated `WorldTick` with the mutable field retroactively rescales the whole match history (a ten-minute game read 05:00 the instant you pressed 2x). The distinguishing pathology is *retroactive rescaling of an accumulator*, not "uses `world.Timestep`": most `FormatTime` call sites convert a **future** duration (support-power charge, build time) where the currently-effective timestep is arguably right. When auditing this class of bug, grep for `WorldTick`-derived quantities, not for `Timestep`. The three `WorldTick`-anchored readouts (game clock `GameTimerLogic`, time-limit countdown `TimeLimitManager`, strategic victory `StrategicProgressWidget`) all use `GameSpeed.Timestep` and carry PITFALL comments. **ww3mod time limits are honest in gametime:** `TimeLimitManager` converts with `TickTime.TicksForMinutes`, which multiplies before dividing, so a 90-minute limit really runs 90 minutes; `world.Timestep` is still the source but is read **once in the constructor**, before any of the three mutators can have run. Do not reintroduce `ticksPerSecond = 1000 / World.Timestep`: in integer division against the 60 ms baseline that is 16, not 16.67, and ran every limit ~4 % short (a "20 minute" limit was 19200 ticks = 1152 s). The lobby values were deliberately NOT re-tuned to preserve the old short durations — the clock was made honest and matches got ~4 % longer. **The Lua test harness derives its rate from the same baseline:** `TestHarness.TimestepMs = 60` (`mods/ww3mod/scripts/test-helpers.lua`) mirrors `mod.yaml`'s `GameSpeeds: DefaultSpeed: default` → that block's `Timestep: 60` ⇒ **16.67 ticks/s**, and seconds convert through `TestHarness.TicksForSeconds`, which mirrors `TickTime.TicksForSeconds`. **The hazard is in windows authored against the old `TestHarness.TicksPerSecond = 25` constant:** under it every `AssertWithin(n)` window was **1.5× longer than it read**, so nothing was ever truncated; none was re-authored when the harness switched to the true rate, so every such seconds-literal window allows a third less time than its author measured. Treat a deadline from that era as un-audited until the suite has run.
- **`Game.RunAfterTick` runs INSIDE synced code — so the "click this button for the test driver" idiom CTDs on any in-match UI action.** `RunAfterTick` queues onto `delayedActions`, which is drained by `PerformDelayedActions()` **from the first line of `Game.LogicTick()`** — a synced context. Any UI action that touches the order generator therefore trips `World.OrderGenerator`'s setter → `Sync.AssertUnsynced("The current order generator may not be changed from synced code")` and throws. **That is the ONLY `AssertUnsynced` call site in the engine** — `git grep -n AssertUnsynced engine/` returns the definition in `Sync.cs`, this one call in `World.cs`, and otherwise only comments/string literals (in `TestGlobal.cs` and `ScriptGlobalSyncTest.cs`). One tripwire guarding one setter is why *"dispatches a UI handler from Lua"* is a silent bug class: everything a UI handler does other than reassigning `World.OrderGenerator` runs synced with nothing to complain. (Scope the claim to `AssertUnsynced` — `Sync.RunUnsynced` has a *separate* guard that throws `"RunUnsynced: sync-changing code may not run here"` on a hash mismatch.) **When you need to know whether a state is protected, enumerate its WRITERS, not the callers of the guard** — `OrderGenerator = …`/`CancelInputMode()` has dozens of sites across dozens of files (`git grep -nE '[^a-zA-Z]OrderGenerator = |CancelInputMode\(\)' -- '*.cs'`). `World.CancelInputMode()` is the common route in, so opening an in-game menu panel is enough. **The asymmetry is what makes this easy to ship: the real `OnClick` never trips it**, because input handling is already an unsynced context — the button works for a human and explodes for the driver. The pre-match idiom got away with it only because at the **main menu** there is no `World` and nothing asserts sync at all; copying it into a hook that fires in-match is where it breaks. **Fix is one wrapper:** `Game.RunAfterTick(() => Sync.RunUnsynced(world, () => …))`. Keep both — `RunAfterTick` defers past construction, `RunUnsynced` solves the synced-frame problem, and they are different problems.
  - **Diagnostic corollary for screenshot drivers: a crash still produces a PNG, and it is BLACK.** The pending capture command is consumed by the CTD, so the manifest gains a normal-looking entry pointing at an all-black image. **File size is the cheap tell** (~34 KB vs ~1.1 MB for a healthy full-screen capture) — a driver that only checks "did the PNG appear" reports success on a crashed game. Grep the log for `Exception of type` before trusting a capture.
- **A right-click resolves through an `OrderPriority` contest, so naming the order a test expects assumes the answer to a question the UI decides at click time.** `UnitOrderGenerator.OrderForUnit` walks the `IIssueOrder` targeters in **descending `OrderPriority`** and takes the first that accepts (`OpenRA.Mods.Common/Orders/UnitOrderGenerator.cs` — the file is under `OpenRA.Mods.Common/`, not `OpenRA.Game/`; the whole descending list is walked *twice*, the second pass against `Target.FromCell`). `AttendAlly`'s targeter is priority **7** (`AttendAllyInfo.OrderPriority`) and `AttackBase`'s is **6** (`new AttackOrderTargeter(this, 6)` in `Attack/AttackBase.cs`) — so clicking a wounded ally with a medic selected issues **`AttendAlly`, not `Attack`**, and the medic ends up in an `AttackMoveActivity` (the `AttendAllyActivity` subclass) wrapped around a `Follow` (`AttendAlly`'s `ResolveOrder`) where treatment happens only via that activity's 10-tick rescan. A scripted test that issues the order by name therefore never runs the contest and can pass while the player-visible path stays broken. **Use `Test.ClickOrder` (`Scripting/Global/TestGlobal.cs`), which resolves the real chain and returns the winning OrderString, whenever routing is part of what is under test.**
  - **`OrderForUnit` returns the FIRST accepting targeter and stops, so a lower-priority targeter on the same actor is not a fallback — it is unreachable.** Every downstream consumer therefore sees **one** `UnitOrderResult` per actor, including the selection-override pass, which asks each *resolved* order's `TargetOverridesSelection`. So **a high-priority targeter that accepts broadly silently disables the selection behaviour of every targeter beneath it**: `AttendAlly` at priority 7 accepts any allied infantry, so `AttackBase`'s targeter at 6 — whose `TargetOverridesSelection` is unconditionally true — is never consulted. Note the debugging story this invites and the reason it is a dead end: reasoning from `TargetOverridesSelection` alone predicts a *damage-dependent* bug (a wounded ally is rescued by the attack targeter, an undamaged one is not). There is no such dependency — priority resolved the question before damage was ever consulted.
  - **The CURSOR and the CLICK are resolved by different rules and can disagree.** `GetCursor` goes through `CursorForOrders` over the same resolved list but never consults `TargetOverridesSelection`, **so the cursor can promise an order the click will not issue.** A user report of the shape *"I get the special icon, so it looks like I can issue that order"* is evidence about the cursor path and not about the order path.
  - **A target LINE is drawn from the top-level activity's MOVE node and never from an attack child**, because the renderer walks `CurrentActivity → NextActivity` and does not descend into children (`Render/DrawLineToTarget.cs`) while `AttackMoveActivity.TargetLineNodes` yields only the move's. So an attack-move draws to the *place* while shooting at something else — defensible for attack-move, which names a destination, and **actively misleading for any order that names a person**, because the player checks the line and it agrees with them.
  - **The drawn cursor and the executed click resolve through the SAME path, so a cursor that lies and a click that misbehaves are one defect, not two**. `UnitOrderGenerator.Order` and `GetCursor` both funnel into `OrdersForSelection` → `OrderForUnit`; the deliberate comment in `GetCursor` says so. **The practical consequence is the one worth carrying: a trait that accepts a click it cannot act on is a silent veto over every lower-priority targeter, and it still draws a cursor** — `OrderForUnit` returns the *first* acceptor walking down priority and never reconsiders. `Repairable`'s targeter is priority **5** (`Repairable`'s `IIssueOrder.Orders`), below Restock 7 and Deliver 6, so a higher-priority acceptor that no-ops swallows the repair the player was aiming for. **When a cursor is wrong, look for a targeter that accepts too eagerly, not for a rendering bug.**
- **Deleting one term of a compound `PauseOnCondition`/`RequiresCondition` silently changes what the survivor MEANS.** A surviving term stops being a qualifier scoped by the deleted one and becomes the whole rule. The worked example: `PauseOnCondition: !ammo-primary || suppressed >= 10` was an *ammo* gate with a suppression term riding along as boilerplate; removing the unit's ammo pool made `!ammo-primary` dead, it was stripped, and `suppressed >= 10` was promoted unexamined into the armament's entire gating rule — switching that unit off under any incoming fire at all. **Grep for other truncated two-term conditions before trusting any of them as intentional**, and when you delete a term, re-derive the survivor's intent rather than assuming it was already independent.
- **Healing is DAMAGE WITH THE SIGN FLIPPED, and the sign decides which half of the modifier stack applies.** `Health.InflictDamage` gates its whole modifier loop on `!ignoreModifiers && damage.Value > 0` (`Health.cs:221`). A heal is negative damage, so that comparison partitions the stack: **victim-side `IDamageModifier`s are SKIPPED for healing** — veterancy damage multipliers, forest-cover density, prone modifiers, garrison cover — while **firer-side modifiers still apply**, because they are attached at the warhead call site rather than in `Health` (`Armament` collects the firing actor's `IFirepowerModifier`s at `:351` into `args.DamageModifiers` at `:627`, and `DamageWarhead` applies them at `:299`). Two consequences that are only visible in the arithmetic: the intuitive reading *"a tough unit is also hard to heal"* is **wrong**, and **a veteran medic heals more**. Anything that meters or modifies healing must be attached firer-side; a victim-side modifier is a no-op on the heal path with no error and no lint.
- **The FOLLOW layer decides where the heal scan is standing, so a casualty outside the notice radius was never a candidate — not merely outranked.** `HealerAutoTarget.SearchRange` (`8c0` on `^MEDI`, `infantry.yaml:2328`) is measured from wherever the medic is, and nothing in `HealerAutoTarget` ever moves him; what moves him is `AutoFollowAlly`, whose own `SearchRange` is `20c0` (`:2361`). **Two autonomy layers, one positioning the other's scan centre, with radii that disagree by 2.5×**: a healthy ally standing closer parked the medic at `FollowDistance` and everything past 8 cells stayed invisible, so `FindBestTarget` never enumerated the casualty and the patient-outranks-ally precedence already in the follow layer had nothing to fire on. Two readings that are plausible from the symptom and wrong: it is *not* a ranking contest (there was no contest — the casualty was outside the candidate set), and it is *not* the follow move pinning the medic non-idle (acquisition **is** idle-gated, but the medic here never queued a move). Two structural rules came out of the fix and generalise past medics: **a stickiness margin that compares raw distances silently reverses any tier you add below it** — an incumbent at 2 cells against a challenger at 10 gives `2 − 10 = −8 < SwitchMargin`, so the tier must be checked *before* the margin (`AutoFollowAlly.FindNearestAlly`); and **the two layers must ask ONE predicate**, which is why the follow layer calls `HealerAutoTarget.WouldTreat` (via `AutoFollowAlly.IsCasualty`) rather than carrying its own copy of "wounded" — two copies let the medic march to a man the healer then declines, and park there.
- **Triage is an ACQUISITION-TIME decision only: a medic re-ranks while walking and never while treating.** `SelectPatient` is reachable only from `TryGetAutoTargetOverride` ← `AutoTarget.ScanForTarget`, which needs the medic idle or running a move child; treating is a top-level `Attack` activity that holds him non-idle through the whole `BurstWait` between pulses. So `StabilizeThreshold`'s stabilize-and-switch block cannot run in the case it was written for — a man who goes critical at the medic's feet waits until the current patient is finished. Two adjacent facts worth having: `TryGetAutoTargetOverride` fills its target **only** when the ranked patient is already inside heal range (the `IsInHealRange` test after `SelectPatient`), so on any path where nothing is free to walk the medic there he can stand beside a treatable man and treat nobody; and `FindBestTarget` skips `a == self`, so **a medic cannot treat himself** — which, combined with the mod-wide speed-zero and bleed-out below 50% HP, puts a lone sub-50% medic on an unrecoverable slope. **Do not "fix" the automatic ranking on the strength of an ordered-path failure; they are different failures.**
- **Every per-target switch costs a re-aim, and the default is 15 ticks.** `ArmamentInfo.AimingDelay` defaults to `15`; `CheckFire` resets it on *any* target change and `CanFire` declines while `IsAiming`. So a unit that legitimately re-picks its target pays ~0.9 s of silence per switch on top of any walk, and a stickiness margin that stops oscillation does not stop this — it only stops the switches it prevents.
- **`RequiresForceFire` is an ARMAMENT-level gate, not an input-level one — it disables autonomous fire as completely as `HoldFire` does.** The filter lives in `AttackBase.ChooseArmamentsForTarget` (`!(a.Info.RequiresForceFire && !forceAttack)`), and **auto-target calls that method with `forceAttack: false`** (`AutoTarget.ChooseTarget`, `AutoTarget.EstimatePercentDamage`). A force-fire-only armament therefore returns no armaments to the auto-target scan, so a bot at `FireAtWill` never fires it. The name reads like a player-input rule ("this needs Ctrl+Alt"), which is how shipped launchers came to pair it with a permissive `InitialStanceAI` — **an actor pairing the two is describing a behaviour it does not have**, and when you remove `RequiresForceFire` the stance beside it has to be re-decided in the same edit or bot behaviour changes as an invisible side effect.
- **A unit below `Hunt` stance never WALKS to an auto-target, so a scan radius wider than the weapon buys nothing.** `AutoTarget` computes `allowMove = allowMovement && engagementStance >= EngagementStance.Hunt` (in `INotifyIdle.TickIdle`, and identically in `INotifyDamage.Damaged` and `TickPreemption`), and `Attack.TickAttack` turns that into a hard refusal: inside `if (!callerAllowedMove)` (`Activities/Attack.cs`), HoldPosition returns `UnableToAttack` unconditionally and Defensive returns it for a target that is merely **out of range**. Only a *direct player order* passes `callerAllowedMove: true`. Note the Defensive refusal is narrow — a `tooClose` or `losBlocked` target still gets a `MoveWithinRange`. Consequence: widening the scan radius on a trait feeding `IOverrideAutoTarget` does nothing for a Defensive unit, because the attack layer is where the walking would have to happen and it declines; pair it with the idle/follow path instead. **Second-order trap that bites units you did not change:** `AttackMoveActivity` re-scans every 10 ticks *while marching* and unconditionally cancels its move child whenever the scan returns any target (both in `AttackMoveActivity.Tick`), then queues an attack a Defensive unit immediately abandons — so an `IOverrideAutoTarget` returning targets its owner cannot act on **interrupts that unit's march every 10 ticks, indefinitely**. Return only targets that are actionable *right now*; keep "noticed but not reached" state private (`HealerAutoTarget.TryGetAutoTargetOverride` gates on `IsInHealRange` for exactly this reason).
- **`SelectionPriorityModifier@OutOfAmmo` permanently demotes any unit that has an armament but NO `AmmoPool` — giving a support unit a pool silently deletes the behaviour.** `Armament` grants `weapon-<Name>` on creation (`Armament.Created`), and `^Soldier`'s modifier (`infantry.yaml`) fires on `^AmmoDecoration`'s "no armed weapon has ammo" expression — `(weapon-primary || weapon-secondary) && !(weapon-primary && ammo-primary) && …`, the same predicate as its `WithDecoration@AmmoNone` pip (`defaults.yaml`) — which a `weapon-primary` with no `ammo-primary` satisfies. A unit with an armament named `primary` and no `AmmoPool` anywhere in its ancestry therefore satisfies that predicate **permanently** and carries `Modifier: -1` for the whole match — which is why a drag-selected squad never picks the medic as its representative. Nothing anywhere says "prefer other units over the medic"; the behaviour is entirely a side effect of having no ammo. **So adding an `AmmoPool` to a support unit (to meter heals, say), or renaming its armament, removes the demotion without a line of selection code changing** — and the cause will look nothing like the change that produced it. If such metering is ever needed, either keep it off `AmmoPool` or write the selection template explicitly first.
- **`ChooseArmamentsForTarget` filters `IsTraitDisabled` but NOT `IsTraitPaused`, so a fully paused unit ACCEPTS an attack order and then wedges silently.** `AttackBase.ChooseArmamentsForTarget` (with a standing `// FF TODO Check ammo?` and a PITFALL explaining why pause is deliberately not filtered there) ignores pause, and `AttackOrderTargeter.CanTargetActor` *deliberately* tolerates it — `ordered.FirstOrDefault(x => !x.IsTraitPaused) ?? ordered[0]`. So the order is accepted and a cursor is shown. What follows is the trap: `Attack.TickAttack` computes ranges, closes, turns and reaches `DoAttack`, which calls `Armament.CheckFire` → `CanFire` → `false` on `IsTraitPaused` and returns null. Nothing fires, nothing is logged, `TickAttack` still reports `Attacking`, and **the activity never completes** — the unit stands aiming for as long as the pause lasts, and because it is never idle, every `INotifyIdle` behaviour it owns (auto-target, auto-follow, auto-rearm) is silenced too. **Distinguish this from a genuine order refusal by whether a cursor appears and whether the unit walks:** refusal is silent and stationary from the first frame; the wedge walks, arrives, and then does nothing. The opt-in escape is `AttackBaseInfo.AbandonWhenArmamentsPaused` (default `false` so everything else keeps holding aim through brief pauses), consumed through the shared `AttackBase.RefusesForPause` by both `Attack.TickAttack` and `AttackOrderTargeter` — so an opted-in unit refuses the order up front rather than accepting it. **Do NOT widen it into "abandon when the unit cannot fire":** `Armament.CanFire` is also false on `IsReloading`, `IsWaitingBurst` and `IsAiming`, all of which are true on ordinary ticks of a healthy weapon, so the general version would drop every attack order in the game between shots. Pause is the only one of those states that can *last*, and duration is the whole reason the wedge is visible.
- **`FiringLOS.HasClearLOS` returns true unconditionally inside two cells** — `if (distSq < 4) return true;` (`FiringLOS.HasClearLOS`), because there is no shadow data at that range. So **any weapon with a range of ≤2 cells can never wedge on line of sight**, and the graded-detection system cannot reach it either. Worth knowing before diagnosing a short-range weapon's failure to fire as an LOS problem.
- **The activity framework has NO failure outcome at all, which is the root the `MoveResult` defect sits on.** `Activity.Tick` returns `bool` — done or not done (`engine/OpenRA.Game/Activities/Activity.cs`). An activity that discovers its task is impossible therefore has exactly two options: **lie** (return true, indistinguishable from success) or **spin** (return false forever). Every "accepted the order, cannot possibly do it, never gives up" bug in this codebase is one of those two choices, which is why they all look alike — and why no single patch reaches them all. Treat "fix the stuck-activity archetype" as at least two work items.
- **`Mobile.MoveResult` is declared and read but NEVER ASSIGNED — it is permanently `InProgress`.** The property exists (`Mobile.MoveResult`) and is read by `MoveAdjacentTo.cs:107` and `MoveCooldownHelper.cs:69,76`, but a repo-wide grep finds no assignment anywhere, and `InProgress` is enum value 0 (`enum MoveResult`, `TraitsInterfaces.cs`).
  - **Assigning it would NOT fix `Enter`, and that is worth knowing before anyone costs the repair.** `MoveCooldownHelper.cs:76` gives up only when `!RetryIfDestinationBlocked`, and `Enter` sets `RetryIfDestinationBlocked = true` deliberately — so even with `MoveResult` assigned correctly, `Enter` re-queues its approach forever. The comment there says genuine blocks abort via `TryStartEnter`'s Cancel path, but `TryStartEnter` is reached only once `CanEnterTargetNow` is true, i.e. once already adjacent — which an unreachable target never is. **A dead escape hatch guarded by a second, independent dead escape hatch** is why this survived review twice; `Enter` is now bounded by its own approach timeout instead.
  - **Blast radius, for whoever costs the repair later.** `MoveCooldownHelper` is consumed by `Follow`, `Enter`, `Resupply`, `MoveToDock`, `LayMines` and `LeapAttack`; `MoveAdjacentTo` underlies `MoveWithinRange`, `MoveOnto` and `SmartMoveActivity`. Assigning `MoveResult` turns two dormant branches live in essentially every "go to a thing and do something" activity, on both bot profiles at once — not a change to make without game runs. **A move that cannot path therefore never reports failure**, so any "did this unit fail to get there?" logic must key on observable evidence (has the unit changed cell in N ticks?) rather than on the result. When you write such a detector, enumerate the *success* states that also produce the absence you are keying on — arrival, completion and being-already-correct are all just as still as being stuck — and put that early-out **before** the guard, refreshing the guard's counters rather than merely skipping them (the arrival early-out in `AutoFollowAlly`'s `TickIdle`, which calls `stall.MarkProgress` before `TrackStall`, is the worked example).
- **An unreachable destination does not merely fail to report failure — `Move` reports SUCCESS, at the cell the unit was already standing on.** `PathFinder.FindPathToTargetCell` bails to `NoPath` up front when the target cell fails `CellAllowsMovement` or has an unreachable movement cost. `Move.Tick` then sees `path.Count == 0`, rewrites `destination = mobile.ToCell` and returns false; on the next tick `destination == mobile.ToCell` returns **true**. So the activity completes in about two ticks having gone nowhere, and **any follow-on queued behind it runs at the wrong cell** — a `MoveTo(cell)` → `CallFunc(do the thing)` chain does the thing at the origin. **`MoveTo(cell, nearEnough)` does not protect against this and reads as though it does:** the tolerance is consumed only inside `PopPath`'s *actor-blocked* branch, which needs a path to exist in order to have a next cell, so a terrain-impassable destination never reaches it. Any queued action that assumes arrival needs its own arrival check against the ordered cell, sharing one tolerance constant with the move.
  - **And that tolerance must be `footprint/2 + margin`, not a flat number — a flat 2 rejects a legitimate diagonal park at a 3×3 host.** A transport stopping at the diagonal corner of a 3×3 building sits `dx = 2, dy = 2` from its centre, which is correct behaviour and which a flat tolerance of 2 reads as "never arrived" — so the follow-on never fires and the defect presents as a transfer that silently does nothing. `SupplyTransferMath.ArrivalTolerance(hostFootprintCells, approachMarginCells)` is the reference implementation (`radius = hostFootprintCells > 0 ? hostFootprintCells / 2 : 0`), NUnit-pinned by the `ArrivalTolerance*` tests in `SupplyTransferMathTest.cs`. **Derive the tolerance from the host's footprint whenever the destination is a building rather than a bare cell.** Note this is the *terrain* case; the separate `MoveResult` defect above is why the *actor-blocked* case cannot report failure either.
- **`Passable` buys MOVEMENT and nothing else — every other "is this cell free" test must go through `World.BlockingActorsAt`.** `LocomotorInfo` unions `Passes` ∪ `Crushes` into `PassableClasses`, and that set is read **only by the movement layer**: `Locomotor.IsBlockedBy`, the cell-blocking cache (`Locomotor.UpdateCellBlocking`), its hierarchical-pathfinder mirror (`HierarchicalPathFinder.ActorIsBlocking`) and the pass/crush-notification loop (`Mobile.PassAction`). Every *other* question of the form "may I do something **in** this cell" — land, unload, deploy, drop a crate, leave a husk, build — is a separately hand-rolled `world.ActorMap.GetActorsAt(cell)` LINQ query that never consults a pass class and therefore refuses on an actor the unit can walk straight over. ww3mod feels this acutely because it renders crop fields as ordinary 1x1 `Building` actors (`^CivField`, `rules/ingame/civilian.yaml`), densely: **3187 of `river-zeta-ww3`'s 4544 actors are cosmetic ground cover** (the `^CivField` heirs `v14`–`v18` and `rice` in its `map.yaml`). `AircraftInfo` is the sharper edge — it declares `Crushes` but **no `Passes`** (`Traits/Air/Aircraft.cs`), and `Aircraft.IsBlockedBy` uses `Crushes` as its pass set, so an aircraft can only be un-blocked by something it would *destroy*; no YAML can make such a cell landable. The shared predicate is `World.BlockingActorsAt(cell)` (`Traits/CellOccupancy.cs`), which skips actors whose `PassableInfo.GroundCover` is set (default `false` so nothing but fields changes). Its users include `Aircraft.CanLand`/`CanEnterCell`/`CanEnterTargetNow`, `HeliEmergencyLanding`, `Husk`, `Crate`, `CrateSpawner`, `DropsCrate`, `DropsSupplyCache`, `BuildingUtils.IsCellBuildable`, `LayMines`, `Gate`, `Parachutable` and `LeaveSmudgeWarhead`; list them with `git grep -n 'BlockingActorsAt(' engine/`. **`Locomotor` is deliberately NOT routed through it** — it has richer per-locomotor rules and rerouting it would move pathfinding. Read `GroundCover` off `ActorInfo` (`CellOccupancy.IsGroundCover` does), never off the trait dictionary: `GetActorsAt` results can contain an actor destroyed earlier in the same tick, and any trait read on a disposed actor throws. **This is a convention, not an invariant** — a newly written `GetActorsAt` occupancy test silently reintroduces the whole bug class.
- **An armament pause collapses `AutoTarget`'s scan radius to ZERO — a paused unit stops *seeing*, not just shooting.** `AutoTarget` picks its scan range as `Info.ScanRadius > 0 ? WDist.FromCells(Info.ScanRadius) : ab.GetMaximumRange()` (in `AutoTarget.ScanForTarget` and `TryFindHigherBandTarget`), and `ScanRadius` defaults to `-1`, so the fallback is the normal path, not the exception. `AttackBase.GetMaximumRange()` skips every armament that is `IsTraitDisabled` **or `IsTraitPaused`** and returns `WDist.Zero` when all of them are — the scan circle becomes a point and the unit auto-acquires nothing at all. **No vehicle in the mod pins `ScanRadius`.** It appears only on infantry templates (`^CamoSoldier` and `^DR` in `infantry.yaml`, `ScanRadius: 25`), plus maps that pin it map-locally (`arena-tank-duel`, `shellmap-open-field`, `river-zeta-ww3/scenarios.yaml`); recount with `git grep -nw ScanRadius -- mods/` — which is why the effect hides in the scenarios most used for testing. This is not an ammo story: *any* `PauseOnCondition` **on the armament** does it, and the shipped armament-level set is broad — `!ammo-primary`/`-secondary`/`-tertiary`, `!ammo`, `empdisable`, `heavy-damage-attained`, `unit.docked`, `disabled`, `inwater`, `!loaded`, `firing-primary`, `suppressed >= 10`, and `!alwaysdisabled` (a permanently-paused armament). Mind the level: `garrisoned-at-port` and `build-incomplete` appear only on non-armament traits (`Mobile`, `AttackFrontal`, `Production` and the like), never on an `Armament`, so they do not reach this path. So an EMP'd, docked, dry, in-water, or below-50%-HP vehicle is sensor-dead, and it does not re-acquire the instant the condition lifts — it re-acquires on its next scan tick. When reasoning about autotarget behaviour under a pause, "holds fire" and "cannot see a target to hold fire at" are different mechanisms with different recovery. The honest fix, if one is ever wanted, is an unpaused-if-any-else-all fallback (the `maxFallback` shape `GetMaximumRangeVersusTarget` already uses), not sprinkling `ScanRadius` onto every vehicle.
- **With `PreemptScanInterval: 0` (the engine default) `AutoTarget.ChooseTarget` is reachable ONLY from the idle path, so an engaged unit never re-evaluates target priority.** The chain is `ChooseTarget` ← `ScanForTarget` ← `ScanAndAttack` ← `INotifyIdle.TickIdle`, and `Actor.IsIdle` is `CurrentActivity == null` — an engaged unit is never idle, so the comparison that would notice a higher-priority target simply never runs a second time. The priority *data* and the priority *math* are both fine: `priorityValue` subtracts `effectivePriority * (1<<24)`, a bucket that dwarfs any map range, so a higher band always wins regardless of distance. **Three independent locks hold the incumbent, which is why partial fixes here do not work:** (1) not idle ⇒ `TickIdle` never fires; (2) `ScanForTarget`'s `overrideAutoTarget` loop returns the incumbent *before* scanning, and `AttackFollow.TryGetAutoTargetOverride` hands back `RequestedTarget` verbatim — so even the forced rescans in `AttackMoveActivity`/`SmartMoveActivity` get the old target back; (3) `AttackFollow.Tick` short-circuits to `DoAttack` while `RequestedTarget.IsValidFor(self)`, and `AttackActivity.Tick` re-imposes `SetRequestedTarget` every tick from its own field. **`AttackBase.EvaluateTarget` is not a substitute** — it picks the *closest healthy* actor, i.e. it is priority-blind, and it returns early on `isManualTarget: true`, which is what the main `AttackFollow` path passes. The escape is `AutoTargetInfo.PreemptScanInterval` (**default 0 = disabled**; ww3mod sets `25` on the shared templates in `defaults.yaml`), which drives a cadenced rescan from `ITick` (`AutoTarget.TickPreemption`) that calls `ChooseTarget` **directly** (via `TryFindHigherBandTarget`) and switches only on a *strictly higher* band. Note it deliberately does **not** route through `ScanForTarget`: that would re-arm `nextScanTime` from `SharedRandom.Next`, shifting the shared stream (see the `SharedRandom` rule above) and starving the existing scanners. Its cadence gate is `ActorID`-staggered and draws zero RNG. No cooldown or hysteresis is needed and adding one would be wrong — the range, cluster and soft-overkill terms are bounded by construction to stay strictly inside one bucket, so **the band comparison IS the hysteresis**.
- **A killing hit fires `Damaged` AND `Killed`, so any per-hit handler with a death handler beside it runs BOTH.** `Health.InflictDamage` clamps damage (`Health.ApplyDamageToHp`), builds the `AttackInfo` with the DamageState evaluated *post*-clamp but `Damage` left unclamped, then fires `INotifyDamage.Damaged` and, on a fatal hit (`HP == 0`), `INotifyKilled.Killed`. `Cargo` implements both — a per-hit passenger-damage curve (`Cargo.ForwardDamageToPassengers`, called from `Damaged`) and a transport-death curve (`Killed`, via `PassengerDamageFromTransportDeath`) — so passengers of a transport destroyed in one blow take **both**. The load-bearing detail when writing such a guard is that `DamageState.Dead >= DamageState.Heavy`, so a naive "bail out if badly hurt" threshold also matches death; `Cargo.ShouldEmergencyBail` spells the exclusion out as `current >= bailAt && current < DamageState.Dead`. One documented exception: `Cargo.ForwardDamageToPassengers` returns early for actors with `GarrisonProtectionInfo`, so "both curves always run" is not quite universal.
  - **That guard is the worked example of the general hazard of guarding by early exit: a guard written to skip ONE block silently acquires every block later appended below it.** The `GarrisonProtectionInfo` bail was added (`c9699af9`) at the top of `Cargo`'s `Damaged` to skip legacy passenger-damage forwarding, and its comment said exactly that. The emergency-bail block was appended below it in a later, unrelated change (`4e8e29e2`), so the bail silently never ran for a garrison building: every garrisonable building in the mod carries `GarrisonProtection` beside its `Cargo` (`^CivBuilding` in `civilian.yaml`; GTWR, PBOX, HBOX in `structures-defenses.yaml`), and no vehicle or aircraft `Cargo` carries the trait, so the guard bit buildings only and the feature looked alive everywhere it was tested. The guard now sits inside `Cargo.ForwardDamageToPassengers`, the only block it is about (pinned by `GarrisonBailReachabilityTest`), and garrison buildings switch the bail off explicitly with `EmergencyBailDamageState: Dead` (pinned by `GarrisonBailDisabledTest`). **Detector: when you append to a method, read upward for early returns before assuming your code runs** — and when a guard's comment names a narrower reason than its position enforces, the comment is the artefact that will stop the next reader checking.
- **`Cargo.AfterUnloadDelay` is an EXIT delay, not a spacing delay, despite the name.** It is applied only in the `if (!unloadAll || !cargo.CanUnload())` branch (`Activities/UnloadCargo.cs`, via `UnloadCargo.Finish`) — i.e. once, on the way out — and ww3mod sets it to `0` on every APC while aircraft use 40. The *spacing* between passengers is a separate mechanism: `NextUnloadDelay()` queued between passengers (shared with the emergency bail via `Cargo.NextUnloadDelay`), with `CargoInfo` defaults `UnloadGroupSize = 2` and `InterGroupUnloadDelayMultiplier = 3`. Tuning dismount pacing means the latter, never `AfterUnloadDelay`.
- **"Critical damage" names TWO thresholds 25 percentage points apart, and the `critical-damage` condition is the stricter one.** `Health.DamageState` puts `Critical` below **25%** MaxHP and `Heavy` below **50%** In YAML, `critical-damage` is granted only at `Critical` (`defaults.yaml`, `GrantConditionOnDamageState@CriticalDamage`, `ValidDamageStates: Critical`) while `heavy-damage-attained` covers Heavy **and** Critical (`@HeavyDamageAttained`). Note `heavy-damage` — no suffix — is `ValidDamageStates: Heavy` **alone** (`@HeavyDamage`), so it stops applying below 25%; the `-attained` suffix is what makes a condition cumulative downward, and picking the wrong one leaves a consumer dark in exactly the band it was meant to cover. Meanwhile the mod's "badly damaged" *design* cluster all fires at 50% — cook-off `StartIfBelow: 50`, smoke `StartFraction: 50`, and `VehicleCrew.EjectionDamageState = DamageState.Heavy`. **So a rule written against `critical-damage` because it "means badly damaged" fires at half the health the neighbouring rules do**, and the gap is silent. Pick the condition by threshold, not by name.
- **Selection priority is a GROUP FILTER, not a ranking.** `SelectableExts.SubsetWithHighestSelectionPriority` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/SelectableExts.cs`) groups candidates by *exact* priority and returns only the top group — so a modifier cannot re-rank within a selection, it can only move a unit into or out of the winning band. `BaseSelectionPriority` seeds `int.MaxValue` (under the Ctrl/Alt modifier keys) and `ISelectionPriorityModifier` values are added afterwards, in `SelectionPriority`. The shipped ladder is **10, 8, 6, 4, 2, 1, 0** — there is deliberately nothing at 9, so a `-1` modifier demotes a unit out of its band without landing it in another one. Modifiers can be made conditional on a held key via `SelectionPriorityModifier.SuppressedBy` (default `None`).
  - **The viewer-relationship offset is a bigger splitter than the entire YAML ladder, so "equalise the `Priority` values" cannot merge two actors with different owners.** `SelectionPriority` subtracts `PriorityRange = 30` per relationship step — Ally `−30`, Neutral `−60`, Enemy `−90` (`SelectableExts.SelectionPriority`) — against a shipped ladder whose entire span is 10. Since the group filter keys on **exact** equality, an own building and a neutral one can never share a band whatever their `Priority`, and a drag-box over both returns only the own one. **When a selection behaves as if a `Priority` edit did nothing, check the owners first**: the ladder only ever discriminates *within* one relationship tier. (The offset is skipped entirely when `a.Owner == viewer` or there is no viewer — so it is also invisible in any single-player-owned test setup.)
  - **A C# default that no template overrides is easy to misread as "unset".** `SelectableInfo.Priority` defaults to `10` (`Traits/Selectable.cs`), and `^SelectableBuilding` pins `2` (`defaults.yaml`) — so an actor that inherits nothing selection-related sits at the **top** of the ladder rather than the bottom. Confirm which template an actor actually reaches before reasoning about its band.
- **A commented-out trait block is not necessarily "disabled" — it can be a trait the engine no longer HAS.** `SmokeTrailWhenDamaged` exists in this tree only as an `UpdateRules` migration (`UpdateRules/Rules/20210321/RemoveSmokeTrailWhenDamaged.cs`); there is no such trait class. So a commented block naming it is not a feature someone switched off and could switch back on — uncommenting it would fail to load. **Before "re-enabling" a commented trait, check the class still exists**; the modern replacement here is `LeavesTrails`. Related authoring trap in the same family: `WeaponInfo.LoadWarheads` (`GameRules/WeaponInfo.cs`) collects warheads by a raw `StartsWith("Warhead")` with **no de-duplication**, unlike the trait path (which merges last-value-wins at the first key's position, above) — so two `Warhead@Smudge` blocks in one weapon really do both load.
- **Two WW3MOD-only filters drop a target on the AUTO path alone, and their asymmetry is diagnostic.** `AutoTarget.ChooseTarget` skips a candidate on **overkill** (`Info.OverkillThreshold >= 0 && target.Actor.AverageDamagePercent > OverkillThreshold`; default `100`) and on **break-off** (`Info.BreakOffCondition`, default `critical-damage`). **The overkill comparison is strictly `>`, not `>=`, and that is load-bearing** — `EstimatePercentDamage` caps one shooter's claim at 100 and the threshold is 100, so under `>=` a single committed shooter tripped the skip exactly and blinded every other unit; the PITFALL above that comparison in `ChooseTarget` records the measurement. The consequence to carry: **one committed shooter can never deter a second**, deliberately. Break-off has **three further sites** beyond the scan: `Activities/Attack.cs` (`TickAttack`, the `AttackFrontal` activity) and both of `AttackFollow`'s targets — `RequestedTarget` and `OpportunityTarget`. All are scoped through `AttackBase.BreakOffApplies`, which exempts deliberate and force-attack orders. Overkill has exactly one site. **So the two behave differently on a manual order: overkill is skip/fire/fire, break-off is skip/skip/fire.** If a unit refuses an auto-target but obeys a right-click, suspect overkill; if it refuses both but obeys force-fire, suspect break-off. There is also a **soft** overkill term (`SoftOverkillThreshold`, default `50`) applied in `ChooseTarget` as a range-scaled priority penalty rather than a skip, and a `declinedShootableTarget` flag that records `LastHeldFireTick` when these filters are why nothing was chosen — use it rather than inferring.
- **The overkill mark is a sum on the TARGET of per-attacker RESERVATIONS, and a reservation is released when the shot resolves.** `Actor.AverageDamagePercent` is a plain public int that any code may read, but write it through the claim API: `Actor.ClaimForAttack` reserves a share and `Actor.ReleaseAttackClaim` hands it back, both delegating to `OverkillClaim` (`engine/OpenRA.Game/OverkillClaim.cs`). **The release fires from `Armament`'s delayed fire action (the `ScheduleDelayedAction` in `Armament.FireBarrel`) — at the trigger pull, above the target re-validation return, so a shot that misses, expires short, spawns no projectile, or is aborted because the target died in the `FireDelay` gap all release too.** Two invariants follow from the claim having an owner, and both are pinned in `engine/OpenRA.Test/OverkillClaimTest.cs`: **one shooter is one claim** (re-committing replaces rather than stacks, so a unit re-acquiring the same target every rescan cannot inflate the tally alone), and **a claim taken and released leaves the tally where it started**. `MarkForDestruction` is the raw ownerless `+=` — it has no way back and decays out instead, so prefer `ClaimForAttack` from anything representing a shooter.
  - **What is still NOT released, and decays out rather than being handed back:** a claim whose holder dies before firing, one whose holder is ordered elsewhere before firing, and any raw `MarkForDestruction` bump. So killing the committing unit does not clear its mark. The halving every 60 ticks (in `Actor.Tick`, whose PITFALL records why the interval went 20 → 60: a 20-tick halving let other units re-target a tank mid-reload) is the cleanup path for exactly those cases.
  - **Release is clamped at zero** (`OverkillClaimMath.Release`, `OverkillClaim.cs`) because the decay runs underneath a live claim: a claim of 100 held across two halvings is worth 25 on the tally when it comes back, and subtracting the recorded 100 unclamped would go negative. The clamp can under-count while another shooter still holds a claim — that errs toward units engaging, which is the safe direction.
  - **Comments, scenarios or commits that describe the mark as ownerless and self-clearing predate the claim API** — when it was the only downward force, commitments accumulated and a target under sustained attention read as permanently over-committed. Read them as history, not as the current mechanism.
- **A single attacker's claim is capped at one kill's worth, and aggregate commitment is self-limiting by design.** `EstimatePercentDamage` returns `Math.Min(totalDamage * 100 / health.MaxHP, 100)` (`AutoTarget.EstimatePercentDamage`) — before that cap, one MANPAD marked a 600-HP helicopter at 500 against an `OverkillThreshold` of 100, needing three halvings to clear. The intended shape is that attackers commit until the marked total covers the target's health and the rest stand down, which is correct in aggregate; the failure mode was **granularity at the fragile end**, not a runaway loop. Note the residual shape still holds: **suppression duration scales as `damage × 100 / MaxHP`, so the most fragile target is marked hardest and suppressed longest** — which is exactly the case a player expects a battery of AA to shred. The mark itself is invisible in the simulation (no cursor, no message, no target line); `WithHoldingFireDecoration` (render-only, reads `AutoTarget.LastHeldFireTick`, writes nothing) is what surfaces it.
- **Detection gates BOTH attack paths, so "auto dead / manual alive" is the wrong mental model.** An undetected actor stays clickable, but `Activities/Attack.cs`'s `Tick` sets `useLastVisibleTarget = targetIsHiddenActor || !target.IsValidFor(self)` and then returns true — ending the activity — when that last-visible target is invalid. **`forceAttack` does not appear anywhere in that path**, and `AttackBase.AttackTarget` carries no visibility gate of its own, so a manual order on an undetected actor is accepted and then quietly does nothing. Visibility splits into two comparisons and they are not interchangeable: **cell** queries about fog and exploration are strictly-greater (`OpenRA.Game/Traits/Player/MapLayers.cs`, `IsVisible`, `ResolvedVisibility[puv] > visibility`), while **actor detection** is non-strict (`MapLayers.IsDetected`, reached via `Detectable`) — a matching observer strength reveals. They differ because `Tick` stamps `ResolvedVisibility` 1 on every explored cell whether or not anyone is looking, so level 1 means "seen at the weakest band" and "nobody is there" interchangeably; `IsVisible`'s callers pass 1 meaning "better than merely explored", and `IsDetected` floors its threshold at 2 for the same reason. Shadow is subtracted from the OBSERVER and floored at 1 (`MapLayers.AddSource`). Note `Map.ForestGroundShadow` became **superlinear above a knee** (`ForestShadowKneeDensity = 20`, `Map.cs`), so any extrapolation of tree-blinding past density 20 understates it.
- **A blocked airborne LOS deadlocks both attack paths, and this defect is LIVE.** `MoveWithinRange.ShouldStop` has **no LOS term** — it is `AtCorrectRange && CanInteractWithGroundLayer && CanStayInCell`, where `AtCorrectRange` is purely a distance test. But `Attack.TickAttack` folds `losBlocked` into `needsToMove` and then queues a `MoveWithinRange` to fix it. A unit already at the correct range with blocked line of sight therefore queues a move that completes instantly having gone nowhere, and re-queues it forever: **the LOS term that created the need is invisible to the activity meant to satisfy it.** No guard has been added. Per-weapon gate is `Armament.CheckFire` (`FiringLOS.HasClearLOS(self, target, Weapon.ClearSightThreshold)`, default `5`, `WeaponInfo.ClearSightThreshold`); the auto-path reject is the per-unit LOS check in `AutoTarget.ChooseTarget`. Recall short-range immunity: `FiringLOS` returns true unconditionally inside two cells (above), so this can only bite at range.
- **`Player.RelationshipWith` returns `Ally` for ANY spectating player — so a defeated player reads as an ally the instant they lose.** `Player.RelationshipWith`: *"Observers are considered allies to active combatants"* — `if (other == null || other.Spectating) return NonCombatant ? Neutral : Ally;`, checked **before** the alliance mask. Since a `Lost` player is immediately `Spectating`, any loop that mutates `WinState` while its own team-membership test *reads* `WinState` through `RelationshipWith`/`IsAlliedWith` will progressively mis-classify everyone it has already processed — the mechanism behind "destroying one bot's Supply Route defeated every player slotted after it". **Snapshot team membership before the first mutation, and test membership with alliance masks (`SameTeam`), which are immune to `Spectating`.**
- **A passenger reserving space SILENTLY CANCELS the transport's whole activity queue — including a human player's shift-queue.** `Passenger.Reserve` → `Cargo.ReserveSpace` calls `Cargo.LockForPickup`, which sets `state = Locked` and then calls **`self.CancelActivity()` on the transport**. So a unit deciding to board wipes whatever the transport was doing or had queued, with no order issued by anyone and nothing in the log. This is the mechanism to suspect when a carrier abandons a queued route the moment infantry are told to board — and it is why "order the passengers, then queue the carrier's move in the same pass" is unsafe in both directions (see the bot-boarding rule at the end of this list).
- **`Move` and `ForceMove` are TWO order strings in this fork, and `ForceMove` bypasses every `IWrapMove` wrapper.** Stock OpenRA emits one `"Move"` order for both gestures; WW3MOD emits `mot.IsForceMove ? "ForceMove" : "Move"` (`Mobile.IssueOrder`). `ResolveOrder` then treats them deliberately differently: `"Move"` is queued as `WrapMove(new Move(...))`, so every `IWrapMove` trait gets to interpose — `SmartMoveActivity` among them — while `"ForceMove"` queues a bare `Move` (comment *"Force-move bypasses WrapMove — pure movement, no SmartMove wrapping"*, and additionally `NoReverse = true`). **So "the unit ignores plain Move but obeys Force-Move" is a precise diagnostic signature: the fault is in a move *wrapper*, not in the pathfinder, the locomotor or the order.** Anything that pins a wrapper pins ordinary movement while leaving force-move working, and an errand issued through `Mobile.MoveWithinRange` is wrapped too — so its target line can be drawn by an activity whose movement never runs.
- **`Activity.ActivitiesImplementing<T>()` is NOT "the current activity" — it descends into `ChildActivity` AND walks `NextActivity`** (`Activity.cs`). So guarding anything with `CurrentActivity.ActivitiesImplementing<IAttackActivity>()` also matches an autotarget attack nested *under* a player Move (`SmartMoveActivity` queues one as a child) — and acting on that match with `Actor.CancelActivity` cancels the **whole** top-level activity, silently destroying the player's move order. Read the top-level `self.CurrentActivity` type instead when you mean "what is this unit doing right now".
- **Ending a unit's attack activity does NOT mean it stays idle — `AutoTarget` hands it straight back.** `ScanOnIdle` defaults true, so the tick after an attack activity ends an idle unit rescans and queues a fresh attack. Crucially `ChooseTarget` calls the same ammo-blind `AttackBase.ChooseArmamentsForTarget`, so **a unit with zero ammo re-acquires perfectly well** — it is not passively ignored by the targeting layer, it actively signs up for fights. What actually stops the loop is a range coincidence: `ChooseTarget` filters armaments to those *already in range* whenever `allowMove` is false, which it is below `Hunt`, so a unit that halts **outside** its own weapon range finds no candidate and rests, while one sitting inside weapon range of a visible enemy cycles — queue attack, guard ends it, go idle, re-queue. The cycle is throttled to one pass per scan interval (16–32 ticks for WW3MOD infantry, `MinimumScanTimeInterval`/`MaximumScanTimeInterval` in `infantry.yaml`), not one per tick, and it cannot race the resupply it dispatches: `Actor.Tick`'s two arms (`if (!wasIdle && IsIdle)` / `else if (wasIdle)`) are mutually exclusive, so the tick that dispatches an errand is never the tick that re-issues an attack. Anything gating on `IsIdle` should expect it to **alternate** rather than latch for such a unit. Closing this properly belongs in `AutoTarget` — teaching the scan not to acquire for a unit that cannot fire — not in another guard in the activities.
- **Becoming idle CONSUMES `SharedRandom` — so a change to *whether* a unit idles is a determinism change, not only a behaviour change.** Idle is not a quiet state. `WithInfantryBody.TickIdle` draws **twice** per `Waiting → Idle` transition — `IdleSequences.Random(SharedRandom)` and `SharedRandom.Next(MinIdleDelay, MaxIdleDelay)` (`Render/WithInfantryBody.cs`) — gated on `IdleSequences.Length > 0`, which every soldier class with an `IdleSequences:` line in `infantry.yaml` satisfies (`grep -c IdleSequences: mods/ww3mod/rules/ingame/infantry.yaml` counts them). That state **re-arms itself**: `Tick` calls `PlayStandAnimation`, which sets `state = Waiting`, whenever a unit in `Idle`/`IdleAnimating` becomes non-idle — so every idle→busy→idle cycle buys a fresh pair of draws, and a permanently idle soldier keeps drawing through the `Idle → IdleAnimating → PlayStandAnimation → Waiting` animation loop. `AutoTarget.ScanForTarget` draws once per scan to re-arm `nextScanTime`. (`TurnOnIdle` draws too, but no ww3mod rule uses it.) Because `SharedRandom` is one synced stream folded into the sync hash, each draw shifts stream position for **every** later consumer in the match — the behavioural change is local, the RNG consequence is global. Practical consequence: any fix that lets units reach idle where they previously never did (releasing a stuck unit from an activity, say) invalidates tick-for-tick comparison against an older `@stable` baseline even where the tactical outcome is identical. **Re-take the baseline; do not diff replays across it.** See the `World.SharedRandom` rule above and the `Actor.IsIdle` rule below.
- **`IHitShape.CenterProximityPercent` is measured from the CENTRE, and only two of the four shapes return a percentage at all — so giving an actor a `Capsule` or `Polygon` hitshape silently breaks `TargetDamageWarhead`.** The method (renamed from the actively misleading `PercentFromEdge`) is consumed at exactly one site, as a direct percentage damage modifier: `TargetDamageWarhead.DoImpact`. `Circle` (`HitShapes/Circle.cs`) is the only implementation the old name described — for a circle, percent-in-from-the-centre and percent-in-from-the-edge are the same number. `Rectangle` (`Rectangle.cs`) normalises against the half-**diagonal** (centre-to-corner) whichever direction the impact came from, so **only the four corners read 0**: on the Abrams shape (`abrams` in `vehicles-america.yaml`, `TopLeft: -365, -790` / `BottomRight: 365, 790`) dead centre is 100, mid-flank 58, nose-on 9 — a 6.4× swing that is an accidental armour-facing effect of hull aspect ratio, unrelated to `ArmorDirection`. `Capsule` (`Capsule.cs`) and `Polygon` (`Polygon.cs`) are verbatim copies of their own `DistanceFromEdge` with the `WDist` wrapper stripped: they return a raw world-unit distance with the **opposite sense** — 0 anywhere inside the shape — so a dead-centre hit deals **0** damage while a graze outside is handed a modifier in the hundreds. Both carry explanatory comments and are left as-is deliberately (correcting them is a balance change). **Exposure today is nil, which is what makes it dangerous:** every `Type: Capsule` block in `mods/ww3mod/` is commented out (`rules/ingame/naval.yaml`) and there is no `Type: Polygon` anywhere, so the first actor given one becomes immune to every `TargetDamageWarhead` in the game with no error and no log line. Working: [`WORKSPACE/audit/hitshape-percent-semantics.md`](../../WORKSPACE/audit/hitshape-percent-semantics.md).
- **Position-bin queries (`FindActorsInCircle`/`ActorsInBox`, and the Lua `Map.ActorsInCircle`/`ActorsInBox`) return NOTHING until the first world tick — a `WorldLoaded` query reports an empty world.** `World.FindActorsInCircle` (`WorldUtils.cs`) resolves to `ActorMap.ActorsInBox`, which reads the position bins; `ActorMap.AddPosition` only queues into `addActorPosition` and the bins are filled in `ActorMap.TickFunction`, invoked from `ITick.Tick` — i.e. after `WorldLoaded` has returned. The call is correct, the arguments are correct, there is no error, and the answer is wrong. **`ActorMap.GetActorsAt` is cell-keyed off the influence layers and updated on add, so it does NOT share this.** The dangerous shape is not the assertion that fails loudly but ordinary setup code — "find the units near X at load and put them on HoldFire" sets nothing, throws nothing, and the scenario runs to a confident verdict against a world that was never built. **When setup is driven by a query, assert the query returned something before acting on it**; an empty lookup must never be allowed to mean "nothing to do".
- **Resolving the Neutral player by `InternalName` is a silent-failure pattern — the world owner is guaranteed structurally, the name is not.** `CreateMapPlayers` throws at world creation unless some `PlayerReference` carries `OwnsWorld: True` (`CreateMapPlayers`) and parks that player on `WorldActor.Owner` via `World.SetWorldOwner`, so **`world.WorldActor.Owner` is non-null on every map that loads at all**, whatever the author named the player. Every shipped WW3MOD map happens to name it `Neutral`, which is precisely why the weakness is invisible in testing — it only bites on a hand-authored or third-party map. Both failure modes are bad in different ways: `FirstOrDefault(p => p.InternalName == "Neutral")` returns null and the feature quietly does nothing (`GarrisonManager`'s `INotifyCreated.Created`, `HeliEmergencyLanding.OnSafeLanding`, both left alone because they degrade to a no-op), while `First(...)` throws an unhandled `InvalidOperationException` mid-match (`OwnerLostAction`, whose `Owner` defaults to `"Neutral"`). **Prefer `WorldActor.Owner` to either** — `Activities/CaptureActor.cs` records the reasoning inline (`CaptureToNeutral`).
- **Eluant does not marshal Lua numbers to `float`.** A script-binding parameter typed `float` fails at call time with `Unable to convert parameter 0 to Single`; use `double` or `int` and cast inside (`Scripting/Global/TestGlobal.cs`, `public double SetZoom(double scale)`; `CameraGlobal.cs`'s `Zoom`/`MinZoom`/`MaxZoom` are `double` for the same reason).
- **Condition tokens are PER-ACTOR counters, so a token minted on actor A is very often *valid* on actor B — holding the wrong actor's token is a live mis-revoke, not a harmless no-op.** `Actor.nextConditionToken` is initialised to `1` on **every** actor and incremented per grant, and `Actor.TokenValid` is just `conditionTokens.ContainsKey(token)`. So two soldiers in one garrison routinely both hold tokens `1`, `2`, `3`, and revoking A's token against B removes **whatever unrelated condition on B happens to carry that id** (`Actor.RevokeCondition`). The intuition that a foreign token "just won't match" is wrong in the direction that produces a silent, mislocated state change — and its reciprocal is a leak, since A's real token is now recorded nowhere and can never be revoked. Note `RevokeCondition` *throws* on an unknown token, so the usual `if (TokenValid(t))` guard converts the crash into the silent variant. **A stored token is only meaningful paired with the actor it was minted on; move the two together or store neither.**
- **"Cached X" living beside "live X" is a two-reader hazard — find the readers before you name the symptom, because the field name gives the loud wrong one.** Worked example: `GarrisonManager.CachedArmaments` reads like the firing path and is not. The shot iterates the occupant's **live** traits — `AttackGarrisoned.DoGarrisonedAttack` walks `ps.DeployedSoldier.TraitsImplementing<Armament>()` (`AttackGarrisoned.cs`) and re-checks weapon validity and range per armament — so a stale cache can never fire a weapon the occupant does not have, and damage numbers are never wrong. The cache has exactly **one** reader, `GarrisonManager.ScanForTarget`, which uses it to size the scan circle and filter candidates. A desynchronised cache therefore *selects* targets on one soldier's weapon profile and hands them to a firing loop that rejects them on another's: **the port goes silent rather than misbehaving visibly.** Reasoning from the field name predicted "fires the wrong weapon" in two WORKSPACE docs; reading the readers gives the real symptom, plus a `NullReferenceException` at its `foreach (var a in armaments)` whenever a slot holds a soldier and a null cache. **Enumerate a cache's readers before predicting what its staleness looks like** — this is README shape 3 (the cited mechanism does not do the work) in its most ordinary form. PITFALL at the temptation site, `GarrisonManager.SwapPortOccupants`.
- **Ordering a bot passenger to board while queuing the carrier's Move in the SAME pass flies the transport EMPTY.** The `EnterTransport` orders land on the *infantry's* activity queues, but a queued `Move` on the *carrier's* own (empty, idle) queue starts IMMEDIATELY — so the carrier departs before any soldier boards. The engine gives no boarding-complete callback; boarding "completes" only when `Cargo.Load` fires, observable only by polling `Cargo.PassengerCount`. The correct shape (used by `MountedTransportBotModule` and now `HelicopterSquadBotModule.TryLaunchTransportMission`): stage a `Loading` task, dispatch the delivery only once `PassengerCount >= min` (or a partial on timeout), abort to the idle pool if nobody boarded — and queue the carrier's RETURN move right behind the `Unload` so it doesn't idle at the drop (a dedicated transport is free-pool-excluded, so nothing else re-collects it — see architecture.md §"The bot free pool self-heals").

- **`<player>.GetActors()` in Lua returns that player's own `PlayerActor` — the Lua twin of the
  `world.Actors` bullet above, with a worse failure mode.** `PlayerProperties.GetActors`
  (`Scripting/Properties/PlayerProperties.cs`) filters
  `actor.Owner == Player && !actor.IsDead && actor.IsInWorld` over `Player.World.Actors`, and **a
  `PlayerActor` satisfies all three**: `Player.cs` builds it with `new Actor(...)` then `Initialize(true)`,
  and that `true` is `addToWorld`, so `Actor.Initialize` ends in `World.Add(this)`; it has no `Health`, so
  `IsDead` is false; its `Owner` is its own `Player`. A sweep that destroys the result therefore destroys the
  player. **`Destroy()` is not a local operation on a player actor and nothing complains when it happens** —
  Lua `Destroy()` queues `RemoveSelf` → `Actor.Dispose()`, which at frame end runs
  `World.TraitDict.RemoveActor` and sets `Disposed`; the next tick proceeds normally and **the failure
